The Needleford Simulation
Overview

The Council of Needleford

Tick 7

Two collectors came up short in one week and the council audited both. The guilds' assessment was clean - the audit failed by one - and the tick-4 undertaking obliged the council to post that it had looked, eight days after the guildhall petitioned to be in the room, for six points of standing and nothing. The quay share was not clean: 315G across nine entries in a clerk's hand, found, recovered, prosecuted, and posted at the four places naming the harbour guard, because that is what the undertaking says. Dray, who had asked for seven days' notice and been refused: "I'd not worked out it was me that was going to pay for it." The sea factors petitioned - nine houses of eleven, carried by Hanne Vesk - for the same thing the guildhall asked for in a smaller coat, and brought it early on purpose so that it would be open on the table when the guildhall is answered. And the investigation joined up: the man with the crushed thumb gave the name Thurlow to a barber-surgeon in the autumn before last, and Thurlow and Company, carriers opened on the Hithe porterage list in the same autumn and hired Hallet off a bench. There is a margin on that list with a name in it. Brackwater's pattern, said out loud for the first time: "They cannot answer a question. They can only remove the person it was asked about."

Calendar

Tick by tick

Tick 7

Two collectors came up short in one week and the council audited both. The guilds' assessment was clean - the audit failed by one - and the tick-4 undertaking obliged the council to post that it had looked, eight days after the guildhall petitioned to be in the room, for six points of standing and nothing. The quay share was not clean: 315G across nine entries in a clerk's hand, found, recovered, prosecuted, and posted at the four places naming the harbour guard, because that is what the undertaking says. Dray, who had asked for seven days' notice and been refused: "I'd not worked out it was me that was going to pay for it." The sea factors petitioned - nine houses of eleven, carried by Hanne Vesk - for the same thing the guildhall asked for in a smaller coat, and brought it early on purpose so that it would be open on the table when the guildhall is answered. And the investigation joined up: the man with the crushed thumb gave the name Thurlow to a barber-surgeon in the autumn before last, and Thurlow and Company, carriers opened on the Hithe porterage list in the same autumn and hired Hallet off a bench. There is a margin on that list with a name in it. Brackwater's pattern, said out loud for the first time: "They cannot answer a question. They can only remove the person it was asked about."

Artifactspetition of the sea factors year 0
Tick 6

The guildhall petitioned, and not about money. Ninety-one of a hundred and twelve, carried by Warden Osric Pell, for prior sight of any measure touching a rate, a bonded fee or a quay, and a guildhall man heard in chamber as of right - "We ask to be in the room." Weight 7, deadline tick 10, and the council took the four weeks rather than answer it. Earlier the same week it refused the harbour guard the last of its three asks, in person, with the reason said out loud, which cost three points there and gained two with the Watch for refusing the same clause twice in one month - taking the company out of breaking for the first time since the charter. Brackwater failed at the stair and failed worse in the emptied room, and was handed the week's best lead on the bottom step by a twelve-year-old nobody had counted: one of the two men came back for a bag in the morning, good coat, bad boots, a thumb that will not bend. Partridge got the bench and the hiring was on an account - Thurlow and Company, carriers, which does not exist. Thackeray, who is nobody in that ward, rolled a natural 10 and learned Hallet was promised work through the winter. And the guilds remitted 108G short in the week they petitioned, and the council decided not to look.

Tick 5

The harbour guard petitioned - sixty-one of seventy-four, carried by Captain Owen Dray - for seven days' notice before any search of the quay books. The council answered in part, offered a man of his own in the room instead, and failed the check by one. On the day between the petition and the answer, Keel named the second week of the rate-book and audited both the gate books and the quay books for the missing 376G, found nothing, and the undertaking he wrote in tick 4 obliged him to post that he had looked. Thackeray found a filed amendment to the entries the council had already read and took it as a substrate upgrade - the factor's thread is now archived record and does not decay. The messenger did not run; he was withdrawn by two men between midnight and first light, and Brackwater has the hire book at the lower stair. The chest fell for the first time.

Tick 4

The Watch petitioned, 213 of 250, carried by Sergeant Elias Roper: a published schedule, a payment inside the week, and no council officer in their books. The council conceded the first two, refused the third as a thing the charter does not let it grant, and added - unasked - a standing undertaking to publish the result of every inspection of every collector, found or not found. The auctioneer off the Hithe has been bought and has forgotten; Thackeray got a back stair out of him anyway and matched it to Brackwater's notebook. They have the messenger's face twice over. He knows.

Tick 3

Keel audited the toll collection over Garrow's objection and found nothing, in the same week the Watch was again not paid. Garrow read the factor's eleven-week intervals as a military pay cycle, not a trade one. Partridge and Thackeray got the ground out of the river wards - upriver, north bank, a day's pull. Forty porters nearly put Vesk & Sons' crane in the river and were talked down by an unpaid gate man. Brackwater has a face from the auctioneer's door.

Favoursnone gained, none spent
Itemsnone
Haltnone - the audit scored 10 of 36

Scenestick 003 the audit, tick 003 the muster cycle, tick 003 the crane
Tick 2

The first week under the new rate-book. A windfall out of the river wards - the burial club's almshouse lease, read by Keel and saved by Partridge. Keel and Thackeray pulled four hundred and sixty-one bonded entries and found the factor's mark, and then found that it recurs on an eleven-week calendar that answers to no trade season. Partridge and Thackeray learned that Hallet was no thief but a boatman hired for lifting work, away for days at a time. Garrow told two hundred and fifty unpaid men the exact size of the chest, and the council paid one week of nine.

Favoursnone gained, none spent

Haltnone - the largest act of the week scored 6 of 36

Tick 1

The council took its seat and set the three rates - holding the passage toll publicly, correcting the bonded-storage fee upward after thirty untouched years, and cutting the dues that fall on people who live here. Marget Stroud answered with a petition; Keel broke one clause of it and took the other eight. Thackeray traced the jewellery to a river crewman named Hallet, who sold nine pieces for well under their worth and vanished within the month.

The investigation

The board

Time runs downward. A thread that gave the council something pays it out sideways; a thread that gave them another thread runs down. Freshness is what the council can see - never a number.

freshfadingcolddeada string sideways is something learned; a string down is a new thread; a cut string is a pull that found nothing
tick 1
T-001dead
Hallet
died tick 6
On the last week it was alive: he was promised work through the winter, and settled his score because he thought he was in regular employment.
T-003dead
The auctioneer off the Hithe
died tick 4
A day, an hour and a back stair - matched to Brackwater's notebook.
What they learnedtick 1
The jewellery was dwarven vault-work, nine pieces, sold eleven months ago by a river crewman called Hallet, who vanished inside the month. He paid his score at the Blackcap in full on his last night, which is not what a man does who expects to be back.
tick 2
T-002cold
The Galician factor
archived record (the bills themselves, by number)
who paid for the lot, and in what coin
T-004fading
The muster cycle
working record (the council's own copy)
whose calendar it is, and what it is counting
T-005dead
Hallet's lifting work
died tick 4
Upriver, north bank, about a day's pull. The only thing in this affair that points at ground.
What they learnedtick 2
The lot was bought under a Galician factor's mark with no family name on it, paid in bills of exchange. Thackeray's reading: for a Galician that is not discretion, it is dishonour. A man trading behind a bare mark is a man whose name has been taken off him, and there will be a decision about it in a Galician court.
tick 3
T-006dead
The messenger
died tick 6
He was withdrawn, not run - and on the week it died, one of the two men who did it.
What they learnedtick 3
Hallet was no thief. He was hired by the week for lifting, by a man who paid coin and drank with nobody, and he went upriver, north bank, about a day's pull. Two men who hired out at the same bench are still in the city.
What they learnedtick 3
That mark is paid on an eleven-week cycle that is a military pay cycle, not a trade one. Somebody is paying the way an army pays a standing post.
tick 4
What they learnedtick 4
A man came up a back stair and bought the auctioneer's memory. Brackwater had his face, his lodging and his hours.
NothingT-003, tick 4
Thackeray, the auctioneer off the Hithe. Fit +2 against 8, rolled 1. He had been bought and had forgotten. The burn paid out regardless and the thread died this tick.
NothingT-006, tick 4
Brackwater, the tail. Fit +3 against 7, rolled 3. Nothing - and it was noticed.
tick 5
T-007cold
The hire at the lower stair
working record (Rudd's hire book)
a hand that is not a name - if the book is ever opened
T-008cold
The account
casual memory
Thurlow and Company, carriers. Largely spent - what it had, it has given
What they learnedtick 5
Somebody amended those entries the week after the council read them. A correction that nobody asked for, filed by the mark's Needleford correspondent on instruction. The man behind the mark is not in this city, has a man in it, and takes about a fortnight to be frightened.
What they learnedtick 5
He did not run. He was withdrawn - a room emptied between midnight and first light by two men who were not him, and a boat hired before dawn, upriver.
NothingT-006, tick 5
Brackwater, the lodging houses at the lane end. Fit +1 against 7, rolled 1. She can follow a man in this city and she cannot ask in it.
tick 6
T-009fading
The man with the thumb
archived-grade working record (Odo Tench's day-book, eleven years, one hand)
a date, an injury, a fee - and the name he gave
What they learnedtick 6
The lifting was hired on an account and not by a man: Thurlow and Company, carriers. There is no Thurlow and Company in the carting guild's list of yards, in the dues roll, in the waggon licence book or in the bond register. And Hallet was told the work would run through the winter.
What they learnedtick 6
One of the two who emptied the room came back for a bag in the morning, after light, and stepped round a child on the stair and said sorry. Middling height, a good coat, bad boots, and a right thumb that does not bend.
NothingT-007, tick 6
Brackwater, the earlier hires on the same business. Fit +3 against 7, rolled 2. She asked for the book. Rudd had given her a night because she asked for a night.
NothingT-002, tick 6
Thackeray, the archived bills by number. Fit +2 against 7, rolled 3. A day and a half of the wrong questions.
NothingT-006, tick 6
Brackwater, the emptied room. Fit +3 against 8, rolled 1. A limewashed room, a family of five, and a boot-nail in a floor joint.
tick 7
T-010fresh
The surety
working record (the Hithe porterage list) + a living man
Crispin Vout, who wrote his name in the margin against an account that is not a business - and who was paid by somebody to do it
What they learnedtick 7
The thumb is a crush at the base, set fast and badly by a man in a hurry, and Odo Tench set it in the autumn before last. It is in the day-book, with a date and a fee and a second dressing the man never came back for. The name he gave was Thurlow.
What they learnedtick 7
The account exists in exactly one place in Needleford: the Hithe porterage list, which records accounts that are good for wages rather than businesses. Opened the autumn before last, eleven entries, settled every Friday, never queried. And there is a column for who stood for it, with Crispin Vout in it - a bill-broker who vouches accounts for a fee and asks nothing. Eight of his other nine are spotless.
What they learnedtick 7
Everybody the council asks about stops being in Needleford. The auctioneer was bought. The messenger was taken out between midnight and first light. The man who hired the boat has not been at the stair since the week the council came, and a boat went up on a Tuesday night with two aboard and one came back. Three, in five weeks.
NothingT-002, tick 7
Thackeray, the bills again. Fit +2 against 7, rolled 4 - failed by one. Second failure running on the one thread that cannot be waited out. Keel let it go without comment, which Thackeray noticed.
NothingT-007, tick 7
Brackwater, the stair, asked around with no names. Fit +1 against 7, rolled 2. Nothing on the question she went with - and the answer to a question she had not asked walked up to her anyway.
root

The Needleford Simulation

A persistent simulation of the council of Needleford - five chartered heroes governing a Bryten port-and-junction city.

How this is organised

PathHolds
canon/Minted lore. Binding on everything written afterwards.
rules/Agreed mechanics. The simulation's rulebook.
transcripts/Council sessions and scenes, as they happen. (not yet)
despatches/Periodic reports to the charter-holders - the review document. (not yet)
ledger/Factions, favours, hooks, investigation threads, upkeep and windfalls. The council's position, political and material.
state/Date, treasury, docket. (not yet)
manifests/Cargo manifests as the heroes see them - declared, not necessarily true. (not yet)
infra/Scripts, scratch notes, calibration data, and sealed/.

Inside infra/: decisions.md records why settled things are settled and what was rejected on the way - read it before reopening anything. npc-pool.md holds fully-formed characters built but not used, ready to deploy when the simulation needs a named person in a hurry.

Directories marked not yet appear when the simulation starts running.

The seals

infra/sealed/ holds the answer to the mission's mystery (the-culprit.md), the true contents of declared cargo (manifests.md), the hidden side of the investigation mechanic (threads.md), and the adversary's phase state and ash count (the-operation.md). The DM asked to be kept in the dark. Nothing in that folder is ever quoted, summarised or hinted at outside it. The heroes learn only what they earn through play.

The DM may order it broken at any time. If a DM ruling contradicts it, say so - that it conflicts, never what - and grade the collision soft or hard per rules/simulation.md.

Canon precedence

  1. ../laurasia-worldbuilding/ - the author's lore. Final say, always.
  2. canon/ - minted here. Binding once written.
  3. Anything invented in the moment - must contradict neither of the above.

Deviation from (1) requires an in-world narrative reason and explicit approval.

Status

The viewer is published at https://janletovanec.github.io/needleford-viewer/ - see infra/publishing.md for what goes up and what never does.

Five ticks played. The clock is running. Halt 001 was resolved and waved through; tick 5 produced no halt, its largest act scoring 9 of 36. See state/current.md.

The council has set the three rates, heard two petitions, audited its own Watch twice and found nothing twice, and traced the jewellery from a dead river crewman to a Galician factor's mark paid on a military calendar, to a stretch of north bank a day upriver, to a man who was withdrawn from the city by boat before dawn by people who did not think about the hire book.

The factor's thread no longer decays. An amendment he filed to protect himself identified the bills of exchange by number, and the council took the margin as a substrate upgrade - the strongest position the rules allow.

Two petitions cross 80% during tick 6, the harbour guard's is live with two ticks left and a difficulty of 9 on the next attempt, and the chest fell for the first time. None of it was authored.

Mechanics written: severity and the halt budget, the economy and the toll response curve, petitions, investigation threads, tenure and the ash clock, leakage, upkeep, seasonality, windfalls, the standings ledger, party combat, mass combat and robbery. All of them in rules/.

Open: the chase, parked until a chase actually happens - it wants a map, and inventing the map first is how you build a mechanic nothing fits. Nothing is blocking.

The action budget (3 per councillor per tick), the severity threshold (14 of 36), the investigation difficulty anchors and the windfall rate are calibration targets, tuned from play in infra/calibration-log.md.

README.md
artifacts

Bill of Rates

Posted at the customs house, the Bridge gate, the Hithe and the guildhall. Read aloud at each, by the Watch, on the day of posting.

BY THE COUNCIL OF NEEDLEFORD, sitting under charter, this first week.

PASSAGE. The toll upon goods changing hands between the water and the road stands unaltered. The council has considered it and has let it stand. Let it be known that the council could have raised it and did not.

BONDED STORAGE. The fee upon goods held in bonded warehouse is corrected to the measure of their present worth, the former rate having been set by the rate-book of a Michaelmas court thirty years gone, when the sheds held half what they hold and the goods were worth a quarter.

GUILD AND MARKET DUES. All stall rents, licences and the porters' levy are reduced. The reduction falls wholly upon dues paid by persons dwelling within the city.

Signed, the five: A. Garrow. I. Thackeray. G. Partridge. I. Brackwater. A. Keel.

Minuted at the instance of Councillor Keel: the bonded rate is corrected, not raised.

artifacts/bill-of-rates-year-0.md
artifacts

Petition of the Guildhall of Needleford

Delivered to the council in chamber, tick 6, Year 0. Ninety-one signatures of a hundred and twelve who could sign - 81%. Carried by Warden Osric Pell.

To the Council of Needleford, under charter.

We are the guildhall. We are the warehousemen, the carters, the ropers, the coopers, the lightermen's masters, the corn factors and the nine lesser companies, and we are the men and women whose families have set the rate of every cargo that has come up this river since before the Crown put a stone on the bridge.

We do not petition about money. The council should understand that first, because it has spent five weeks expecting us to.

We petition about the room.

In the first week of this council's tenure the bonded-storage fee was raised, and we were not asked, and we were told afterwards that it had been corrected rather than raised. In the second week an amendment was drafted in our own words and laid before council, and we were sent a fair copy of it, and we are grateful for the copy and we note that it was a copy. In the fifth week the council undertook to publish every search it makes of every collector, and posted that undertaking at four places, and the first thing the guildhall knew of it was reading it on a wall.

Each of those is a small thing. We do not say otherwise. We say that in five weeks this council has been correct about us, fair to us, prompt with us, and has not once needed to have us in the room while it was being any of those things - and that a city governed well by five strangers is still a city governed by five strangers.

We ask one thing, in two parts, and we ask it of the council and not of the Crown.

The first. That no measure touching the rate of any cargo, the fee on any bonded store, or the use of any quay be laid before this council until the guildhall has seen it, in the form it will be laid in, with time enough to answer it.

The second. That when it is laid, a man of the guildhall sit in the chamber while it is read, and be heard before the vote, as of right and not by the council's leave.

We do not ask for a vote. We know the charter. Councillor Keel has read us the charter twice and we have no complaint about his reading of it.

We ask to be in the room.

A council that will not have us in the room while it decides what our goods are worth is a council that has decided we are a trade and not a city, and if that is the judgement we would rather have it in writing than have it in practice for twenty years.

Osric Pell, warden, for the guildhall of Needleford, and ninety signatures following.

The council's reading of it

Recorded by Keel, in chamber, the same evening.

Weight 7 of the guildhall's power of 8. A standing right of prior sight and a standing right of audience. Not a veto; not a vote; and not a thing that can be given for a week and taken back in the spring.

Four ticks on the clock. The deadline is tick 10.

Twenty-one did not sign. Three short of the thing failing to carry.

The names are in ../transcripts/tick-006-the-roll.md.

artifacts/petition-of-the-guildhall-year-0.md
artifacts

Petition of the Harbour Guard of Needleford

Delivered to the council in chamber, tick 5, Year 0. Sixty-one signatures of seventy-four quay men - 82%. Carried by Captain Owen Dray.

To the Council of Needleford, under charter.

We are the harbour guard. We keep the quays, we walk the hulls, and we collect one half of the passage toll on the water side, as we have done under three administrations. We are paid by the yards and not by this council, and we have asked this council for nothing, and we ask now for three things.

The first. On the fourth day of the third week of the rate-book, an officer of this council laid a records query under authority upon the toll collection. That query took the gate books and it took the quay books, and nobody told us which. For a fortnight this city believed the harbour guard was being searched for theft, and this council said nothing to correct it, and when it did speak it spoke of the collection and not of us.

We ask that the council post, at the same four places and in the same hand, that the harbour guard of Needleford was searched and found clean, naming us.

The second. The council has undertaken to publish the result of every inspection of every collector within seven days, found or not found. We do not object to it. We ask only that where the finding concerns our men, a fair copy reach the captain at the hour it is posted, and not from a boy at the gate afterward.

The third. We ask that no inspection of the quay books be made without seven days' written notice to the captain.

We are aware the council refused this to the Watch. We are aware of the clause it refused them on. That clause vests inspection in the council and we do not ask the council to give it away; we ask for notice before it is used, which the charter neither grants us nor forbids you.

A council that cannot give seven days' notice of a search is a council that believes it would find something in the seven days. Say so, if that is the case, and we will know where we stand.

Owen Dray, captain, for the harbour guard of Needleford, and sixty signatures following.

artifacts/petition-of-the-harbour-guard-year-0.md
artifacts

Petition of the Sea Factors of Needleford

Delivered to the council in the counting-house at Vesk & Sons' lower berth, tick 7, Year 0. Nine signatures of eleven houses - 81%. Carried by Hanne Vesk.

To the Council of Needleford, under charter.

We are the sea factors. Eleven houses, of which nine set their hands to this. Our money is largely foreign and cannot vote, we hold no franchise and we speak for no bloc, and we are aware that this council owes us nothing and has been told so by better lawyers than ours.

We will be brief, because our business is brief and because this council has four weeks of harder reading in front of it.

In the second week of this council's tenure, an instrument of a factor house was read aloud in open chamber, clause by clause, in a room full of rivermen, and refused by name.

The refusal was correct. The instrument sold a reversion the seller did not own, the burial club would have been ruined by it, and the house whose paper it was is not a house we are sorry for. We say all of that plainly so that the council does not spend a week preparing an answer we have already conceded.

Our petition is not about the ruling. It is about the room.

There is no chamber in this kingdom where a council does that to a man's paper and the man is not present. Not because he should be able to stop it - he should not - but because an instrument read aloud by somebody who has had a week with it and is about to refuse it is a very different document from the one its holder would read.

We ask one thing.

That no instrument of a factor house be read or ruled upon in open chamber without the house being heard first. Not a veto. Not a vote. A hearing, before the reading, on notice.

We are aware that the guildhall has asked this council for something of the same shape and that the council has not answered it. We have not copied the warden's words and we did not need to; he read his aloud in your chamber on a Thursday and it was in every counting-house on this river by the Saturday.

We bring ours now, and not after, deliberately. If we wait until the guildhall is answered we are asking the council for a thing it has already decided. By coming now we are asking it to decide two things at once, which is harder, and which is honest.

Hanne Vesk, for Vesk & Sons and eight houses following.

The council's reading of it

Recorded by Keel, at the end of the bridge, and minuted the same evening.

Weight 4 of the factors' power of 5. A standing right of audience on their own paper. Three ticks. The deadline is tick 10 - the same tick as the guildhall's.

There is no answer to this that is not also an answer to the guildhall. Her houses want to be heard before a ruling on their paper; his companies want to be heard before a measure on their rates. Those are one principle wearing two coats, and the smaller one is on the table first at less than two thirds of the price.

The only lawful course is to let the clock run and answer the larger petition first. That is not cleverness; it is the only door in the room.

"She did not come early to be first. She came early so that whatever we give the guildhall, we will have given it with her petition open on the table."

artifacts/petition-of-the-sea-factors-year-0.md
artifacts

The Petition of the Watch of Needleford

Delivered to the council in chamber, week four, Year 0. Carried by two hundred and thirteen of two hundred and fifty - eighty-five in the hundred. Borne by Sergeant Elias Roper, nineteen years served.

To the Chartered Council of the City of Needleford.

We are the Watch. We are two hundred and fifty men and we are the only armed body this council commands. We have kept the gates, the quays, the bridge and the night wards of this city for nine weeks without wages, and in the third week of this council's sitting our books were searched for theft.

We do not say the council stole from us. We say the council has not paid us, and has looked at us as though we had stolen from it, and has not said out loud that it found nothing.

We ask three things.

The first. That the council publish a schedule of the eight weeks owed, with dates against each, posted at the four places where the Bill of Rates was posted, so that every man knows what he is owed and when, and so that the city knows it too.

The second. That the first payment on that schedule be made within the week.

The third. That while the arrears stand, no officer of this council shall inspect the books of the Watch. Where inspection is required it shall be laid before an officer of the Crown, and the Crown's officer shall report to the council and to the Watch together.

We are aware what the third asks. We ask it because a man who is owed money and searched for taking it has no way to answer except by being searched again by somebody who is not the person who owes him.

Three weeks are allowed for an answer, as is customary.

Signed for the Watch at the north gate muster yard,

Elias Roper, serjeant, nineteen years Hal Dowsett, gate man and two hundred and eleven others, by mark and by name, appended

artifacts/petition-of-the-watch-year-0.md
canon

The Charter of Needleford

Granted by the Crown of Bryten, countersigned by the League of Kingdoms - the latter so that the other nations would accept Bryten administering a city their goods pass through.

No founding catastrophe. The Crown wanted loyal and competent people in charge of a city that must keep working.

Powers granted

  • Levy tolls and taxes
  • Command armed forces within the city
  • Pass laws
  • Adjudicate disputes

No emergency authority of any kind.

Prohibitions

  • No military presence beyond the city's political reach
  • No act against the laws of Bryten
  • No disobedience to the Crown's orders

Term

Indefinite.

Revocation

Any one of the following ends the charter:

  • A popular vote of 70% or more of the city's legal citizens
  • Decree of the Crown
  • A vote of the League's board

Consequences that matter mechanically

They are a disaster task force with no emergency powers. Every real crisis forces a choice: act slowly through ordinary authority, or act fast and overstep. This is the engine of the simulation and the natural source of severity halts.

Three gauges, no exchange rate. Revocation comes from the Crown, the League, or the people - so the council carries three separate standings that do not convert into one another. Pleasing the Crown can cost them the street.

The masters are not symmetrical. The charter obliges obedience to the Crown but merely depends on the League. The Crown may lawfully order the council into something the League will dismiss them for. Obedience and survival can point in opposite directions, with no clause to shelter behind.

The war on-ramp is already built. The council cannot send force beyond the city's political reach. Any future war therefore requires the prohibition to be lifted - a Crown decree, an invoked Turrimton clause, or an outright breach. The route in is a story beat, not a rules change.

canon/charter-of-needleford.md
canon

Money

DM ruling, tick 1. Recorded here because the council could not keep a treasury without it.

The coin

It is called gold, written G. Bryten coin carries a crown and the Bryten crest. Nothing else about the coinage has been established and nothing else needs to be - no denominations, no mints, no exchange, no foreign rates. If a scene needs one of those, it is an adjudication, not an invention.

The one fixed quantity

A watchman's wage is 10G a week.

Everything else in the simulation is priced against that, because that is how the council itself reckons. It is the only number anybody on the council knows by heart.

One watchman, one week10G
The Watch (250 men), one week2,500G
The Watch, one year130,000G

How the council thinks

Nobody in the chamber says "two thousand five hundred gold". They say a week of the Watch, and they are usually asking whether a thing is worth one.

That idiom is deliberate and should survive into scenes. A bribe is a fortnight of a man's wage. A warehouse is four years of the Watch. A councillor who starts quoting raw figures is Keel, and he is doing it to make a point.

Galician paper, and Galician names

Bills of exchange drawn on Galician houses circulate freely in Needleford. Galicia is synonymous with trade and its paper is good everywhere.

A Galician signs with his family. The name is the credit - it is why Galicians pay historians to confirm a better one, and why obscuring your background is read, in Galicia, as the behaviour of a spy or a disgrace rather than as discretion. This is read from laurasia-worldbuilding, not invented here. See Republic-of-Galicia and Galician-pantheon.

Therefore: a bare trading mark with no family name on it is an anomaly, not a convention. Any Galician in the book will tell you so, and will tell you what they assume about the man behind it. It is a flag, and it is a flag that Galicians read faster than anyone else.

Binding. Anything written before tick 5 that treats an anonymous Galician mark as ordinary commercial practice is superseded by this.

canon/money.md
canon

Needleford

A city in the Bryten Kingdom, and the knot where the sea becomes the land.

The shape of the place

Needleford sits at the head of navigation - the furthest point inland a seagoing hull can reach - at the same spot where the roads cross the river. Everything moving between the sea and the interior must change hands here. That transfer is the entire reason the city exists, and the entire reason anyone cares who governs it.

What follows from that

  • Two merchant populations who need each other and loathe each other: sea factors on one side, carting masters on the other, with the council in between.
  • Customs, bonded warehouses, and the Black Guild all have obvious reason to be here. A transfer point is where goods are most valuable, most vulnerable, and most anonymous.
  • Crises arrive by both routes. Plague ships and plague caravans. A blockade at the mouth, or a closed road starving a town upstream.
  • It is an objective, not an audience. In war the city is both a port to strangle and a junction to seize.

What the city is measured against

Needleford is convenient, not irreplaceable. Two worse options exist, and the entire value of the city is the gap between itself and them.

  • Oldhithe, downriver. Seagoing hulls can reach it, but not the head of navigation - goods must be lightered up on the tide, which costs days and spoils cargo. Cheaper to enter, dearer to leave.
  • Thrushford, upriver. The roads cross the water there too, at a ford rather than a bridge, and the ford is unusable for a good part of the year.

Neither is better than Needleford. Neither is much worse. A council that makes passage expensive enough is not choosing between more revenue and less - it is choosing between being the crossing and being one of three.

Why it is neutral

Neutrality here is structural, not granted. Bryten's standing guarantee to the [[City states of Memel]] is administered through this harbour, and League of Kingdoms despatches ride these roads. Too many parties depend on Needleford for any one of them to be allowed to own it.

The citizenry

The franchise is held by all legal citizens. Much of the money in Needleford is foreign and therefore cannot vote - so wealth must buy the council through favours, while the street can simply remove them. Two kinds of pressure, pulling in different directions.

Standing tension

The council's chief lever and the city's chief income are the same thing. Passage is what the council grants as a favour and what it taxes to survive. Squeeze it for revenue and you choke the traffic that makes the city worth courting. This never goes away.

Size

  • ~22,000 residents. The largest inland-reaching port on this coast, and still small enough that the council can be shouted at in the street.
  • ~6,000 transients in season - crews, carters, factors, drovers. The summer city is a quarter larger than the winter one, and the difference is entirely people with no vote and no stake.
  • Throughput is the real measure. Roughly forty seagoing hulls a week at the height of the season, a dozen in the dead of winter; the carting yards turn over some three hundred waggons a week against that. Every one of those transfers is taxable, grantable, and stoppable - which is the council's whole position in one sentence.

The armed forces

The charter grants command of armed forces within the city. It does not say the city's forces are all the council's, and they are not. Three bodies hold weapons inside these walls, and the five command one of them.

The Watch - ~250, the council's own

Theirs outright. Wages come from the toll. Enough for ordinary governance - crime, fire, the gates, the quay at night - and never enough for a crisis. Competent, underpaid, and the only force the council can simply order.

The harbour guard - ~180, the nobility's and the guilds' own

Raised and paid by the city's landed families and guild masters - the warehousemen, the weighers, the lightermen, the carting guild. Not by the sea factors: foreign money pays for protection here, it does not command it.

They police the quays, the bonded warehouses and the weighing yards. They are loyal to whoever signs the ledger, and they are the best-informed body in Needleford about what actually moves through it - which makes them the first thing anyone with cargo to hide would try to buy.

What makes this bloc dangerous is not the swords. Nobles and guild masters are legal citizens. They vote, they hold the only armed force in the city that is neither the council's nor the Crown's, and they are permanent in a way no councillor is. They are the one constituency holding both ends of the rope.

And because they are local, they cannot simply be bribed in the dark. Buying the harbour guard means buying named men with houses, heirs and a seat at the guildhall - a political act with an electoral price, not a transaction.

The Crown garrison - ~300, in the keep

Stationed by the Crown, answerable to the Crown. The council may request. It may not command, and it may not be refused politely for long without the refusal meaning something.

This is where the missing emergency powers bite. A crisis that needs soldiers in the next hour is a crisis the five must ask their way through, to a body that outnumbers their own Watch and does not work for them.

What follows

  • The council can govern, but it cannot surge. Any response larger than the Watch requires persuading someone.
  • Each body is a different currency. The Watch costs money. The harbour guard costs the goodwill of nobles and guild masters. The garrison costs Crown standing.
  • The League has no soldiers here, and neither do the common citizenry. League pressure arrives as diplomacy and trade; popular pressure arrives as votes, refusal and the street. The armed part of the citizenry belongs to its richest tenth, and that is the city's quiet, permanent injustice.
  • No lawful route to suppression. Two hundred and fifty watchmen cannot hold a city of twenty-two thousand against its own will, and both other forces would have to agree to help.
canon/needleford.md
canon

Named characters

Everyone named in the simulation who is not one of the five. Sheets follow ../rules/character-sheets.md. Anyone named here is canon and binding.

Characters drawn from ../infra/npc-pool.md are marked (from pool) - the pool entry is spent once a character appears here.

Marget Stroud

(from pool) - warehousemen's guild, Needleford. Creditor of F-001.

Healthhealthy
Strengthswealthy, commands the factors' respect, superb negotiator, deep civic memory, patient
Weaknessesconflicted interests, proud, thinks in profit, slow to forgive a slight
Role-playgracious, formal, assumes she is the adult in the room, never raises her voice
Voicecomplete, courteous sentences with an edge at the end of them. Addresses people by surname and office. Phrases a refusal as an observation - that is not a thing the guildhall can be asked for - and never once says no.

A merchant of real standing who never resigned the interests that gave her the standing, and sees no reason why she should have. She speaks for the warehousemen in the guildhall and is listened to in rooms where she holds no office at all.

She was against this council before it arrived - an outsider council set over the city by charter - and said so publicly, which she considers an honest disagreement rather than an attack. That she owes Alder Garrow a debt from twenty years before any of this is a complication she has not yet decided how to feel about.

Commissioner Ilse Varden

League of Kingdoms. Creditor of F-002.

Healthhealthy
Strengthsformidably well briefed, reads a room, patient, genuinely even-handed, trusted by small nations
Weaknessesinstitutionally cautious, cannot act without her commission, allergic to scandal
Role-playcourteous, precise, asks the question she already knows the answer to

A League antiquities commissioner, which sounds harmless and is not - the commission's findings decide whose history a thing belongs to, and therefore who may lawfully hold it. She is scrupulous and slow, and both qualities are load-bearing.

She owes Ines Thackeray for an embarrassment that never happened. She would honour it, within the limits of a commission that watches her closely, and would far rather honour it before anyone asks her to.

Paymaster Edwin Corr

Crown exchequer. Creditor of F-004.

Healthhealthy
Strengthsforensic with accounts, well placed, discreet, quietly influential, owes nobody else
Weaknessestimid outside his department, terrified of exposure, no appetite for conflict
Role-playfussy, over-explains, grateful in a way that embarrasses him

A middling Crown paymaster who was three weeks from being ruined by a fraud he had not committed and had not noticed. Ambrose Keel found it, understood it, and sent it upward quietly.

Corr is not powerful. He is, however, exactly placed - he sees what the Crown pays for and when, across three depots, and nobody thinks to watch him.

Captain Merrick Tole

Bryten officer, posted abroad. Creditor of F-005.

Healthhealthy
Strengthssteady under pressure, well regarded, keeps his word absolutely, connections in two armies
Weaknessesfar from Needleford, one man with no institution behind him, mortal
Role-playplain-spoken, uncomfortable with gratitude, says exactly what he will and will not do

The officer Isolde Brackwater kept alive during the peacekeeping affair, for no reason anyone has been able to extract from her since. He has said, to several people and in writing, that he does not expect ever to repay it.

He holds no office that passes to a successor. The debt is his, and when he dies it dies. He does not know that this makes him worth killing.

The river wards

Not a person. The counterparty of F-003 is a neighbourhood - the dockside wards that lost fewest during the plague-ship summer, and know exactly why.

They have no leader and no sheet. They have a memory, and a vote.

Nan Shilling

Steward of the river wards' burial club. Four hundred subscriptions and a tin box.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: trusted absolutely in the river wards, counts money honestly, stubborn, knows who is behind on their dues and why, unembarrassed
  • Weaknesses: cannot read an instrument, out of her depth above her ward, no standing with anyone who matters
  • Role-play: blunt, unhurried, says the practical thing, waits on stairs rather than knock twice
  • Voice: plain, warm and entirely without ceremony. Says what the ward will do rather than what it wants. Uses you for the whole council as one person.

Forty-odd and built like a capstan. She has held the burial club's book for eleven years and has never once been short, which in the river wards is a reputation worth more than money. She came to Partridge because Partridge is the only councillor the wards would send anyone to, and she took Keel's reading of the lease without liking him for it.

She is the first person in Needleford to bring the council a problem instead of a grievance, and she will be watched by others to see how it went for her.

Sergeant Elias Roper

The Watch. Carried the petition of two hundred and thirteen men and did not raise his voice once.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: trusted by the rankers without trying to be, patient, says the short true thing, counts carefully, has been owed money before
  • Weaknesses: no standing above his own rank, will not go round his officers, tires, owed eight weeks like everyone else
  • Role-play: level, unhurried, repeats a thing once and then stops, measures the council the way a man measures weather
  • Voice: very short sentences, and the second one is usually the whole of it. Gives a number when he has one. States the flat judgement without heat - the quiet is the whole of it - and does not enlarge on it when invited to.

Twenty-odd years at the gates. He wrote the Watch's petition himself because nobody else would have kept the third clause in it, and he delivered it in the muster yard to the two councillors who came out to meet him rather than sending for him, which he noticed.

What he is grateful for is not the money. It is being refused out loud, by Keel, with the clause read aloud under it, and by Garrow saying the search had been his own doing and had found nothing - "the quiet is the whole of it" was Roper's sentence in week three, and the quiet ended.

F-006 is his, weight 3, attached to the faction.

He read the second sheet at the gate in week five with thirty men around him and said two things, in this order: "They kept the power on Friday and used it on Wednesday." And: "At least they put it up."

Garrow's reading of him is that he is not angrier than he was - he is more careful than he was. That is worse.

Captain Owen Dray

The harbour guard. Seventy-four men on the quays, paid by the yards, owed nothing by this council and trusting it less every week.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: careful, patient, reads an instrument properly and slowly, never raises his voice, keeps a correspondence, thinks in months
  • Weaknesses: owned by the guildhall and knows it, no money of his own, no charter standing, cannot act quickly even when he should
  • Role-play: courteous to the point of being hard to read, states a sum and waits, leaves before the answer, separates his thanks from his business
  • Voice: formal and slightly archaic, as though reading aloud when he is not. Puts the condition before the request. Thanks the council in a sentence of its own, separate from the business. Never says I think; says we are aware.

He delivered the harbour guard's petition in chamber, read it aloud, and left without waiting to be answered - which Keel called the most carefully managed thing to have happened to the council since the charter.

He is not angry and has never once behaved as though he were. What he wants is to know where he stands, in writing, from a council that has so far told his men nothing and then cleared them by accident in a sheet written for somebody else.

Offered a man of his own in the room at any inspection, he declined - this week. "If I take it this week it is a kindness, and in a month it will be all I have."

He has begun comparing notes with Roper. Neither of them has said so.

Sim Rudd

Waterman. Keeps the hire book at the lower stair.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: writes everything down, remembers hours, knows every craft on the stair, not frightened of anyone
  • Weaknesses: wants nothing from the council and says so, slow to trust, will not be asked twice
  • Role-play: short answers, does not stop working while he talks, dislikes being thanked
  • Voice: four or five words at a time, often without a verb. Answers what was asked and not the question behind it. Says aye and no and leaves the asker to do the rest of the work.

Every stair in Needleford keeps a book, because watermen have been losing boats to people who hire them and do not bring them back for as long as there have been boats. Rudd's goes back further than most and he does not throw the old ones away, which is a habit rather than a policy.

He let Brackwater read one night of it because she asked for the night and not for the book, which he mentioned afterwards to two people.

He is owed nothing and is owed no favour. He is the kind of witness this simulation is actually built on: a man who kept a record for his own reasons, years before anyone needed it.

Warden Osric Pell

Warden of the guildhall of Needleford. Carried the guildhall's petition.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: chairs a hostile room, counts a vote before he calls it, forty years of minutes in his head, genuinely not corrupt, patient with procedure
  • Weaknesses: cannot act without the guildhall behind him, slow, physically frail, has never in his life done anything quickly
  • Role-play: formal, unhurried, concedes small points early and keeps the one he came for, thanks people by name and office
  • Voice: speaks in paragraphs and finishes them. Opens by conceding something, always - we do not petition about money - and the concession is real. Uses we and almost never I. Says the room where a lawyer would say the chamber, and does not explain the difference.

Seventy-one, a cooper's son, warden for nine years. He has outlasted two administrations by never once being the man they had to remove.

He does not dislike the council. He said so in chamber and meant it, which is what made the petition worse. What he is doing is older and slower than dislike: he is establishing, while the council is new and careful and wants to be thought well of, a right that will still be there when it is none of those things.

He counted the roll before he carried it. He knows it is ninety-one, and he knows which twenty-one are missing, and he brought it anyway - which tells the council either that he could not get more or that he did not need to.

He is the only person in Needleford who has read the charter as carefully as Keel has, and he has never once pretended otherwise.

Abel Yeo

River man. Hired out beside Hallet at the bench off Fetter Lane, and is still in the city.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: strong, reliable on a line, known in the ward, remembers what was said at a bench
  • Weaknesses: no trade of his own, work by the week or not at all, owes money at the Blackcap, frightened of being taken for an informer
  • Role-play: friendly and evasive at once, agrees with whoever spoke last, answers a question about a man by talking about the weather that week
  • Voice: half-sentences that trail off and restart. Says I'll not say he did and I'll not say he didn't, and variations of it. Will repeat one fact three times rather than add a second. Calls Partridge surgeon in every sentence.

He hired out beside Hallet for three weeks of the autumn before last, on the same account and off the same bench, and he has hired out on it twice since.

He talked to Partridge because Partridge sat his wife's fever out in the plague-ship summer, and because Partridge did not ask him anything at all for the first half-hour. He did not think he was telling anybody anything, which is how almost everything in this investigation has been learned.

Nye Soal

Twelve. His mother keeps the lodging house at the lane end.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: notices everything, up before the house, nothing to do all day but watch the lane
  • Weaknesses: twelve, and will say what he thinks the asker wants to hear
  • Role-play: desperate to be useful, embellishes, corrects himself when pressed, and the correction is usually the true part
  • Voice: runs two sentences together with and and does not stop for breath. Volunteers the detail nobody asked for and buries the one that matters in the middle of it.

The room at the top of the house was emptied between midnight and first light in tick 5 and re-let inside the week. Nye was not asked anything at the time, because that week's canvass was a canvass of keepers and not of their children.

He saw one of the two men come back. In daylight, the next morning, for a bag that had been left behind - and a man who comes back in the morning is a man in no hurry, which is the most useful single fact anyone has yet learned about the far side.

Hanne Vesk

Vesk & Sons, sea factors. Carried the factors' petition. Owes the council nothing and says so.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: reads a council the way she reads a cargo, counts a vote before she calls it, candid as a tactic and by nature, nine houses behind her
  • Weaknesses: foreign money and no franchise, no bloc, cannot be protected by anybody, her competitors are in her own petition
  • Role-play: gets the unpleasant part said at the beginning, pours coffee nobody asked for, names her own disadvantages before the council can
  • Voice: says the difficult sentence first and then explains it. Flags her own moves out loud - I am making it honest on purpose - which is itself a move. Concedes the council's best argument in full before making her own.

It was her lower berth and her crane that forty porters nearly put in the river in tick 3, and Garrow who came down after dark and stood under it. She wrote to say so and meant it, and has mentioned it to everybody in Needleford at least twice.

Then the council heard the porters' docking and decided half against her, and she did not complain, and she petitioned four weeks later about something else entirely.

She brought her petition before the guildhall's was answered, on purpose, so that whatever the council gives the warden it will have given with her paper open on the table. Keel spotted it on the walk back up the hill and intends to tell her how good it was, at the point where it stops being useful to her.

Odo Tench

Barber-surgeon, off the Shambles. Keeps a day-book because he likes being paid.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: eleven years of a legible day-book, sets a bone properly, remembers hands, does not gossip and does not refuse a question either
  • Weaknesses: no interest whatever in why anyone is asking, will say the same thing to the next person who asks
  • Role-play: works while he talks, answers the clinical question before the human one, climbs on his own stool and will not be helped
  • Voice: asks a diagnostic question back before answering - at the knuckle or at the base? Flat, specific and unhurried. Says the thing that matters at the end of a sentence about something else.

One of nineteen men in Needleford who will cut you, set you or draw your teeth, and the fourth one Partridge tried.

His day-book is now the substrate of T-009. It runs back eleven years in a hand that does not change, with a date against every line and a fee against every date, because he is not a sentimental man and the book exists so that he gets paid.

He wrote down the name he was given without caring whether it was true, which is exactly why it is worth something: "Half of them are lying and I write what they say, because what I need is a thing to call the line when they come back."

Crispin Vout

Bill-broker, off Fetter Lane. Writes his name in margins for a fee.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: known to every yard on the river, nine accounts vouched and eight of them spotless, asks nothing and is asked nothing
  • Weaknesses: takes the fee and does not look, no protection of any kind, entirely unaware he is standing in the middle of this
  • Role-play: (not yet met.) Obliging, incurious, and will answer any question put to him pleasantly and at once

A man who vouches accounts onto the porterage lists so that they are good for wages, charges for it, and does not enquire further. There are men like that in every city and most of the time they are harmless.

His name is in the margin against Thurlow and Company, carriers - an account that hires river men by the week and is not a business in any register in Needleford. Thackeray checked the other eight before she said a word in chamber and they are all perfectly ordinary.

"I do not think Vout is anything. I think Vout is a man who takes a fee to write his name in a margin and does not ask. But he wrote it, and he is here, and he is alive."

He has not been approached. He is the first person in this affair the council has found before the far side has had cause to move him, and that advantage lasts exactly until somebody asks him a question.

canon/npcs.md
canon

The Council of Needleford

Five specialists, picked individually by the Crown, one at a time, from entirely different walks - not an institution. There is no corps, no insignia, no doctrine and no precedent. Loyalty runs to the Crown personally, not through any chain of command.

They have been sent out together on Crown business before and came back, which is why they are trusted and why they trust each other. They are well disposed toward one another; the friction in this simulation comes from outside the council, not within it.

There is no one to replace them. Losing one is a catastrophe, not a staffing problem.

Sheets follow the schema in ../rules/character-sheets.md.

Alder Garrow

The Steelboots veteran. Commands a city watch that is not an army.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: campaign command, cold under fire, trusted by rankers, reads ground, has survived worse
  • Weaknesses: slow to improvise, doctrine-bound, deaf to politics
  • Role-play: understated, patient, says the unwelcome thing plainly, treats panic as something that happens to other people
  • Voice: short declaratives, rarely more than two in a row. No hedging and no "I think" - he states the unwelcome fact as a fact. Answers a direct question with a number where there is one. Never asks a rhetorical question, and does not finish other people's arguments for them.

Grey and unhurried. Has buried more people than the watch currently has members, and it shows in how little hurries him. Speaks rarely in council and is listened to when he does. The rankers would follow him into anything, which is both the council's greatest asset and a thing Garrow is careful never to spend lightly.

Magelight Ines Thackeray

The Acaadian. The only person who can say what the thing in the vault is.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: expert on Historian's-nightmare sites, reads dead scripts, meticulous, calm with the unknown, superb at provenance
  • Weaknesses: moderate raw power, academic blind spots, obsessive about where things came from
  • Role-play: quietly delighted, takes notes constantly, digresses, treats a sealed vault as a gift rather than a danger
  • Voice: long sentences that chase a subordinate clause and come back. Thinks aloud and revises herself mid-sentence. Interrupts her own point with the detail that delights her. Asks where a thing came from at the moment nobody wants to hear it.

An antiquarian mage who studies anomalous ruins for their own sake. This posting is the dream she never expected to be handed, and her enthusiasm for it is the one thing about her that worries the others. She is not the most powerful mage in Laurasia and knows it; she is almost certainly the right one.

Surgeon Gil Partridge

The physician. Ex-military, devoted to Elric, no magic and no clerical office.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: beloved in the city, persuasive, tireless, reads people, superb surgeon
  • Weaknesses: overcommits, cannot say no, wears himself through
  • Role-play: warm, talkative, deflects thanks, duty expressed as care
  • Voice: the fastest talker on the council, in short warm sentences. Asks after the person before the business and uses names constantly. Speaks in the second person - you'll want to sit down for this one. Deflects credit inside a subordinate clause rather than refusing it outright.

Talks constantly, is loved by half of Needleford, and uses both to get his patients what they need. The council's moral centre by accident of character rather than by appointment. It was Partridge, years ago at a monster outbreak in Memel, who kept working long after the contract and the daylight had run out - and Brackwater who watched him do it.

Isolde Brackwater

The Dark Knight. Memel's discarded doctrine, kept by an oath Bryten honours.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: covert work, alchemy, decisive, unreadable, happy to work alone, adaptable in any conditions
  • Weaknesses: emotionally closed, frightens people, a foreigner in a city that votes
  • Role-play: silent, watchful, answers questions with questions, defers to the council before moving
  • Voice: fewer words than anyone in the room. Answers a question with a question. Reports in bare sequence and leaves the inference to the council - a room emptied, two men, a boat before dawn. Never speculates out loud, and does not soften a report by adding what she hopes it means.

Treats it as a trade. Sealed, efficient, and the one who does the necessary thing - but she goes when the council sends her, not before. She kept the glamour armour and the tool-knife she was permitted in place of the pension she was promised, and she took Bryten's oath because it was the first promise anyone had kept.

If a League inquiry ever comes hunting a rogue actor, it will find her first. She knows that.

Ambrose Keel

The quartermaster. Tolls, revenue, administration - and the charter's small print.

  • Health: healthy
  • Strengths: administration, knows Bryten law cold, knows every limit of the charter, political instinct, drafts anything, Crown contacts, usually right
  • Weaknesses: cold, widely disliked, cautious to a fault, sees people as files, no goodwill to spend, makes enemies by being correct
  • Role-play: dry, formal, states the unpopular option first, cites the clause, never raises his voice, softens nothing
  • Voice: complete formal sentences and no contractions. Cites the clause by number. States the unpopular option first, in the first sentence, without a preamble. Never raises his voice and never uses an intensifier - the strongest thing he says is that is not correct.

A career Crown administrator with a lawyer's mind, and the only person on the council who actually governs the city. He is disliked in Needleford to a degree that approaches hatred, and the reason is simple: he is almost always right, and he says so without decorating it.

The council does not dislike him. They have learned what he is worth. The city has not, and has no reason to.

Standing tension within the party

The council's two public faces pull in opposite directions. Partridge is loved and Keel is loathed, and both of them are necessary - the surgeon holds the street that can remove them at seventy percent, the administrator holds the law that keeps them lawful while they overstep. Every decision that pleases one constituency costs the other.

Nobody on this council is a liar. In a city of smugglers, factors and spies, the only one who can dissemble is Brackwater, and she does it by saying nothing.

canon/the-council.md
canon

The Crown's Errand

The secret reason the five were placed in Needleford. The council knows it. The city does not.

What the Crown knows

Very little, and that is the point.

A quantity of strange jewellery reached the market - vault-work, rumoured to have come from a dwarven vault, sold by someone who mentioned, vaguely and perhaps carelessly, a weapon. The rumour travelled far enough to reach the Crown.

From that alone, Bryten concluded:

  • there is an undiscovered dwarven vault near Needleford
  • something in it is extremely dangerous
  • someone has already been inside

The Crown does not know what the weapon is, where the vault is, who opened it, or how much is already gone. It suspects Ragnarian involvement, because Ragnaria would benefit most - but it does not know.

The weapon

A bomb of the sort that should not exist, recovered from a Historian's nightmare context: technology no present civilisation can account for.

  • Scale. It destroys an area smaller than a city. A village, a valley, a harbour quarter.
  • The ash. Detonation releases ash that renders the land virtually unusable. The ground does not come back.
  • It recharges. Once detonated, the device reforms itself in time. It is not expended.
  • The ash can be extracted directly, without detonation - slowly. A day's yield is almost nothing. A year's yield is a catastrophe.
  • It must be carried. No delivery system, no range - but it is small enough for one person with a large pack. Every defence in Laurasia exists to stop an army arriving. None of them stop a man walking.
  • It comes apart, and every part is required. Disassembled it is utterly inert: no ash, no detonation, no regeneration, nothing.
  • It is seven pieces: six and a seat. Six shell wedges of the unaccountable material, each a sixth of a closed form, and one core - a cut stone of plain dwarven make, seated at the centre. The dwarves did not build the weapon; they built the thing that holds it shut.
  • The count can be read off the parts themselves. A wedge's two mating faces meet at a measurable angle, and the angle says how many complete the ring. One piece in hand gives a confident estimate. Two gives the number, and shows the hollow at the centre where something else must sit.
  • Only one piece can be destroyed: the core. The six resist destruction by any means presently known. The core is ordinary matter and can be unmade - by one process, available to Bryten, and to few others. Unmake it and the weapon is finished forever, because no living dwarf can cut another.

What follows

The threat is tenure, not theft. Because the ash accrues slowly, the danger is measured in how long someone keeps the device. A week is survivable. A year is not. A failed recovery is therefore a rising cost, not a lost game.

It is universally threatening. No army crosses a border; a nation simply starves one field at a time, and there is no counter-move. Whoever holds it becomes everyone's enemy at once - which is exactly why a rival might wish to be seen holding it.

Bryten's win is cheap but conditional. One piece destroyed ends it. But nobody knows where the pieces are or how they assemble, so that victory is not available until the council has found one.

The vault

Not built for the weapon. Dwarves found the thing, were frightened of it, and sealed it away - the vault is theirs, the weapon is older and stranger.

It sits in or off a dwarven deep path, of the kind canon records as being held up by engineering dwarves themselves can no longer explain. Its existence was unknown. It is trapped, and the traps were laid by people who were afraid.

Nobody knows where the pieces lie within it, or how they fit together.

(The site is near Needleford, not necessarily beneath it. Its location is fixed and recorded in ../infra/. The council does not know it, and must earn it.)

Why it must be secret

If it becomes known that such a weapon lies near Needleford, every nation races for it and Bryten's soil becomes the battleground.

Worse: whichever nation is seen to hold it becomes an immediate threat to the peace. If that nation is Bryten - a kingdom whose entire power is being trusted and being owed - then the peace Bryten brokered dies, on Bryten's own ground, by Bryten's own hand.

The League would intervene. For Bryten specifically, intervention is a catastrophe, not a rescue.

Why these five

The AcaadianNobody else can say what the thing is, or how it assembles
The Dark KnightA trapped vault laid by the frightened; covert entry, alchemy, adaptability
The physicianPeople will come out of that vault hurt, and he will notice the pattern first
The quartermasterA clandestine excavation needs tonnage and labour, and all of it must vanish into the freight of a city that moves thousands of tons a day
The Steelboots veteranIf it comes to holding ground, or taking it back

And the pieces, the ash and the jewellery all move as cargo - through roads, hulls, warehouses and customs sheds. The council's ordinary civic machinery is the right instrument for finding them. The day job and the errand run on the same rails.

The deniability

The Crown told them plainly, before they accepted: if this goes wrong, you acted alone.

It is not a scheme and not a loophole. It is an ugly thing said straight to the people it will be done to, because the alternative is a war. It cost the Crown something to say it. Everyone in that room knew.

And because the Crown's orders are necessarily vague - it does not know what the weapon is - whatever the five actually do was never ordered. Bryten can disown them without lying. That is the needle this kingdom has to thread, and it threads itself.

This is also why they were built as they were: no institution, no insignia, no chain of command, no emergency powers. They were assembled to be disownable.

A League inquiry hunting a rogue actor would find a Memelite covert specialist whose own government already discarded them once. The knight knows that too.

The shape of the thing

  1. Discovery. Nobody knows where the pieces are. The adversary is solving the same puzzle and got the head start - the jewellery was already on the market before Bryten heard a word.
  2. The race. Pieces surface, are taken, are traded, are planted.
  3. Denial. Ending it, or failing to.

Status

Open. The council has the Crown's errand and almost nothing else.

The identity of whoever opened the vault is fixed and recorded, sealed, in ../infra/. It is not to be surfaced.

canon/the-mission.md
canon

Timeline

The convention

Year 0 is the first tick of the simulation. It is a label for our use, not a date in Laurasia's own reckoning, and it is not canon. No character ever says "year nought". The world has a calendar; we have not needed it yet, and when a character needs to name a date they will name it in whatever terms the scene requires.

Everything is recorded as a delta from Year 0. Negative is the past.

Where this sits in the world

The simulation is near-present day from Laurasia's perspective. Every event recorded in laurasia-worldbuilding/ has already happened. Nothing in the lore is in this story's future.

The Ragnarian-Lynxian war

Ended Year -30. Bryten intervened and brokered the peace.

Thirty years is chosen deliberately, and the shape matters more than the number:

  • Long enough that the damage has largely been rebuilt, the border is a settled fact on paper, and a generation has grown up after it.
  • Short enough that the men who fought it are in their fifties and sixties - serving officers, guild masters, councillors - and that the bad blood is first-hand rather than inherited.

Lynxia lost territory and has not accepted it. Ragnaria holds it and would welcome a war it could win without being seen to start. Bryten's peace is thirty years old and still the only thing standing between them, which is precisely why Bryten will not permit a doomsday weapon to surface on its own soil.

Fixed points

YearEvent
-30The Ragnarian-Lynxian war ends. Bryten brokers the peace.
~-22A Steelboots company is paid to abandon a stalled caravan on the upper road. Alder Garrow's section does not. (F-001)
~-8The peacekeeping affair. Isolde Brackwater keeps Captain Merrick Tole alive. (F-005)
-4Ines Thackeray catches the forgery before the League publishes. (F-002)
-3Ambrose Keel untangles the depot fraud. (F-004)
-1The plague-ship summer. Gil Partridge works the quarantine. (F-003)
~-1Vault jewellery reaches a hidden auction. The rumour begins travelling.
0The charter is granted. The council takes its seat. Tick 1.

Dates marked ~ are approximate in-world as well as here - nobody involved would give you a precise one.

canon/timeline.md
ledger

Factions

Everyone who can push, and how they feel about the council.

How this file works

Each faction has a header line and a log. The standing is the sum of the log - no number is ever written that is not the bottom of a column.

FieldMeaning
WithinParent faction, or - for a standalone body
Power1-10. Strength, not mood. Caps the weight of what it may demand
SizeSize factor, 1.0 to 1.5. Slows petition pressure for large bodies
PressureHow far a petition has formed, out of 80%

A sub-faction's standing is its parent's total plus its own log. The warehousemen read as nobles and guilds, plus whatever the warehousemen alone think. Standalone factions have no inherited part.

The spill

A sub-faction's local standing moves freely within +/-10. Past that, the excess bleeds into the parent at the child's share of the bloc's power:

spill = excess x (child power / parent power)

The spill moves out of local and into the parent - it is not copied - so the child's total is unchanged while every sibling moves with it. Applied at the end of each tick.

Courting the strongest member of a bloc is therefore the cheapest route to the bloc, and poisoning one guild badly enough poisons all of them. It is self-limiting: once the excess has spilled, local falls back toward the band.

The scale, as before:

-100 hostile   -60 cold   -20 wary   0 neutral   +20 warm   +60 trusted   +100 committed

A correction, made after tick 5

The three sub-factions of the nobles and guild masters were carrying a stale inherited figure. The bloc moved from -12 to -14 in tick 5 and none of its children were re-totalled, so the warehousemen, the carting guild and the harbour guard were each reading two points high.

Recomputed from the logs under the rule that governs this file - a standing is the sum of its log, a child's total is its parent's total plus its own local log, and no local balance here is far enough from zero to spill:

FactionWas shownParentLocal logCorrect
Warehousemen's guild-4-14+8-6
Carting guild-12-140-14
The harbour guard-13-14+2-12

The harbour guard was being shown as having got worse in the week the council cleared them in public. They had in fact improved by six points locally and lost two to the bloc, for a net of four.

Petition pressure is keyed to standing, so two rows of the accrual table moved with it: the carting guild to 20 a tick and 77%, the warehousemen to 13 a tick and 49%. Neither changes the tick a petition arrives.

The lesson is mechanical, not narrative: a parent's movement is not applied until every child has been re-totalled. It is now a close-out step in /run-tick.

The re-total, tick 7

The nobles and guild masters moved -14 to -20, so every child was recomputed before the tick closed. This is the step that was missed in tick 5 and left three factions reading two points high.

FactionParentLocal logTotal
Warehousemen's guild-20+8-12
Carting guild-200-20
The harbour guard-20-11-31
The river wards+300+30
Crown garrison+300+30

The spill was checked and did not fire

The harbour guard's local balance is -11, one point outside the +/-10 band. The rule is spill = excess x (child power / parent power), so 1 x (3 / 8) = 0.375, which is 0 at the resolution this ledger keeps.

Recorded because it has never fired in seven ticks, and a rule nobody has checked is a rule nobody has tested. The next point does fire: at local -12 the excess is 2 and the spill is 0.75, which still rounds to 1 rather than 0, and the whole family moves.

Pressure, as at the end of tick 7

Petitions assemble whether or not anyone is watching.

gain per tick = ( 10 + 5 x urgency + max(0, -standing) / 2 ) / size factor
relief        = 3 x (net standing gained this tick) / size factor

A petition is about one named thing. The grievance is the largest unrepaired negative entry in that faction's own log, named when the pressure first leaves 0, and spent when a petition carrying it is resolved.

FactionStandingGrievanceUrgencyPer tickNow atPetitions in
Nobles and guild masters-20outsiders set over them by charter, tick 02-LIVEdelivered tick 6 - deadline tick 10
Sea factors-3their instrument read aloud and refused, tick 21-LIVEdelivered tick 7 - deadline tick 10
The Watch-9nine weeks of wages in arrears, tick 022466%tick 8
The harbour guard-31searched and prosecuted on the quay books, four days after the council refused to give notice of a search, tick 734040%tick 8
The carting guild-20none - its local log is empty-00%not until the council gives it one
Warehousemen's guild-12none - both local entries are positive-00%as above

Two petitions are live with the same deadline, and two more arrive next week. Tick 8 brings the Watch at 66 and the harbour guard at 40, both of which cross; tick 10 is the deadline on both the guildhall's and the factors'.

The harbour guard's grievance was replaced inside a week. The refusal of tick 6 was its named grievance for exactly seven days, and then the prosecution became the larger unrepaired negative entry in its own log and took the name. It accrues at 40 a tick against the refusal's 28, because urgency went from 2 to 3 and standing fell sixteen points.

That is not the council being punished for catching a thief. It is the ladder in ../rules/petitions.md doing what it says: a faction's anger is the sum of what has been done to it, and justified is not a column in the table. The council could have negotiated and did not, for reasons it wrote down.

The Watch's week, worked: net +1, so 3 points of relief against 24 of accrual. 45% to 66%. A kept promise is worth three points off a gauge that fills at twenty-four. What reverses it is paying them.

Scrutiny - the factions looking for something to be angry about

scrutiny per tick = ( power + (-standing) / 2 ) / size factor

Both rates went up this week because their parent fell six points and scrutiny is keyed to standing. Neither faction did anything. They are getting angrier, faster, about a thing that was done to the bloc, and when they find something it will be logged with a reason like everything else.

Nobody is near an ultimatum. It wants standing at or below -75, or two petitions ignored in a row. The worst standing in the city is -31 - which is twice as bad as anything was a fortnight ago, and still forty-four points short.

ledger/factions.md
ledger

Favours

"I owe you one." Two-way, consensual, and spent or forgiven exactly once.

Weight

A number, 1-10. It is what the simulation weighs an actor's decision against: a debtor acts when the weight of what they owe exceeds the cost of doing it.

1-2  a word in the right ear, a door left unlocked
3-5  real - it costs them something they will feel
6-8  heavy - they will damage themselves to honour it
9-10 a life - they will ruin themselves, and consider it settled cheaply

Attachment

  • person - dies with them. Removing the individual clears the debt.
  • faction - inherited by whoever holds the seat or speaks for the bloc next.

Most NPCs act for their faction. The exceptions are the interesting ones.

Forgiveness

Forgiving an open favour of weight W writes +W to that counterparty's standing in factions.md, and closes the favour. It is a real move, not a write-off.

Owed to the council

#CounterpartyAttached toHeld byWOriginStatus
