  • Dying does not become dead inside the tick it happened. A full tick must pass, start to finish. Hit in tick N, they cannot die before the end of N+1.
  • Anyone may stabilise them. A check, d10 + fit, against a high difficulty. Brackwater has alchemy. Garrow has seen a great many wounds. Keel is useless.
  • Partridge cannot fail it. He is a superb surgeon and this is what that means on the page.
  • Success moves them to bloodied, and costs the treater one action. The patient has none to spend.
  • Treated in tick N they lose that tick's actions. Missed, they lose N and N+1 - six actions - and are dead at the end of it.

Everything above dying

Patient spendsPartridge spendsLevels
Rest1 action-1, and that is the ceiling for the tick
Treated by Partridge1 action1 action3

Healing does not happen on its own. A councillor who does not spend the action does not mend, however quiet the week.

Three levels is everything. The worst a non-dying councillor can be is bloodied, so one action each restores anyone to healthy with a level to spare. Partridge may do it three times in a tick, to three different people, or twice on himself.

What this means, and it is the point

The cost of a fight is no longer weeks. It is Partridge's week.

He does not heal faster per action - he removes the ceiling, and the action economy around him is a different game.

Which makes him the single point of failure, hard. If Partridge is the one dying, nobody auto-stabilises him, and the entire council drops to one level a tick with a rest action apiece. Taking Partridge out first is the correct opening move for any enemy who understands this council, and he is the most beloved and most exposed man in the city.

That is not an oversight. It is the design.

Open

(nothing open. Party combat is settled.)

Parked

  • Sub-fights. A long engagement split into separate fights, with acts refreshing between them. Parked by the DM until a big fight is actually in prospect, so the rule is written against a real one.
  • Plan the fight. Stations declared before the first beat - who leads, who shields, whose standing effect runs - front-loading the whole decision. Parked as a later addition on top of acts, not instead of them.
rules/party-combat.md
rules

Petitions and Requests

How a faction makes the council do something it did not choose to do.

The ladder

Nothing here needs authoring. The council's own conduct manufactures its opposition, in four steps:

  1. A faction at standing 0 or above may make a polite request.
  2. Refusing a request costs nothing in actions, and a little standing.
  3. Enough refusals carry the faction below 0.
  4. Below 0, it petitions - and a petition cannot be waved away.

A council that is pleasant to deal with never sees a petition. A council that is busy, or correct, or merely tired, will see a great many.

Requests - for factions at 0 or above

  • Made in person or by letter. No signatures, no threshold, no clock.
  • Conceding or refusing costs no action. It is a conversation, not an instrument.
  • Refusal costs -1 to -3 standing, by the size of the ask.
  • Repeated refusal is how a friendly faction stops being one.

Petitions - for factions below 0

Who may raise one

Any faction, sub-faction or organisation: a bloc, a guild, a ward, a company, a crew.

It requires 80% of those who could sign. Stroud's petition carried nine of eleven warehousemen - 82% - and she stated plainly that the two who did not sign were not asked.

That threshold is a defensive lever. A divided faction cannot petition at all, so splitting one is a legitimate counter-move: buy, promote or frighten enough members and the thing collapses below 80% before it is ever delivered. Doing so is visible, and is its own kind of answer.

The signatures and the pressure are two different things. Pressure is a 0-100 gauge of how far a petition has assembled; the 80% is a test of how many would sign when it is delivered. Splitting a faction defeats the signatures and leaves the gauge where it is - so a split buys a week, not a year, and the petition re-forms the moment the faction closes ranks.

Power - what a faction may ask for

Each faction has a power of 1-10, recorded with its standing and its log in ../ledger/factions.md. It is strength, not mood: the guilds are powerful whether or not they like the council. Power caps the weight of the demand, on the same 1-10 scale as favours.

A faction cannot demand more than its power. A ward of fishwives may ask for its stall dues back. It may not ask for the harbour guard to be disbanded.

The grievance

A petition is about one thing, and the thing is named before the petition exists.

The moment a faction's pressure first leaves 0, its grievance is named and recorded beside the pressure in ../ledger/factions.md. Not at delivery - at formation, because a grievance nobody has written down is a grievance the council cannot answer early.

The simulation does not invent it. The grievance is the largest unrepaired negative entry in that faction's own standing log. The log already records why every point moved; the grievance is simply the worst of those reasons still standing. That makes it non-arbitrary, visible on the page, and checkable.

A grievance is the thing, not the ask. Nine weeks of wages in arrears is a grievance. Publish a schedule is an ask. The demands are derived from the grievance when the petition is delivered:

The grievance is aboutThe ask is about
Moneypay it, refund it, cut the rate
Process - done to us without warningnotice, or a man of ours in the room
Status - we are outsiders to thisvoice: a bench, a hearing, an office
Safetymen, walls, patrols, lamps

This table is open, like the substrate and intrusiveness tables in investigations.md. A grievance that plainly produces an ask not on it gets a new row rather than the nearest fit.

A grievance that cannot be answered with money is answered by changing who has standing to speak. The nobles' oldest entry is a council of outsiders set over them by charter, which cannot be reversed short of resignation - so their ask is voice: no measure touching rates, storage or the quays laid before council without the guildhall seeing it first, and a guildhall man in the chamber when it is. Weight 6 or 7 of their power 8.

Conceding that resolves the grievance permanently and hands away a share of the council's chief instrument. It is irreversible, it is canon-adjacent, and it is the only kind of concession every other faction below zero can see and copy. The grievances that look unresolvable are the ones that generate the largest decisions in the game.

One grievance, once

A grievance is spent when the petition carrying it is RESOLVED - conceded, refused, or answered in part successfully. Not by IGNORE, which spends nothing, and not by a failed answer in part, which resolves nothing: the petition stands, the clock runs on and the next attempt is one harder.

That distinction is load-bearing. If an attempted answer spent the grievance, a council could deliberately fail a check to burn a faction's complaint for one action, which is the cheapest possible way to make a grievance disappear and exactly the wrong incentive.

Spent means spent, whether or not it was satisfied. A refused petition does not come back as the same petition. The faction must name a different grievance, from a different entry in its log, and start again from 0.

Two consequences, both intended:

  • A faction has a finite number of grievances - exactly the number of unrepaired negative entries in its log. A council that stops manufacturing entries eventually runs a faction out of things to petition about. A council that is pleasant to deal with never sees a petition, and this is the arithmetic of that sentence.
  • A faction may sit below 0 with no grievance left to name. Its pressure resets to 0. There is no petition forming, because there is nothing to petition about. It is still angry - the standing says so - and it has nothing to say with.

And a ruling, once accepted, is hard to undo. A conceded demand becomes a standing commitment. Reversing it is a severity-scored act in its own right and mints a fresh grievance at high urgency from the faction it was taken from. The council cannot grant a bench on Monday and withdraw it in the spring.

Pressure - how a petition forms

Petitions take time to assemble. Each tick a faction accumulates pressure toward the 80% it needs, from 0:

if standing >= 0        gain = 0
if no unspent grievance pressure = 0    (there is no petition forming)
otherwise               gain = ( 10 + 5 x urgency + (-standing) / 2 ) / size
  • urgency 0-4, how pressing the grievance is. It belongs to the grievance, not the faction - a body may have a slow grievance now and a furious one later
  • standing the faction's current gauge value
  • size factor 1.0 for a small body, up to 1.5 for a whole bloc

Nothing accrues at standing 0 or above. The ladder already said a faction at 0 or above makes requests rather than petitions; the gauge now agrees with it.

Size cuts both ways. A large faction is slower to reach 80% because there are more people to convince - and what it may demand when it arrives is correspondingly larger.

Worked: the nobles and guilds at -14, urgency 2, size factor 1.3, gain about 21 points a tick.

Relief - a faction that is treated better is less far along

relief   = 3 x (net standing gained this tick) / size factor
pressure = max(0, pressure + gain - relief)

Net, not gross. A faction experiences the week whole. Roper read tick 5 as "they kept the power on Friday and used it on Wednesday", which is a net judgement, and the mechanic agrees with him.

Relief is three times the rate the deficit accrues at, on purpose. At parity a good week would merely slow the inevitable, and the council's most natural instinct - fix the grievance - would do nothing to the clock. At three times, a genuinely good week pushes the petition back.

Worked, the Watch in tick 5: net +7, size 1.0, so 21 points of relief against 26 of accrual. Pressure moves 21% to 26% instead of 21% to 47%. A very good week nearly holds the line and does not reverse it, because the grievance - seven weeks of wages - is still there. What reverses it is paying them.

Resolving the grievance sets pressure to 0, at any time, by any means, including after delivery, in which case the petition is withdrawn. The bright line is that the entry that caused it must be reversed, not compensated. The Watch's arrears grievance resolves when the arrears reach zero, not when a schedule is published. Publishing bought +3 standing and some relief, and that is the right severity for it.

Manufacturing a grievance

A faction with nothing to petition about goes looking.

Below 0 and out of unspent grievances, a faction accrues scrutiny in place of pressure:

scrutiny per tick = ( power + (-standing) / 2 ) / size factor

At 100 it finds something - a slight, an irregularity, a clause read uncharitably, a question asked of a clerk who should not have answered it. What it finds is logged as a negative standing entry with a reasoned row, like every other, and that entry is then its grievance. Scrutiny resets to 0.

Power is the dominant term, because finding a grievance takes people, time and somebody who reads minutes. A ward of fishwives takes years. The guildhall takes months.

A manufactured grievance is real, and it is thinner:

Urgencycapped at 1
Demand weightcapped at power - 2

A complaint that had to be hunted for is a smaller complaint, and the council can tell the difference when it arrives.

This is also the only decay in the mechanic, and deliberately the only one. A neglected faction gets angrier on its own, slowly, in a way that is written down with a reason - rather than by a drift term that bleeds every sour faction every week. The council has fifteen actions and eleven factions; a maintenance treadmill would not be difficulty, it would be arithmetic the council cannot win. Measured in months, not weeks, and only for factions that have genuinely run out of things to say.

Nobody is told what anyone's scrutiny is. The council finds out when the standing moves and a reason appears under it.

The ultimatum - last resort

An ultimatum is not a petition. It is what a faction does when petitioning has stopped meaning anything.

Two routes in, so it is reachable by the council's conduct and not only by grinding a number down:

Standing at or below -75past cold, approaching hostile
Two petitions IGNORED in a rowfrom the same faction, consecutively. Refusing is an answer; silence twice is not

How it differs from a petition:

  • No 80% test. A desperate faction does not need a quorum, and splitting it is no longer a defence.
  • One demand, inside the faction's power, and the grievance is spent on it.
  • A one-tick clock.
  • Two answers only - concede or refuse. There is no answering in part, and ignoring an ultimatum is refusing it.

Conceding under duress still resolves the grievance, and the faction still owes a favour - weighted heavily, because a favour owed by somebody who had the council over a barrel is worth having.

Refusing means the faction acts. What it does is drawn from what it is and from mechanics that already exist - a riot answered by the Watch, a guild withholding what it collects, an armed body refusing duty, factors taking their money somewhere the toll cannot reach it. The specific consequence is ruled when the first ultimatum is refused and not before, because inventing the table first is how you get a mechanic nothing fits.

Untested, and will stay untested for a while. The worst standing in the city is -14. An ultimatum is a late-game instrument and the thresholds above are a reasoned guess, not a calibrated number.

Parentage - blocs and their children

A grievance belongs to the log that holds it. That one line answers the whole question, and it falls straight out of the spill rule in ../ledger/factions.md: a child's standing is its parent's total plus its own local log, so a child's anger is part inherited and part its own.

  • An entry in the parent's log is the bloc's grievance. It was never the child's to begin with.
  • An entry in a child's local log is that child's alone.

Only the holder petitions.

A child with no unspent grievance in its own local log cannot petition at all, however bad its standing is. Its anger is the bloc's anger, and the bloc speaks.

Its gauge resets to 0. The warehousemen's two local entries are both positive; the carting guild's local log is empty. Neither has anything of its own to say, and their entire negative standing is the bloc's.

Resolving a parent's grievance resolves it for every child, because they were carrying it rather than holding it. Resolving a child's does nothing for the bloc beyond the standing it moves - and the spill moves that automatically, so no further rule is needed.

A child's demand is capped by the child's power, not the bloc's. The harbour guard at power 3 cannot ask for what the guildhall at power 8 can, which is a second reason a bloc-level grievance has to be petitioned at bloc level.

What this does to the pile-up

It dissolves it, by being correct rather than by capping arrivals:

FactionPressureLocal grievancePetitions?
Nobles and guild masters81%outsiders set over them by charter, tick 0yes - tick 6
The carting guild0% (was 77%)none - its local log is emptyno
Warehousemen's guild0% (was 49%)none - both local entries are positiveno
The harbour guardlivethe quay books searched, tick 3already delivered

Three petitions from one family tree in one week becomes one bloc petition and one live local one, which is what the fiction was always describing: the guildhall asking the constitutional question, and its armed body asking the practical one.

The clock

A delivered petition names a deadline. Three ticks is standard. A demand of weight 7 or above gets four or five, because nobody expects the council to restructure the toll in a fortnight.

What a petition demands is always mechanical - move a rate, repeal a clause, dismiss an officer, open a yard, fund a thing. Conceding executes it.

The four answers

CostsEffect
CONCEDE1 actionThe demand is executed. The council takes either a favour at the weight of the ask or +weight standing.
ANSWER IN PART1 action, 1 tickGrant some, refuse some, and talk them into it. A check. On success it lands exactly as a concession
REFUSE1 actionStanding falls by the weight of the ask. An answer, at least.
IGNOREnothingStanding falls heavily, and the grievance keeps its pressure.

CONCEDE

Goodwill from a concession is +the weight of the demand, mirroring the standing a refusal costs. A weight-6 concession is +6 standing, which through relief is 18 points off the gauge on top of resolving the grievance outright. Conceding is supposed to buy peace, and until this was written down it did not: the amount was unspecified, so a council that banked the favour moved no standing, earned no relief, and watched the next petition accrue at exactly the old rate. The most expensive answer in the game bought the smallest change in the gauge, which was backwards.

The council may bank the favour instead of taking the goodwill - that choice is theirs and should be made in the scene. It is a real trade now and not a flavour: a favour it can spend when it chooses, or quiet this week. Conceding to a hostile faction is also the only way in the simulation to be owed by someone who dislikes you.

REFUSE

Costs standing by the weight of the demand. It is still an answer, and is always cheaper than silence.

IGNORE

The heavy one. Standing falls further than a refusal and the pressure is not spent - the grievance stands at full strength and the next petition forms from where this one left off. Ignoring compounds.

ANSWER IN PART

Formerly INSPECT. Hunting the flaw is no longer an answer in itself - it is one of the grounds you may stand on while doing this.

The council grants some of what was asked, refuses the rest, and persuades the petitioner to accept that as the answer. It is a check, resolved exactly like a thread pull: d10 + fit against a difficulty, per investigations.md.

  • Costs 1 action and one tick of the clock.
  • On success: it resolves as a CONCEDE. The petitioner is as satisfied as if the whole thing had been granted - pressure to 0, and a favour owed, weight scaled to what was actually conceded, not to what was asked.
  • On failure: nothing happens. No standing moves. The clock has run, the action is spent, and the council must still CONCEDE, ANSWER IN PART again, REFUSE or IGNORE.
  • Every failed attempt on the same petition adds +1 to the difficulty of the next one, cumulative, whoever does the talking.

There is no danger in pushing back. There is only time, and a three-tick clock holds about two attempts.

Declare the ground

Before the roll, the council says what it is standing on. The ground decides whose fit is scored, which is the whole decision - a council picks the hill it is good at, or the hill that is true, and those are not always the same.

GroundWhat it isCosts
Lawthe clause cannot stand: ultra vires, unlawful, outside the charter-
Candoursay the real reason plainly, including the part that does not flatter the council-
Evidenceshow them something they did not have-
Obligationcall in a favour the petitioner owesthe favour, win or lose
Goodwilltrade on standing already earned. Only at positive standing with that factionstanding, win or lose

This table is open, like the substrate and intrusiveness tables. A ground nobody anticipated gets a row when it arrives.

The fit is then scored -2 to +3 against that ground, from the sheets, and logged in ../infra/aptitude-log.md like any other.

Keel is +3 on law and poor on candour. Partridge is the reverse. The ground is chosen before anyone knows whether it was the right one.

The order of play

This ordering is load-bearing and is not optional.

  1. The council commits its speakers and their grounds.
  2. The fits and the difficulty are then declared, in the resolution record - see investigations.md.
  3. Then the roll.

The council chooses who stands up before it is told how well they will do. Everything below depends on that: if the scores were visible first, the council would send the one right person every time and a second speaker would never be worth an action.

Two speakers

The council may send up to two. Not three - a delegation stops being a conversation.

  • Two speakers costs two actions each. Four actions, out of fifteen, on top of the tick off the clock. One speaker costs one.
  • Each may stand on a different ground. Keel on law, Garrow on candour, in the same room.
  • Take the best of the two fits, scored each against their own ground.
  • +1 if both are positive, on whichever grounds they chose.
  • The weaker speaker never drags the result down. A bad choice wastes two actions; it does not make things worse than sending nobody.

Four actions is the point. A pair is not the sensible default, it is what a council buys when it is frightened - and a council answering two petitions in the same week cannot afford a pair for both. The second speaker is insurance against having picked the wrong hill, bought before anyone knows which hill was right.

A councillor who would be a liability in that room is still a liability in that room. The fit is scored from the sheets against the ground, and the fiction is not obliged to be kind about who was standing there.

Difficulty - how much is being refused

Base, by the share of the petition's weight being refused - weight, not clause count, because one clause is often the entire petition:

RefusedDifficulty
A sliver - one small point among many3
A corner - a quarter or so5
A third to a half7
Most of it9

Above three-quarters there is no check. Refusing nearly everything and calling it a partial answer is a refusal with a covering letter, and the petitioner will say so in those words. Use REFUSE.

Then modify:

The refused part is something the council genuinely cannot grant, and this can be shown-2
The council offers something not demanded that answers the real grievance-2
The council brings evidence the petitioner did not have-1
The refused part is the reason the petition exists+2
Each previous failed attempt on this petition+1

The -2 for offering something unasked-for is the strongest modifier in the table, and it is there on purpose. A petition is a statement of a grievance, not a list of its only remedies. A council that hears what the document is actually about can answer it with something that was never written down, and that is the most valuable thing this mechanic can teach.

Worked: the Watch's petition, tick 4. Three clauses; the refused one was a quarter of the weight (5), genuinely ungrantable under the charter and demonstrably so (-2), was the reason the petition existed (+2), and the council volunteered a standing undertaking nobody had asked for that answered the real grievance (-2). Difficulty 3. Ground: law, which is Keel's.

Formal requests upward

The council may not petition. It may write a formal request to the Crown or to the League of Kingdoms.

There must be a real reason for either to intervene. Both have their own interests, neither is obliged, and a request that is refused is remembered by the body that refused it.

After resolution

OutcomePressure
Concededreset to 0
Answered in part, successfullyreset to 0 - it is a concession
Answered in part, failedunchanged. One tick of the clock is gone
Refusedreset to 20%
Ignoredunchanged

A petition that is answered in part is not re-formed, because it was answered. A petition that is refused comes back asking for less, because the faction has learned what it can actually carry.

rules/petitions.md
rules

Robbery

The third leak. leakage.md has the skim, which is a mystery, and the strike, which is a crisis. This is the one that is an adventure.

Two rolls, and they ask different questions.

1. The attemptis there an opportunity? Darkness, bad roads, thin patrols
2. The responsewill the men who find it wade in?
3. The outcomeonce they have, does it work?

Robbers read the street, not the payroll. They do not care how well the Watch is policing - they care whether there is a patrol on that corner tonight. What the Watch thinks of the council decides something else entirely: whether the two men who stumble onto it do anything about it.

1. The attempt

One roll a tick.

chance = 5%
       + (50 - quays/roads/lamps condition) x 0.5
       + 1 per 10 men the Watch is below establishment
       + 5 in deep winter

Floored at 2%. A city that is lit, paved and fully patrolled is not safe; it is merely not worth the trouble.

Nothing about wages, arrears or Watch standing appears here. A man deciding whether to take a cart is weighing the lamp and the corner, not the council's books.

What it looks like today

Base5
Roads at 70-10
Watch at establishment0
Spring0
2% (floored)

What it looks like by midwinter, if nothing is done

Roads decay. The Watch is shrinking at four a week. Winter adds five.

Base5
Roads at 40+5
Watch at 200+5
Deep winter+5
20%

The mechanic does nothing for months and then does a great deal. That is the intent. Deferred maintenance and a creditor army are not felt in the week they happen.

2. Which kind

Drawn weighted by what is actually exposed that tick. Three kinds, three different costs - the same shape as upkeep's three failures.

The cart - costs money

A collection taken between the gate and the chest. The council knows immediately and knows exactly how much.

The ward - costs standing

A shop, a warehouse, a person in an unlit street. No money leaves the treasury. It costs standing with the citizenry, or with whoever was robbed, because keeping the peace is the thing the charter is actually for and this is the council failing at it where people can see.

The road - costs trade

Banditry beyond the walls, on the upper road, where the charter gives the council no clean authority.

It feeds the hysteresis curve in economy.md. A merchant who reroutes because the road is not safe behaves exactly like one who reroutes because the toll rose: quick to leave, slow to come back, and unmoved by the news that it has been fixed.

Banditry is a toll rise the council never voted for - the same sentence upkeep.md writes about silting, and true for the same reason.

3. The response - will they engage?

Rolled only if an attempt happens. Not can they stop it - whether the men who find it go in.

chance = 30% + the Watch's standing

Capped at 70%. Two hundred and fifty men cannot be everywhere, however willing.

Watch standingInterception
-19 (today)11%
030%
+2050%
+40 or better70% (capped)

This is where the arrears are felt. The Watch's standing is already the sum of a reasoned log that includes nine weeks of unpaid wages and an audit, so nothing extra needs adding. Unpaid men still stand their post. They are simply slower to run toward a knife for a council that has not paid them.

4. The outcome - does it work?

Engaging is not stopping. Once the Watch goes in, how it ends is a check on the circumstances:

d10 + situation   against the robbers' difficulty

Difficulty on the anchors in investigations.md - 6 for an ordinary few men, higher for anyone organised or armed properly.

The situation score

Read off what is actually true of this robbery, -2 to +3, declared before the roll and logged in ../infra/aptitude-log.md like any other fit. This table is open, as the substrate and intrusiveness tables are.

Beside the garrison, the north gate or a guardhouse - help is minutes away+3
A lit ward, on a patrol route+2
Numbers already on the scene, or gate officers with swords+1
An ordinary street at an ordinary hour0
An unlit ward; two men alone-1
Beyond the walls, on the road, with nobody coming-2

And morale is part of the situation

A tired, unpaid, grumbling watchman does not fight well even once he has decided to. The company's cohesion from mass-combat.md modifies the situation score directly - no new number, because the number already exists:

Cohesion band
Steady+1
Wavering0
Breaking-1
Routed-2

So the Watch's standing lands twice, for two different reasons: on the response, because it decides whether they go in, and here, because it decides how they fight once they have. That is not double-counting - it is one cause with two effects, and a council that has let its Watch rot feels both.

What it costs

Result
Margin +3 or betterstopped, and nobody is hurt - the odds were obvious and the robbers ran
Margin 0 to +2stopped, at one man dead or too hurt to serve
Failed by 1 or 2the robbery succeeds anyway, and one man is down
Failed by 3 or moreit succeeds, and two men are down

Winning usually costs headcount, which worsens the next attempt roll. There is rarely a clean outcome, only a cheaper one - and the cheapest ones happen in the places the council paid to light.

If a councillor is present

Then it is not a roll. It is party combat - see party-combat.md - with the Watch present as the body around them, and all the usual consequences including the three-level cap and a halt on a death.

A councillor is present because they chose to escort the collection, or because they were warned. Which brings us to the thing that already exists.

Prevention

Four levers, all of which the council already has:

Fund the roads linethe largest term in the attempt roll, and the slowest to act
Keep the Watch up to strengthpay them, recruit, stop the desertion
Escort or patrola councillor's action, or men committed to it
Be likedsee below

The citizenry windfall channel is the council's early-warning system. windfalls.md already lists "a warning about a robbery that has not happened yet" among its examples, and the windfall roll keys off standing.

So a council the city likes gets told. The loop closes itself: the same goodwill that produces the warning produces the chance to act on it, and acting on it turns a dice roll into a scene.

Recurrence

A robbery is not an actor. Whoever did it is whoever was convenient - the simulation does not maintain a criminal faction with an action economy of its own, because that is a great deal of machinery for a thing that is mostly weather.

But a pattern is different. If robberies recur in the same ward, on the same stretch of road, or against the same stream, that mints a thread in investigations.md - substrate as the fiction supplies it, usually casual memory or physical trace.

The council can then pull on it, and find out whether it is a pattern or only looked like one. No new mechanic, and the thing that was weather becomes a lead.

Dials

Untested. Logged in ../infra/calibration-log.md.

  • (50 - condition) x 0.5 - the roads term, and the one the council can actually act on.
  • The 2% floor and the 5% base.
  • 30% + standing for the response, and the 70% cap.
  • The situation table, and the cohesion band that sits on top of it.
  • The margin bands for casualties, and whether "margin 3+ costs nothing" is too generous.
  • +5 for deep winter, which currently stacks with everything else going wrong at once by design.
rules/robbery.md
rules

Seasonality

Needleford is a river port at the head of navigation and a road junction. Its year is not flat: hulls depend on water, waggons depend on roads, and cargo depends on harvest.

Modelled as a single flat modifier, not as a weather system. The other mechanics supply the volatility; this one supplies the shape.

The modifier

modifier = 1.0 + 0.1 x sin( 2*pi* (tick - 1) / 52 )

A 52-week year. Tick 1 is the beginning of spring, at 1.00.

TickModifier
Spring begins11.00
Midsummer, the peak~141.10
Autumn begins~271.00
Deep winter, the trough~400.90

Rounded to two decimals per tick, so the ledger carries a number a person can follow.

Seasons are called what seasons are called. No Bryten month names are minted - the mechanic needs a season and a week number, and inventing a twelve-month calendar is a canon risk the simulation does not need to take. If the lore repo turns out to name them, those names are used.

What it applies to

StreamSeasonal?Why
Passage tollyesit is traffic, and traffic is weather
Bonded storageyesgoods stored are goods that moved
Guild and market duesnolicences and stalls, paid by people who live here in January as much as in June

Why ten per cent is enough

A swing of ±10% looks gentle beside a skim at the cap or a refused petition. It is not, because of where it crosses.

At the old rates, against a cost base of 8,000G:

SeasonModifierIncomeMargin
Spring1.008,800G+800G
Summer1.109,680G+1,680G
Autumn1.008,800G+800G
Winter0.907,920G-80G

Winter crosses zero. The council does not merely earn less - it earns less than it spends. A council in surplus and a council in deficit are two different animals however small the gap between them.

And everything else lands hardest there. Unpaid wages, a collector skimming at the cap, a petition turning into a strike, a quay giving way in the week there is no slack to repair it. Winter is when the design's other teeth meet.

What the council knows

Everything. Seasons are not a secret and never have been - the pilots, the carters and the guilds have all been doing this for generations, and any councillor who asks is told.

The trap is not ignorance. It is that a council which balances the books in July has not balanced the books.

rules/seasonality.md
rules

Rules of the Simulation

The clock

One tick = one week of world time.

The simulation runs forward on its own. The heroes decide and act in character without supervision.

What stops the clock

Three things, and only three:

1. Severity

Something large or irreversible is about to land. The halt presents:

  • the situation, and
  • the heroes' own verdict, already reached.

The DM is not choosing what the heroes do. The DM rules on what the world does to them - adjusting external pressure, vetoing on lore grounds, or waving it through. The heroes' verdict stands unless overruled.

2. Adjudication

The simulation has hit a question the rules do not answer. Pure mechanics, no character decisions. A first-class halt, so that the rules grow deliberately rather than by improvisation.

3. Quiet

Nothing big has happened for too long. The council writes a despatch to its charter-holders - an in-world artifact that doubles as the DM's review document. This guarantees a regular chance to nudge even in calm weather.

Severity score

Four dimensions, each scored 0-4, then weighted. Anchors are fixed so that scoring stays consistent from tick to tick rather than drifting with mood.

The anchors

IrreversibilityScopeCostCanon-risk
0undoable next weeka handful of peoplepettyinvents nothing
1awkward to undoa ward or a single tradereal coin, or a weight 1-3 favoura local detail
2undoable only at real costa whole bloca weight 4-7 favour, or a standing swing of 10+a Needleford institution, custom or named power
3effectively permanent - the city remembersthe whole citya weight 8-10 favour, or a standing swing of 25+asserts something about a nation, order or faction in the lore repo
4cannot be undone by anyonebeyond the city - Crown, League, another nationsomething that cannot be re-earnedcontradicts, or appears to contradict, laurasia-worldbuilding

The weights

DimensionWeightMax
Canon-riskx416
Irreversibilityx312
Scopex14
Costx14
36

Canon-risk is heaviest because it is the only dimension measuring a risk to something the DM cannot repair afterwards. Irreversibility is next because a permanent act is the one the heroes cannot take back either.

Scope and cost are deliberately light. Spending the council's money, standing and favours is the heroes' own business. The DM is not asked to approve it.

The threshold

Halt at 14 or above.

Trip-wires

Regardless of the total:

  • Canon-risk of 3 or 4 always halts. This halt exists so the veto arrives before something is minted about the world, not after.
  • Irreversibility of 4 always halts. A thing nobody can undo stops the clock even when it is quiet, cheap and touches one person. A secret killing is the archetype.

Calibration

The threshold and the weights are tuned from DM reactions. Every halt is recorded in ../infra/calibration-log.md with its score and whether it deserved to stop.

Halt budget

Checkpoint period: 4 ticks - one month of world time.

Budget: 2 severity halts per period. If more qualify at once they arrive as one docket, not several interruptions. If more qualify than the cap allows, the lesser ones resolve on the heroes' own recommendation and are reported after the fact.

The trip-wires are exempt from the budget. A canon-risk 3+ or an irreversibility 4 always reaches the DM, however busy the month has been. Otherwise a crowded period could silently suppress precisely the veto the trip-wire exists to deliver.

Quiet halt: 4 ticks without any halt and the council writes a despatch.

In a bad month that is roughly one contact a fortnight. In a calm one, one a month.

The DM

The man behind the curtain. The DM:

  • controls external factors - what crises arrive, and when
  • tunes mechanics and answers adjudications
  • flags where the simulation diverges from the intended vision
  • may assume the persona of the Crown's agent when it suits, as a voice to pick up and put down

The DM does not order the heroes about. They are characters, not units.

What every halt carries

Besides the situation and the heroes' verdict, a halt carries:

Mechanic opportunities. Whenever play reaches something that wants a rule and does not have one - a raid on a hideout, mass combat, a riot with demands, a trial, a siege, a negotiation with real stakes - it is raised at the next halt for the DM to design with. The simulation does not quietly improvise a subsystem and present it as settled. Flagging is the default; silence is the exception.

Scenes

Every important decision gets a scene. Not a summary of a decision - a scene, with people in a room, in their own voices. Short is fine; two hundred words of Keel being correct at somebody is worth more than a paragraph reporting that he was.

Scenes are written for:

  • any decision the council actually has to make
  • anything interesting happening to a named character
  • every halt, so the DM rules on a moment rather than on a bullet point

Everything else - the ordinary running of the city - can be narrated.

Scenes are written to ../transcripts/.

Artifacts

The simulation produces things, not only prose. Maps, letters, manifests, charters, bills of lading, watch rosters, guild petitions, despatches, warrants, handbills nailed to a door.

If an object exists in the fiction and a person could hold it, it should probably exist on disk. These go in their own directories under simulation/ and are named as the world would name them.

The viewer

../infra/viewer/build.py renders everything human-readable under simulation/ into one self-contained page. No server, no dependencies, no build step - it opens straight off the filesystem.

It never reads infra/. The seals are excluded by construction, not by care.

Rebuild it at the end of every tick. The DM has the URL and does not need it repeated.

Canon

  1. ../../laurasia-worldbuilding/ - final say, always.
  2. ../canon/ - binding once written.
  3. In-the-moment invention - must contradict neither.

Anything deviating from (1) needs an in-world narrative reason and explicit approval.

Persistence

Every human-readable thing the simulation produces is written to the file system. Nothing of consequence lives only in conversation.

The seal, and collisions with it

The solution to the mission's mystery is fixed and written down in ../infra/sealed/the-culprit.md. The DM asked to be kept in the dark, on purpose. Nothing in ../infra/sealed/ is ever quoted, summarised, paraphrased or hinted at outside that folder. The heroes learn only what they earn through play. The DM may order the seal broken at any time - only then.

The DM also rules on whether a faction's behaviour makes sense for that faction. Those rulings can contradict the sealed solution, so:

When a DM ruling collides with the seal, say that it does - never what it collides with - and grade it:

  • Soft collision. Nothing the heroes have seen yet depends on the contradicted part. Re-seal quietly, adjust, say it was soft. The mystery stays fair and the DM need do nothing.
  • Hard collision. Clues already delivered would become lies, and the mystery becomes unsolvable or unfair. Say so explicitly and stop. This is the one case where a veto carries a cost the DM cannot see.

On a hard collision the DM has three doors: override (the solution is rebuilt around the ruling), unseal (decide knowingly), or defer (an in-world reason is found for the odd behaviour, which is the standing rule for deviation anyway).

Flagging a collision leaks a sliver - the DM learns that something they vetoed mattered. That is accepted. Silently breaking the mystery is worse.

rules/simulation.md
rules

Tenure - the Ash Clock

The threat is tenure, not theft. The canon is in ../canon/the-mission.md: disassembled the device is utterly inert, and assembled it bleeds ash whether anybody wants it to or not. Nobody has to detonate anything for this to become a catastrophe. Somebody only has to hold it together and wait.

This is the only mechanic in the simulation that makes doing nothing expensive.

The phases

The adversary cannot skip a phase and cannot run two at once. Each step is work, and work costs actions.

PhaseWhat it takesWhere it leaks
1. Recoveryone action per attempt; the vault is trapped and attempts can fail badlycorpses, hired replacements, cargo moving out of nowhere
2. Researchseveral actions; cannot be done in-housethey must ask someone who knows - a scholar, a text, a dwarf
3. Assemblyone action, once, and only with every piece in one placethe largest single movement of the operation
4. Generationfree, and automatic, foreverthe ash itself, which is heavy and has to go somewhere

Research cannot be done in-house. This is structural, not flavour. Nobody in a criminal crew knows how an unaccountable object fits together, so they have to reach outside themselves, and reaching outside is what makes threads. It is the phase where a careful council is most likely to catch them.

Recovery is dangerous

Whatever the pieces are kept behind was built to keep them. Each recovery attempt may fail, and a bad failure costs a body or a week or both. This slows the adversary honestly rather than by fiat, and a body is a lead.

Generation

Once assembled, the device produces one crate of ash per tick, forever, costing the adversary nothing. Switching it on is the last decision they ever have to make.

Ten crates is the line. That is enough ash to do catastrophic, permanent harm to ground that does not come back - whether it is shipped out, buried under a warehouse, or stacked where it stands. At ten crates the situation has passed from a recovery into a disaster, and the simulation changes mode rather than ending.

The crates leak

Generation is free. Keeping the ash is not. A crate is a physical object, and canon has the ash moving as cargo - through roads, quays, bonded storage and manifests, the same as anything else in this city.

So the adversary chooses, and both choices cost:

  • Move them as they come. One action per movement. Ten small traces: a carter, a bonded entry, a warehouseman who remembers an odd weight.
  • Let them stack. Free, but a growing pile in one place is conspicuous, and whatever is guarding it is guarding something.

Either way the council's invisible clock has ten visible shadows. The council loses this by missing ten chances, not by failing a count they were never shown.

Where the ash goes - a warehouse, a cellar, a mansion, a hull on the tide - is the adversary's decision, not the simulation's.

The adversary's actions

Every actor worth simulating has one action per tick, the adversary included. Covering tracks, recovering a piece, researching, moving ash, buying a favour - all the same single action. See investigations.md for covering and the burn.

This is the real constraint. A week spent hiding is a week the vault is not emptied; a week spent moving ash is a week nothing is learned. The adversary is never doing everything at once, and what they are not doing is where the council gets in.

Headcount

Headcount is the adversary's health. A crew whittled down works slower: below a threshold they drop to one action every other tick.

Reinforcements take three or four ticks to arrive and leave their own trail - people travel, people are paid, people are housed, and somebody notices strangers.

This gives the council a way to slow the clock without having solved anything, which is the mercy the rest of this design does not offer.

What the council can see

Nothing. There is no gauge, no counter, no warning. The ash clock is sealed and stays sealed.

What the council sees is a city where odd cargo moves, strangers arrive, a scholar is asked an unusual question, and a road nobody has used in years carries wheel-ruts. The clock is only ever legible through its shadows.

Panic and the endgame

The adversary is not a schedule. If the council gets close - heat in the city, lanterns in the dungeon - they may run, they may dig in and fight, they may move the whole operation somewhere worse. Where they assemble, whether they ship, and whether they stand are their decisions, taken in the moment from what they believe about the council's attention.

That belief is tracked in ../infra/sealed/the-operation.md, and it can be wrong.

rules/tenure.md
rules

Upkeep - Deferred Maintenance as Debt

Three lines in the cost base can be cut without anyone noticing inside the week. All three compound. Cutting them is cheap now and expensive later, by design - see economy.md.

This mechanic makes "later" a number.

Condition

Each upkeep line carries a condition, 0-100.

BandCondition
Sound75-100
Worn50-74
Failing25-49
Derelict0-24

What the council can see

Not the same for each line. This is the heart of the mechanic.

LineObvious without lookingOtherwise
Dredgingat 40 or belowgaugers and pilots know first; the council does not
Quays, roads, lampsat 20 or belowa rotten quay looks like a quay
Relief, fire, contingencyneverthere is nothing to see until the day there is

Above those thresholds the condition must be inspected - one action, a surveyor, a pilot, a walk along the lower quays - or learned by accident in play.

An inspection returns the true number. Not a band, not an impression - the exact condition, which the council may then reason with, budget against and argue over. There is no roll and no chance of a bad read: an action spent looking is an action that tells you what is there.

It tells them nothing about when. The event roll stays hidden, so a council that inspects knows precisely how bad it is and still cannot say whether it has a month or a week.

Relief and fire are never obviously in disrepair. That is what makes the smallest line the most dangerous one: it is the only place where cutting leaves no visible mark at all, right up until a fire is not contained.

How it moves

Full funding holds a line steady. It does not improve it. The cost base pays for keeping up, not catching up.

  • Underfunding drops condition by (share of the line not paid) x d[15-20] per tick - random, so the council can never budget its neglect precisely. Skipping dredging entirely is −15 to −20 a tick; paying half is −7 to −10.
  • Overfunding raises it, at the catch-up rate below.

The debt

Every gold not spent becomes arrears - work owed to the city. Arrears are not repaid at face value.

Catch-up multiplier: 2 + (consecutive ticks neglected / 4)

A week of skipped dredging saves 700G and costs 1,400G to put right. Skip it for two months and the same week costs 2,800G. Neglect is a loan at a punitive rate, taken out against a lender who does not negotiate.

Arrears are tracked in ../ledger/upkeep.md, and are not the treasury's Owed - nobody is waiting to be paid. The city is simply owed work.

What failure looks like

Dredging - 700G

The head of navigation silts. Usable draught falls and the largest hulls stop coming up.

At Failing, the passage toll freezes. It stops answering the rate. The council can raise the toll all it likes and the revenue does not move, because the hulls that would have paid the difference cannot get up the river. The city's chief revenue and chief political instrument both go dead at once, and the only thing that unfreezes them is a dredger.

Worse, the hysteresis in economy.md applies in full. Trade that reroutes because the channel shoaled does not come back when it is dredged, any more than it comes back when a toll is lowered.

Silting is a toll rise the council never voted for, and cannot repeal.

Below 25: the channel begins to change rather than merely fill. Some lost draught is permanent and no dredging restores it.

Quays, bridge, roads, lamps - 900G

Dark streets, bad roads, a bridge that closes to waggons.

This line feeds the chance of robbery - see robbery.md, where it is the largest term in the attempt roll and the one the council can actually act on. Money moving through a city with unlit wards and broken roads is money moving slowly in the dark.

Below 50 it adds to the robbery chance at half a point per point lost; above 50 it subtracts. At 70 the city is effectively safe. At 25 it is not.

Below 25: something falls down that is not rebuilt in this campaign.

Relief, fire, contingency - 400G

The first thing anyone proposes cutting, the smallest line on the page, and the only one with no warning signs at all.

Failure is a fire that is not contained, or a winter the river wards do not eat through. It costs standing with the citizenry and the river wards - the two gauges the council currently holds in credit, and therefore the two it has furthest to fall from.

And a fire burns things. A failure on this line can take condition off the quays-and-roads line on top of the standing loss. Neglecting the cheapest line is how a council damages the expensive one without ever deciding to.

Below 25: the city stops expecting anything from the council - a standing floor that money cannot buy back.

The hidden part

Visibility is per-line, above. The event roll is never visible. The council may know the quays are failing; they never know which week a quay gives way. That is enough - the dread does the work.

Starting condition, Year 0

The previous administration spent the averaged figure and no more, for years, on a city that had been growing.

LineConditionObvious?
Dredging60no - not until 40worn. The head of navigation is shoaling and the pilots know it
Quays, bridge, roads, lamps70no - not until 20worn. The bridge is sound; the lower quays are not
Relief, fire, contingency80neversound, barely. Nothing has been tested yet

Nobody has told the council any of this, and nothing yet looks wrong enough to ask about. Finding out costs an action and the thought of spending it.

rules/upkeep.md
rules

Windfalls

Every other mechanic in this simulation is a way to lose. Traffic falls, standing decays, threads go cold, collectors skim, maintenance rots, ash accrues, winter crosses zero.

Windfalls are the only way to win a week. One-off bursts of standing, favour or gold, arriving outside the usual channels, and gone if not taken in the tick they appear.

Two sources

Luck, which is not luck

Standing-based. People bring their problems to a council they trust. A lady at the chamber door, a letter, a warning about a robbery that has not happened yet.

This is the only place in the design where positive standing does something. Everything else keys off the negative - skim chance, petitions, withholding. A council at +18 with the citizenry has, until now, been paid nothing for it.

One roll a tick, against the pool. Not one roll per faction - a single roll for whether, then a weighted draw for who.

1. The pool. Sum the standing of every faction in credit. Remote and institutional factions - the Crown, the League, the sea factors - are quartered into the sum. They are not people who walk up to a councillor in the street; what they offer is a commission, and it is rarer.

2. The roll. chance = pool x 1.0%, capped at 60%.

3. The source, if it hits: drawn at random, weighted by each faction's contribution to the pool. A faction at +5 is still the source one time in twenty. Someone the council barely knows occasionally knocks, which is truer than the best-liked faction winning every week.

The source is drawn, not chosen. What is offered follows from who is offering.

These always arrive at the start of the tick, with the full action budget still in hand. The council is never denied one by the clock.

Skill

Earned. The investigation mechanic in investigations.md grades margin and pays an intel bonus at margin 3+. At margin 5 or above the council may take that bonus material instead: they did not only find the lead, they found what was under it.

It replaces the intel bonus, never adds to it, and it is available only where the fiction already supplies something material. Both conditions are in investigations.md and both are load-bearing.

For a competent, well-matched pair at the default difficulty this is three pulls in ten - raised from one in five by the DM after tick 5, when the tier had gone five ticks without firing. Still the same order as the standing roll, so neither channel becomes the one the council lives on.

Negotiations, inspections and audits can do the same - the other party, well handled, mentions a thing they need done.

No separate roll. Frequency is governed by how often the council does things well, which is the correct governor.

And by what they are willing to give up. The choice at margin 5 is between a lead and a payday, on a thread that is 10 weaker either way. Every earned windfall in this simulation is a week the council chose money over the mission.

These arrive when they arrive - mid-tick, attached to the action that earned them. If a councillor's last action generates one, it is lost unless somebody else still has an action to spend.

That is deliberate. A council that spends every councillor down in parallel can never catch a windfall, so keeping somebody in reserve becomes a real strategy - and it costs them tempo on the mission to do it.

Taking one

A windfall costs an action to claim. This is the difference between a windfall and income: income is passive and arrives whether the council acts or not. A windfall is an errand, and an errand is a thing somebody does.

Scale

Roughly half a week of the Watch:

KindSize
Gold~1,250G
Favourweight 3-5
Standing+3 to +6

Enough to matter against an 8,000G week. Not enough to be a plan.

Not all of them are free

A windfall with no risk is not a decision, it is a button. If every errand is pure upside the council takes all of them reflexively, and the mechanic is only slower income after all.

Some are straightforwardly good. Others are errands with teeth - stopping a robbery, a difficult negotiation, a confiscation whose owner wants it back. These are resolved exactly like a thread pull: the demand is named, the councillors in play are scored against it from their sheets, and the result follows the margin. See investigations.md.

Failure costs what the fiction says it costs - injury, standing from a botched negotiation, a favour spent to get out of it.

Resolving a windfall never triggers another windfall. Whatever the margin.

Consequences outlast the windfall

No windfall itself lasts past the tick. Its consequences do.

A robbery not prevented is a shop robbed, and a shopkeeper who remembers. A stash of weapons confiscated is somebody looking for their weapons. A negotiation gone well is a person who now expects the council to be reasonable next time, and a negotiation gone badly is the reverse.

Every windfall taken or refused writes its consequence into the world, the same as anything else.

Authoring

The simulation does not invent windfalls outside these rules. Not for now.

Adding one because a scene seemed to earn it is right for story and is also the single place in this design where the simulation could quietly make the game easier every time things felt bad. The DM will mark the points where a windfall should have appeared, and the rules are calibrated from that.

Every windfall is logged with its source - rolled, earned, or authored - in ../ledger/windfalls.md, so the rate is visible and can be argued with.

The rate, and why the cap is there

At the Year 0 standings the pool is 26.75 - the citizenry at 18, the Crown quartered to 7.5, the League quartered to 1.25 - giving 27%, or a windfall roughly every fourth week.

The cap is the promise. A pooled roll grows linearly with how liked the council is, and without a ceiling a council that wins over four more blocs would be fielding errands most weeks. Capped at 60%, fortune knocks more often as they earn it and never becomes the thing they live on.

Dials, in the order they should be turned: the 1.0% per point, then the 60% cap, then the quartering of remote factions. Recorded in ../infra/calibration-log.md.

The join. The man with the stiff thumb gave the name Thurlow to a barber-surgeon in the autumn before last. In the same autumn an account called Thurlow and Company, carriers opened on the Hithe porterage list and began hiring river men by the week. Hallet was one of them.

"We have been looking for a business. There isn't one."

And Brackwater's pattern, reported in her own order: the auctioneer was bought, the messenger was taken out, and the man who hired the boat is gone from the stair. "Every person I have put a question near has stopped being in this city within the fortnight. They cannot answer a question. They can only remove the person it was asked about."

Scene: ../transcripts/tick-005-the-four-places.md.

Also ruled at this halt: a records query may name a past week. Written into ../rules/investigations.md. The 376G is still sitting in the tick-2 entries and Keel searched the wrong seven days.

The situation

Two hundred and thirteen of two hundred and fifty watchmen have petitioned the council. They are owed eight weeks - 20,000G against a chest of 6,257G - and in week three their books were searched for a 376G shortfall that was never explained and never found.

The petition asks three things: a published schedule, a first payment inside the week, and that no officer of the council inspect the Watch's books while the arrears stand, any inspection to go instead to a Crown officer reporting to both sides.

The third clause is the one that matters, and it is a clause the council may not lawfully grant - the charter vests inspection of revenue in this council and nowhere else.

The full document is at ../artifacts/petition-of-the-watch-year-0.md.

The heroes' verdict, already reached - five to nothing

ClauseAnswer
1. A published schedule, dates against each of eight weeks, at the four placesCONCEDE
2. First payment within the week - 2,500GCONCEDE
3. No council inspection while the arrears standREFUSE, in the posted document, with the reason in full

And, not asked for: the council binds itself to publish the result of any inspection of any collector of the city's revenue, within seven days, found or not found, at the same four places - beginning with the week-three inspection of the Watch, which found nothing.

Keel wrote the fourth part and said in chamber that he did not think it was wise.

Not retried under the new mechanic

ANSWER IN PART was written after this verdict was reached. The DM has ruled that it does not apply retrospectively - this answer is delivered as the council wrote it, and is resolved by the DM's ruling below rather than by a check.

The mechanic governs every petition from here, and the harbour guard's is already at 77%.

The severity score

DimensionRawWeightWeightedWhy
Canon-risk0x40invents nothing; a city council and its own watch
Irreversibility3x39a standing public undertaking cannot be withdrawn without being a scandal. The city will hold them to it
Scope3x13the whole city, and every collector in it - not only the Watch
Cost2x122,500G now, 20,000G on a dated schedule, and the end of quiet audits
14at the threshold

No trip-wire. Canon-risk 0, irreversibility 3.

What the DM is being asked to rule on

Not what the heroes do. They have decided. The DM rules on what the world does to them, and holds the lore veto.

  1. Does the Watch take it? Clause 3 refused, with a transparency undertaking offered in its place that nobody asked for. Roper said out loud that saying it publicly "will do us nearly as well". The simulation's reading is that they take it and the pressure resets to 0 on clauses 1 and 2 and to 20% on clause 3 - a refusal, not a concession.
  2. Does anyone else read it? The harbour guard, the warehousemen and the guilds have all just been told they will be audited in public from now on. The harbour guard is at 49% pressure and climbing.
  3. The Crown. The council has published a limit on its own chartered authority without asking the body that granted it. That is not a breach. It may still be a letter.

Mechanic opportunities raised by this halt

1. Raids and physical action - BUILT, and no longer blocking. Split three ways at the DM's instruction and written during the halt: party combat, mass combat and robbery, with the chase parked until a chase happened.

(It happened in tick 5, and ../rules/chase.md was built after it.) See ../rules/.

2. Answering a petition in part. - SETTLED. Built as a persuasion check on the thread-pull machinery, replacing INSPECT. See ../rules/petitions.md. Ruled not to apply retrospectively, then applied here anyway as the DM's token check, which it comfortably carried.

3. Auditing a named past week. - RULED. Yes, absolutely. A records query may name a week that has gone. Written into ../rules/investigations.md. It matters: the 376G is still in the tick-2 entries.

If the DM waves it through

Tick 5 proceeds: the schedule is posted, 2,500G is paid, the week-three inspection result goes up at the four places, and the council goes back to a board of six threads of which two are dead.

Marget Stroud answered with a petition; Keel broke one clause of it and took the other eight. Thackeray traced the jewellery to a river crewman named Hallet, who sold nine pieces for well under their worth and vanished within the month.

Partridge got the bench and the hiring was on an account - Thurlow and Company, carriers, which does not exist. Thackeray, who is nobody in that ward, rolled a natural 10 and learned Hallet was promised work through the winter. And the guilds remitted 108G short in the week they petitioned, and the council decided not to look.

The Treasury

Tick 7, Year 0. All figures in gold (G). A watchman is 10G a week; a week of the Watch is 2,500G.

The viewer reads this file for the overview, so the three headings below are fixed - keep them exactly as they are.

Balance

Opening5,695G
Closing5,489G

The third falling week, and the smallest fall of the three - because the council caught one of the two men taking from it. Two and a fifth weeks of the Watch.

Coming in

Seasonal modifier 1.07 on the toll and on storage; dues are not seasonal.

LineAmount
Passage toll6,634G (of which 315G was taken on the quay share and recovered)
Bonded storage2,568G
Guild and market dues1,092G (1,200G assessed - 108G short, not found)
Total10,294G

Two collectors short in one week. Five rolls, two hits.

  • The harbour guard, 315G, on the quay share of the passage toll, across nine entries in one clerk's hand. Audited, found, recovered, prosecuted and posted. The clerk is Pellow, who has kept Dray's quay book for eleven years.
  • The guilds, 108G, on the dues - the same figure as tick 6, a second week running. Audited, and the audit failed by one. Nothing was found and the council is obliged by its own undertaking to post that it looked.

The council's tick-6 decision was to wait three weeks and see whether the guilds' shortfall came again. It came again in one, and the audit that followed found nothing and cost six points of standing with the bloc that is mid-petition.

Going out

The Watch2,800G
The Watch - arrears, payment 3 of 82,500G
Customs establishment1,100G
Chamber, courts and gaol600G
Quays, bridge, roads, lamps900G
Dredging700G
Crown remittance1,500G
Poor relief, fire, contingency400G
Total10,500G

Seven weeks, every cuttable line funded in full. No arrears of work. See ../ledger/upkeep.md.

Owed

DebtAmount
The Watch - 5 weeks of wages in arrears12,500G
Total12,500G

Due in tick 8: 2,500G, payment 4 of 8, dated and posted. Three kept out of three.

Notes

The arithmetic of the schedule, at this season.

Margin, ordinary weekWith the 2,500G payment
Now, modifier 1.07+2,402G-98G
Midsummer, 1.10+2,675G+175G
Deep winter, 0.90+890G-1,610G

Five payments remain and the chest is 5,489G. The margin is at its best for the year over the next seven weeks and is still not enough; it crosses into profit for about four weeks around midsummer and then falls for twenty-six.

At the present rate the chest is empty in the twentieth week, with two payments still on the schedule. This has not moved since Keel laid the paper in tick 6, because the 315G recovered and the 108G lost very nearly cancelled.

The irony is recorded because the council noticed it. The week it caught a thief, the chest fell less than either of the two weeks before it - and the 108G it chose in tick 6 not to chase is the one that got away.

The 376G has still not been found, and nobody has looked since tick 5.

All three cuttable lines are known by number. Quays and roads at 70 (tick 2), dredging at 60 (tick 5), relief, fire and contingency at 80, sound (tick 6).

state/treasury.md

Tick 1 - the week in summary

Year 0. The council's first week. No halt.

What happened

The five took their seat in a borrowed room above the bonded sheds and did the only thing the charter unambiguously lets them do: they set the rates.

They held the passage toll and said publicly that they had held it. They corrected the bonded-storage fee upward, which had not been touched in thirty years. They reduced guild and market dues, which fall on people who live here.

Four days later Marget Stroud summoned them to her own weighing yard with a petition signed by nine of eleven warehousemen. Keel found the one clause in it that was wrong, said so, and then took the other eight - including an objection about winter storage that the council had genuinely not considered. Garrow and Stroud did not mention the thing between them, deliberately, in front of witnesses who could not hear it.

Thackeray spent her week on the errand. The jewellery is confirmed: dwarven vault-work, nine pieces, sold as a lot about eleven months ago by a river crewman called Hallet who did not know what he had, to a Galician factor who probably did. Hallet has not been seen since within a month of the sale. He had been telling people he was coming into more.

She drafted a letter to the League and burned it, twice.

What moved

GaugeWasNowWhy
Crown+30+30-
League+5+5-
Citizenry+12+18dues cut; the toll held, publicly
Nobles and guilds-10-14the storage fee corrected; the petition heard

No favours spent. No hooks taken. No halt - the week's largest act, the Bill of Rates, scored 11 against a threshold of 14.

Where it leaves them

The council has bought the street and charged the guildhall for it in the same week, which is a coherent position and an exposed one. The bloc that pays the harbour guard is now at -14 and has learned that the council's quartermaster reads petitions properly.

Eleven months is a long head start.

This tick produced

  • The First Sitting - the three rates set
  • The Weighing Yard - Stroud's petition
  • What the Auctioneer Remembered - the jewellery traced
  • Bill of Rates, Year 0 - posted at four places
  • Current state - the clock, the rates, the treasury
transcripts/tick-001-summary.md
transcripts

The First Sitting

Tick 1, Year 0. The chamber above the customs house, because the council has nowhere else yet.

The room smelled of tar. It was directly over the bonded sheds, and nobody had thought to ask whether that mattered before the charter was read out in it.

Ambrose Keel had the books open before the others had sat down.

"Three rates," he said. "Passage, warehousing, market dues. The charter lets us set all three. The previous arrangement set them in the year the quay was rebuilt and nobody has touched them since, which means the city has been governed for thirty years by a dead man's guess."

"Start with what we need," said Alder Garrow.

"Two hundred and fifty watchmen, paid badly and late. Wages are nine weeks in arrears." Keel did not look up. "That is the whole of the problem. Everything else is preference."

Ines Thackeray said, "Then raise the toll."

"No." Garrow and Keel said it together, which neither of them enjoyed.

Keel recovered first. "The toll is why anyone comes here. Raise it and the hulls stop at Oldhithe and lighter up on the tide, and the carts go round by Thrushford and ford it badly, and then we have the same wages owing against a smaller city. Both of those are worse than us. Neither of them is worse than us by very much."

"Then warehousing," said Gil Partridge.

A silence of the particular kind that happens when the obvious thing is also the expensive thing.

"The yards belong to the guilds," Garrow said. "And the guilds pay the harbour guard. A hundred and eighty men we do not command, employed by the people we would be charging."

"They are also," Keel said, "paying a fee set in a year when the bonded sheds held half what they hold now, on goods worth four times what they were worth then. The rate is wrong. It has been wrong for a generation, and every warehouseman in this city knows it is wrong, and that is precisely why none of them has ever raised it."

"You are going to say that to them," said Thackeray.

"I am going to say it to them correctly."

Partridge put his hand flat on the table. "Then take the market dues down."

Keel finally looked up.

"The dues are paid by people who live here," Partridge went on. "Stall rents, licences, the porters' levy. Eleven shillings a quarter off a woman selling fish. If we are raising a rate on men who own warehouses, we can lower one on women who own baskets. Otherwise we are simply a new set of hands in the same pockets."

"That costs us," Keel said. "Materially. The dues are not nothing."

"It costs us less than the warehouse rise gains," said Partridge. "You told me so yourself, on the stairs."

"I told you so on the stairs because I assumed you would not use it against me in the chamber."

"Then you have met me," Partridge said.

Isolde Brackwater had not spoken. She had been looking at the shutters, which faced the river, and which had been closed since before the five arrived.

"Do it," she said.

They all turned.

"The guilds dislike us already," she said. "They said so in their own hall, before any of us set foot in the city. We are not going to purchase their affection by being timid - we will only purchase their contempt more cheaply. And the street has not decided about us yet." She looked at Partridge. "It is disposed to decide well. That is a thing we have for a short while and will not have again."

"That is an argument for haste," said Garrow.

"It is an argument for spending a position while it is worth something." She went back to the shutters. "Raise the warehouse fee. Lower the dues. Leave passage exactly where it is, and say publicly that we left it, so that the factors understand we could have moved it and chose not to."

Keel wrote for a while.

"For the record," he said, "I want it minuted that the warehouse rate is being corrected, not raised. The distinction will matter when somebody stands up in the guildhall and calls it a raid."

"Will it matter to them?" Thackeray asked.

"No," said Keel. "It will matter to the record."

Resolved: passage toll held. Bonded-warehouse fee raised. Guild and market dues lowered. Posted as the Bill of Rates, Year 0 - see ../artifacts/bill-of-rates-year-0.md.

transcripts/tick-001-the-first-sitting.md
transcripts

The Weighing Yard

Tick 1, Year 0. Four days after the Bill of Rates was posted.

Marget Stroud did not come to the customs house. She waited in her own weighing yard and let it be known that she was waiting, which amounted to the same thing and cost her nothing.

Garrow and Keel went. Partridge offered; Garrow said no, in a tone that ended it.

The yard was working. Two hulls' worth of bonded wool was being weighed out under a roof that had cost somebody a great deal, and eleven of the harbour guard were standing about in it with nothing in particular to do, which was itself the message.

"Sergeant," Stroud said.

"Not for twenty years," said Garrow.

"No. Councillor." She did not smile, but something in her face moved. "I have a petition. It is signed by nine of the eleven warehousemen, and I will tell you honestly that I wrote it, and that the two who did not sign were not asked."

Keel took it. He read it through once, quickly, and then again, slowly, with his lips moving at one particular clause.

"Your third point is wrong," he said.

"Which third point."

"That the fee was set by charter and cannot be altered by the council." He handed it back, open, with one finger on the line. "It was set by a rate-book of the Michaelmas court, which is an instrument of the city, not of the Crown, and the charter grants us the power to levy and amend. If it had been set by charter you would be correct and we would be stopped. It was not, and you are not, and I think you know it, because the other eight points are good and this one is the only one you wrote carelessly."

Stroud looked at him for a moment.

"Most men would have let me keep that," she said.

"Most men would have had to come back and take it off you later, in front of more people."

"Yes." She folded the petition. "The other eight stand?"

"The other eight stand. Three of them are better than anything we said in chamber. The warning about winter storage in particular - we had not thought about what the rise does when the sheds are full and the roads are shut, and you are right that it falls hardest in the eight weeks when nobody can move goods out. I will bring it back to council and I expect we will adjust it."

"You expect."

"I expect. I do not promise, because I am one of five."

Stroud turned to Garrow, who had said nothing at all.

"And you?"

"I think the fee is correct," said Garrow. "I think it will cost you, and I think you can pay it, and I think you will remember that we did it in our first week." He paused. "I also think your father's carts would not have reached Needleford at all in the year I am remembering, and that neither of us has mentioned it, and that I would rather we went on not mentioning it."

The yard went on weighing wool.

"I had not intended to mention it," Stroud said. "It is not a thing to be spent on a warehouse fee."

"No," Garrow agreed. "It is not."

She saw them to the gate herself, which eleven members of the harbour guard observed, and which would be in the guildhall by evening.

Resolved: the petition's eight sound points taken back to council; the winter storage clause to be revisited. No favour spent. Both parties now know the other knows.

transcripts/tick-001-the-weighing-yard.md
transcripts

What the Auctioneer Remembered

Tick 1, Year 0. A back room off the Hithe, by arrangement.

Ines Thackeray had been in the city nine days and had already found the man who ran the quiet auctions, which says more about the city than about her.

He was perfectly willing to talk. That was the first thing that bothered her.

"Dwarven," he said. "Vault-work. Not grave goods - too clean, no corrosion, and the settings were cut for stones that were still in them, which you almost never see. Somebody had kept it dry for a very long time."

"How many pieces?"

"Nine. Sold as a lot, to a Galician factor, for rather less than it was worth, because the seller was in a hurry and did not know what he had."

"You are certain he did not know."

"Magelight." The auctioneer spread his hands. "He asked me what dwarven meant."

Thackeray wrote that down.

"And the seller?"

"Hallet. A crewman, off the river boats." He paused, and for the first time became careful. "He has not been seen since the spring."

"Since the sale?"

"Within the month of it. He was paid, he drank a great deal of it, he told several people he was coming into more, and then he was not there any longer. It happens on the river. Men fall in."

"Do they usually tell several people they are coming into more, first?"

The auctioneer did not answer that.

Thackeray walked back along the Hithe in the dark, past the bonded sheds, and thought about the phrase coming into more. A man who finds a single box of jewellery does not come into more. A man who has seen where a great deal of it is lying, and intends to go back, does.

Eleven months. Whoever had been there was eleven months ahead of her, and had been tidy about it.

She did not write to Commissioner Varden. She drafted the letter twice and burned both. An antiquities commission that was told dwarven vault-work had surfaced near Needleford would be obliged to say so to the League, and the whole arrangement the Crown had made with the five depended on nobody being obliged to say anything to anyone.

It was the first time she understood the deniability as a cage rather than a courtesy.

Resolved: the jewellery confirmed as dwarven vault-work, sold roughly eleven months ago by a river crewman named Hallet who has not been seen since. Thackeray declined to write to the League. No favour spent.

transcripts/tick-001-what-the-auctioneer-remembered.md

Tick 2 - the week in summary

Year 0, week 2. Spring. Seasonal modifier 1.01. No halt.

What happened

The week opened with a woman on the stair. Nan Shilling, steward of the river wards' burial club, had an offer to buy out the almshouse lease and a date on it. Keel read it and found the clause that sold a reversion the club did not own. He explained this to sixty rivermen in a cold room and did it badly; Partridge finished the job in two sentences. The club did not sign. The factor house whose instrument it was heard about it by noon.

Keel and Thackeray pulled the Galician factor's mark out of the customs house - four hundred and sixty-one bonded entries, under council authority, by name. They got the mark. Keel got something better: the entries fall at eleven-week intervals that have nothing to do with trade. Somebody is paying on a calendar that is not a merchant's.

Partridge and Thackeray walked the river wards and found Hallet's life: the Blackcap off Fetter Lane, two people he told he was coming into more, and the fact that he was not a thief - he was a boatman hired for lifting work, and he was away from the city for days at a time.

Garrow called both reliefs of the Watch into the muster yard before dawn and told two hundred and fifty unpaid men the exact size of the chest. Keel objected on the record. The council paid one week - 2,500G - against nine.

Brackwater put a watch on the auctioneer's room, and walked the lime wharf, and found nothing there at all.

What moved
