League+5+5-
Citizenry+18+22the almshouse kept
Nobles and guilds-14-12the winter-storage amendment delivered as undertaken
Warehousemen (total)-10-4the amendment is theirs
The Watch-20-13Garrow stood in front of them; one week paid
Sea factors0-3their instrument taken apart in public

The money

Expected under the new rate-book9,886G
Actually received9,510G
Short by376G

The shortfall is on the passage toll and Keel cannot account for it. He has checked the arithmetic twice. The arithmetic is correct.

Surplus 1,510G, less 2,500G paid against the Watch's arrears. The chest closed at 2,210G - a thousand lighter than it opened, in the first week the new rates were collected.

What is forming

Six factions are below zero and petitions assemble on their own. The Watch is at 37% of the 80% it needs, and gains about thirty-seven a week. On current figures it delivers a petition in tick 3.

Nobody has told the council this. The pressure is in ../ledger/factions.md for anyone who reads it as the factions do.

Where it leaves them

A good week that cost money. Two threads paid out at a margin, the board went from three leads to five, the council's worst bloc moved toward it and its own Watch moved further still - and the chest is thinner than it was, and there is a hole in the toll that nobody can find.

Four hundred and sixty-one entries were pulled by name in a building where thirty people work.

  • The Bonded Entries - the factor's mark, and the calendar
  • The Pay Parade - nine weeks, one week paid
  • Threads - five now, two of them fading
  • Current state - the clock, the rates, the treasury
transcripts/tick-002-summary.md
transcripts

The Almshouse Lease

Tick 2. The chamber above the bonded sheds, first thing on the first morning.

Nan Shilling had been waiting on the stair since before the lamps were out. She was the steward of the river wards' burial club, which meant she held the subscriptions of four hundred people who could not afford to be buried, and she had brought the lease with her wrapped in oilcloth inside her coat.

"I'd not trouble you," she said to Partridge, "except there's a date on it."

The instrument was four pages of a sea factor's clerkly hand. An offer to buy out the club's lease on the almshouse at the Fetter Lane end, at a price that made Partridge's eyebrows go up, with signature wanted inside the week.

"It's a great deal of money," he said.

"It's a great deal of money," she agreed. "Which is why I've not signed it."

Partridge took it to Keel, because Partridge knew exactly what he was good for and exactly what he was not.

Keel read it twice, which from Keel was a statement.

"It is a generous offer," he said, "for the lease. It is not an offer for the lease. Page three, the second clause - the club conveys the lease and the reversion. You do not own a reversion to convey. The almshouse reverts to the parish on the expiry of the term, and the parish has never been asked."

"So we can't sell it."

"You can sell what you have. What this instrument says you are selling is a thing you do not have, and the warranty on page four makes the club good for the difference when that is discovered. Which it would be, in about nine years, by which time nobody signing today is alive to be sued." He set it down. "It is not a fraud. It is drafted by somebody who expected it to be signed by somebody who could not read it."

Nan Shilling looked at the page for a while.

"Say that to them," she said. "Not me. Them."

The room at the Fetter Lane almshouse held about sixty people and smelled of wet wool. Keel stood where he was put and explained the reversion clause three times, correctly, in the same voice he used in council.

It did not go well. It went adequately, which is not the same thing.

He was eleven minutes in before a man at the back asked whether the council had a view on the burial club's money in general, and whether this was the beginning of having a view, and Keel answered the question that had been asked rather than the one that had been meant, and the room cooled by several degrees.

Partridge stepped in at the fourteenth minute. He did not explain anything. He said that Mr Keel had read it so that none of them had to, and that Mr Keel was unpleasant about it the same way a splint is unpleasant, and that the club should not sign.

The club did not sign.

Walking back along Fetter Lane, Partridge said: "That went well."

"It did not go well."

"They kept the almshouse."

"They kept the almshouse," Keel allowed, "and they will remember that you told them to."

"Yes," said Partridge, entirely comfortable. "That's how it works."

The factor's name is on page one. It is a sea-factor house with Galician correspondents, and by noon it knew that the council's quartermaster had taken its instrument apart out loud in a room full of rivermen.

transcripts/tick-002-the-almshouse-lease.md
transcripts

The Bonded Entries

Tick 2. The customs house, three days.

A records query under the council's authority is a piece of paper that makes a clerk unhappy. Keel wrote it himself, which made it worse, because Keel's paper never has the loophole the clerk is hoping for.

They wanted the bonded entries for the eleven months either side of the sale. Thackeray wanted the factor's mark. Keel wanted everything the factor's mark had ever touched.

"You understand," said the under-clerk, "that's four hundred and some entries."

"Four hundred and sixty-one," said Keel. "I have the index."

The mark was not a name. That was the first thing, and Thackeray stopped dead over it.

"That is wrong," she said.

"It is a trading mark. Half the book is trading marks."

"Not for a Galician." She had the page turned toward him before he had finished objecting. "Keel, a Galician signs with his family. It is the whole of how they do business - the name is the credit. They pay historians to find them a better one. A Galician who trades behind a mark and no name is a Galician telling every other Galician in the world that he is either a spy or a disgrace, and they will assume the second one before they assume the first."

Keel looked at the entry for rather longer after that.

"Then why would a man do it?"

"Because he has to. Because the name is not his to use any more." She was writing. "Which means somebody in Galicia took it off him, which means there is a decision about him on paper in a Galician court, which means he is a man with something he is protecting that is not this cargo."

"That," said Keel, "is the most useful thing anybody has said in this building all week."

Thackeray found the mark on the second afternoon. Keel found what it meant on the third.

"Look at the dates," he said.

She looked at the dates. "They're entries."

"They are entries at intervals." He laid his finger down the column. "There. There. There. Eleven weeks, eleven weeks, eleven weeks. Then a gap of six. Then eleven again."

"Trade is seasonal."

"Trade is seasonal in spring and autumn. This is seasonal in nothing. Flax does not arrive every eleven weeks regardless of the river." He straightened. "Somebody is paying on a calendar. It is not a merchant's calendar."

Thackeray did not say anything for a moment, which from Thackeray was also a statement. She was writing.

"Whose calendar is it?"

"I don't know. I know what it isn't."

They did not get the bills. The bills are in a Galician house, and a Galician house does not open its correspondence to a Bryten city council because the city council asked nicely. Thackeray spent her third day of the week drafting the request anyway, on the grounds that a refusal is also information and that somebody, somewhere, would have to decide whether to send it.

Keel read the draft and made two changes, both of which made it colder.

"If they refuse," he said, "we learn they have a reason. If they agree, we learn they do not. Either is worth the postage."

"And if they warn him?"

Keel considered that.

"Then we learn he is worth warning."

Four hundred and sixty-one entries were pulled from the bonded book under council authority, by name, in a building where thirty people work and all of them talk.

transcripts/tick-002-the-bonded-entries.md
transcripts

The Pay Parade

Tick 2. The muster yard behind the north gate, before dawn.

Garrow called the whole Watch out, both reliefs, which had not been done since the appointment and which meant the gates were thin for an hour. He did it anyway.

Two hundred and fifty men stood in the dark and were not paid.

He did not make a speech. He stood where they could all see him and said the number out loud - nine weeks, twenty-two thousand five hundred gold - and then he said what the council had, which was rather less, and then he said what it was going to do, which was one week now and the rest as it came.

"That's one week in nine," somebody said, far enough back to be nobody.

"It is," said Garrow.

He waited to see if there was more. There was not, yet.

"I'll tell you the part you'll hear from somebody else by Thursday," he went on. "The new rate-book brings in about a thousand more a week. At that, the arrears clear around midwinter. I'm not promising midwinter. I'm telling you the sum, so that when a man tells you the council has money it isn't paying you, you'll know what he's leaving out."

Then he reorganised the rosters so that nobody was standing a double, which cost the gates an hour at each change and cost Garrow the rest of his morning, and which the rankers noticed more than the speech.

Afterwards, to Keel, in the chamber:

"You told them the figure."

"I told them our figure."

"You told two hundred and fifty unpaid men the exact size of the chest." Keel set down his pen. "Half of them collect the toll. We have just published, to the only people with their hands on the money, precisely how much we have and precisely how far behind we are."

"They already know how far behind we are. They count it in weeks, same as I do."

"Knowing you are owed is a grievance. Knowing what is in the chest is an argument. You have given the first man who wants to make one his opening."

Garrow thought about it properly, which Keel had learned to wait for.

"Aye," he said. "I'd do it again."

"I know," said Keel. "That is the part I'm recording."

The council paid one week - 2,500G - against the arrears. It was the first money the Watch had seen from this administration, and it was one-ninth of what it was owed.

Tick 3 - the week in summary

Year 0, week 3. Spring. Seasonal modifier 1.02. No halt.

What happened

Keel audited the toll collection and found nothing. The 376G hole from last week did not repeat; the week he was able to look at was clean, and a clean book is not an honest book, it is a book that balances. The query carried three to two over Garrow's objection. Garrow went down to the muster yard ahead of it to say it in his own voice first. It did not help much. The Watch was accused, cleared of nothing in particular, and not paid.

Garrow read the calendar. Thackeray laid the eleven-week intervals in front of him and he recognised them on sight - not a shipping cycle, a pay cycle, the interval at which a chest is run out to a standing post and back, with the gap of six being a double payment after a missed visit. Whoever is paying the Galician factor pays the way an army pays a post. Keel's response was that the correct move, for them, is now a fire in a customs house.

Partridge and Thackeray worked the river wards and got the ground. Hallet was hired by the week for lifting by a man who paid in coin and drank with nobody, and he went upriver - north bank, about a day's pull. It is the first thing in this affair that points at a place.

The porters nearly put a crane in the river. Forty of them, docked a third of a week over a consignment nobody could prove they spoiled. Partridge talked badly for eleven minutes and was saved by an unpaid gate man called Dowsett admitting, in front of the crowd, that he had not been paid since the back end of summer either. The crane is standing. The dispute is postponed, not settled, and forty porters have a date in their heads.

Brackwater's watch reported. Somebody came to the auctioneer's room in the second week and left carrying something. She has his face, his hours and the lane he uses. She spent this week behind him.

The Audit

Tick 3. The customs house, and then the muster yard.

Keel laid the thing out in council before he did it, because Keel never does anything without laying it out in council first.

"Three hundred and seventy-six gold did not arrive. I have checked the rate-book, the season, the throughput and my own arithmetic. The arithmetic is correct. The money is not here."

"It could be a hundred things," said Partridge.

"It could. I propose to find out which." He set down the single sheet. "A records query under authority on the toll collection. The gate books and the quay books, against the weighings."

Garrow had been quiet. He stayed quiet for a moment longer.

"That's the Watch," he said.

"That is half the Watch and half the harbour guard."

"You'll be telling two hundred and fifty men you think they're thieves. In the week they weren't paid."

"I will be telling them I have a gap and no explanation." Keel did not look away. "If I had a gap and an explanation I would not need the books."

"That is not what they will hear."

"No," Keel agreed. "It is not."

He went to the vote and carried it, three to two, and Garrow - having lost - went down to the muster yard ahead of him to say it out loud first, in his own voice, rather than let it arrive sideways.

The books were clean.

Keel spent three days in the gate house with the weighings beside the entries and found nothing: no double tenths, no short weeks, no hand in the column. He read the quay books after and found nothing there either. Whatever happened in the second week of this administration did not happen in the third, and the third is the only week he could see.

"It proves nothing," he told the council. "A clean book is a book that balances. It is not a book that is honest. I had one week to look at and I looked at the wrong one."

"You had the week you had."

"Yes." He was, for Keel, almost uncomfortable. "I would do it again and I accept that it was expensive."

What it cost:

A gate sergeant named Roper, who had been in the Watch nineteen years, asked Garrow in front of his own relief whether the council would be putting the findings up at the four places, same as the rate-book, so that the city would know the Watch had been cleared.

Garrow said he would ask.

The council did not post anything. There was nothing to post, which is exactly the problem: an accusation leaves a mark and an acquittal of nothing leaves none. The muster yard heard that the quartermaster had gone through their books and had not found what he was looking for, and drew its own conclusion about what he had been looking for.

Nobody was paid this week either. Keel refused it - the chest could not carry a week, and paying in the same seven days he had searched their books would look like hush money, which it would also have been.

Garrow did not argue the second point. He thought it was true.

The Crane

Tick 3. The Hithe, after dark.

The word came up from the river wards before the lamps were lit: the porters working Vesk & Sons' lower berth had been docked a third of their week over a spoiled consignment that nobody could prove they had spoiled, and about forty of them were going to put the factor's crane in the water.

They were not wrong about the consignment. They were, Garrow observed, entirely wrong about the crane, which weighs eleven tons and would take out the berth under it and four weeks of everybody's work.

Partridge went. Garrow went with him, and took eight of the Watch, and the eight men he took had not been paid in nine weeks and had spent the week being audited, which Garrow mentioned to them on the walk down and then did not mention again.

It very nearly came down.

Partridge got about eleven minutes - he had worked out by now that eleven minutes was roughly what a room gave him - and he used them badly at first, because he opened by agreeing with them, and a crowd that is agreed with at speed decides it is being managed.

What turned it was not Partridge. It was that one of the eight, a gate man called Dowsett, was recognised by a porter as somebody's cousin, and said, when asked whose side he was on, that he had not been paid since the back end of summer either and would be obliged if nobody made him work tonight on top of it.

The crowd laughed. A crowd that laughs is not a crowd any more.

Partridge got his eleven minutes after that and used them properly: the council would hear the docking as a dispute, in chamber, inside the fortnight, and in the meantime nobody was going in the water.

The crane is standing. The dispute is not settled - it is postponed, which is a different thing, and forty porters now have a date in their heads.

Vesk & Sons, who are sea factors with Galician correspondents and who have had a poor fortnight of the council, sent a note the following morning thanking the chamber for its prompt attention to the security of their plant. Keel read it twice for the barb and could not find one, which annoyed him.

"They mean it," said Partridge.

"People do not mean things in writing."

"Some people do."

Garrow, afterwards, to nobody in particular:

"Eight men who haven't been paid, standing between a wage dispute and a crane. If one of them had gone the other way tonight I'd not have been able to say he was wrong."

The Muster Cycle

Tick 3. The chamber, late, with the lamps going.

Thackeray had copied the intervals out on a long sheet because she could not think about a column in a customs book without also thinking about the customs book. Eleven weeks. Eleven weeks. Eleven weeks. A gap of six. Eleven again.

Keel was in the gate house with the toll books. Garrow was in the chamber because the chamber was warm and the muster yard was not speaking to him.

"You are good with numbers that are not money," Thackeray said. "What is eleven weeks?"

Garrow looked at the sheet for rather longer than she expected.

"It's a pay cycle," he said.

"Trade is seasonal, Keel says. This isn't seasonal."

"It isn't trade." He put a finger on the column, the way Keel had. "Field pay. You don't pay men in the field on a month, you pay them when the chest comes up, and the chest comes up on the march. Eleven weeks is about what it takes to run a paymaster out to a standing post and back, if the post is a long way out and the road is not good." He moved his finger. "And that's your six."

"The gap."

"That's not a gap. That's a double. Somebody missed a visit and paid twice when he came. I've been on the receiving end of that four times." Garrow sat back. "Whoever's paying your factor is paying him the way an army pays a post."

Thackeray did not write for a moment, which from Thackeray was alarming.

"We assumed a merchant," she said. "Everyone assumed a merchant. The money runs through a factor, so the money is commercial."

"The money runs through a factor because a factor is how you move it. That's not what it is. That's what it's wearing."

She wrote it up that night and put it in front of the council in the morning in four lines, which for Thackeray is a feat of compression:

The lot was bought with money that is paid out on a military calendar. We do not know whose army. We do not know that it is an army at all - only that whoever pays this is in the habit of paying posts rather than ports. The Needleford end of it is a mark in a bonded book, and the mark is paper, and paper does not forget.

Keel read it and said one thing.

"Then the correct move, for them, is a fire."

Nobody enjoyed the rest of the morning.

Tick 4 - the week in summary

Year 0, week 4. Spring. Seasonal modifier 1.04. HALT - severity 14.

What happened

The Watch petitioned, two hundred and thirteen of two hundred and fifty, carried in by Sergeant Elias Roper with his helmet under his arm. A published schedule, a payment inside the week, and no council officer in their books while the arrears stand. The third clause is one the charter does not let the council grant, and Roper knew it, and said so, and said that saying it publicly would do them nearly as well.

The council answered five to nothing: concede, concede, refuse - and then added a fourth thing nobody asked for. The council will publish the result of every inspection of every collector of the city's revenue, within seven days, found or not found, beginning with the week-three inspection that found nothing.

Keel wrote it and said in chamber that he did not think it was wise.

The auctioneer has forgotten. Thackeray went back to the room off the Hithe and found a charming man where a precise one had been. The nine pieces are gone out of the record of him. She stopped asking about the jewellery, started asking about him, and got a day, an hour and a back stair.

Brackwater had the same day, the same hour and the same stair in her own notebook, written from a doorway in the second week. The man who bought the auctioneer's memory is the man she has been following. She also reported, flatly, that he crossed the bridge twice on Thursday without needing to and stopped to tie an untied boot on Friday.

He knows he is being followed.

The porters' docking was heard in chamber inside the fortnight, as Partridge promised, and went half the porters' way.

The Auctioneer Has Forgotten

Tick 4. A room off the Hithe, second time.

He was charming about it. That was the first thing wrong.

The first time Thackeray had come to him he had been brusque and precise and faintly insulted by the question, which is what a man is like when he remembers something exactly and is being asked whether he remembers it. This time he was warm. He offered her tea. He said that there had been so very many pieces through this room, and that a year was a long time, and that he would hate to say a thing that turned out to be wrong and send the council off down a road that did not go anywhere.

She asked him nine questions and he gave her nine hedges.

"You have remembered this better before," she said at last.

"Have I? Then I was less careful before."

She did not get what she came for. The nine pieces are gone out of the record of him; whatever he once knew about where the lot went after it left his room is now a man being careful.

She got something else, and she got it because she had stopped asking about the jewellery and started asking about him.

A man had been to see him. Nine or ten days ago. Not a buyer - he had bought nothing and looked at nothing. The auctioneer, having decided to be charming, was charming about that too and said rather more than he intended about how tiresome it was to be interrupted during a sale, and in saying it gave her the day, the hour, and the detail that the man had come up the back stair from the lane rather than through the room.

Thackeray wrote it down and thanked him for the tea.

That night, with Brackwater:

"Back stair. From the lane. Nine or ten days."

Brackwater put her own notebook on the table and opened it to the page she had written in the second week, sitting in a doorway opposite that lane, and turned it round.

Same day. Same hour. Same stair.

"I have been following him since Tuesday," she said.

"And?"

"And on Thursday he crossed the bridge twice without needing to, and on Friday he stopped in the middle of the Hithe to tie a boot that was not untied." She closed the notebook. "He knows."

Two things happened in that room off the Hithe, a week and a half apart, and the council has now matched them to each other. The auctioneer has been bought. The man who bought him is the man Brackwater has a face for.

And he has worked out that he has a face.

What the Petition Asked

Tick 4. The chamber, mid-morning.

Roper came in uniform, with his helmet under his arm, and did not sit when he was offered a chair.

"Two hundred and thirteen of two hundred and fifty, sir," he said to Garrow, and then corrected himself and said it to the council, because that was the proper way of it and Roper had been nineteen years learning the proper way of things. "Eighty-five in the hundred."

Keel took the pages. He read them the way he reads everything, which is to say he read the third clause first.

"You have asked us to give up our audit."

"We've asked you to give it to somebody who doesn't owe us money."

"The charter vests the inspection of revenue in this council. It does not vest it anywhere else, and it does not permit this council to vest it anywhere else." Keel set the page down. "You are asking us for a thing we may not lawfully grant, Sergeant."

Roper had clearly expected this, and had clearly been told by somebody that this was what Keel would say.

"Then say that," he said. "Out loud, at the four places, with the schedule. Say you won't do it and why. That'll do us nearly as well, because the city will have read it."

There was quite a long silence.

"That is a better instrument than the one you have written," said Keel.

"I know," said Roper. "Mr Keel - nobody in that yard thinks you're a thief. They think you looked at them like one and then went quiet about it. The quiet is the whole of it. You could have fixed this for the price of a sheet of paper and you didn't, and now it's cost you a petition."

After he had gone:

"He is right," said Keel.

"He is," said Garrow.

"I want that in the minute. He is right, and I was correct, and the two are not the same thing, and I have now done it twice in four weeks."

transcripts/tick-004-the-petition.md
transcripts

The Verdict

Tick 4. The chamber, end of the week. Five people and one sheet of paper.

"Clause one and clause two are not in dispute," Keel began. "We owe it, we have it, and a schedule costs us nothing we were not already going to spend. I object to neither."

"Then say so and sit down," said Partridge.

"Clause three is the petition." Keel put his hand flat on it. "We cannot grant it. Not will not - cannot. The charter vests inspection of revenue in this council. A clause purporting to vest it in a Crown officer is a cession of chartered authority, and a council that gives away a power the Crown gave it discovers quite quickly that the Crown gave it to the council for a reason."

"So we refuse it," said Garrow, "and two hundred and fifty men hear that the council kept the right to search them and didn't give them anything back."

"Yes. Unless we give them what Roper actually asked for, which is not what is written on the page."

Brackwater looked up. She had not spoken in forty minutes.

"The quiet," she said.

"The quiet." Keel almost looked pleased. "He told us plainly. Nobody in that yard believes I am a thief-taker. They object to being searched in silence and cleared in silence. The grievance is not the audit. It is that an audit of the Watch is an event the city hears about and an acquittal of the Watch is not."

What the council resolved, five to nothing:

Clause one - conceded. A schedule of the eight weeks, with a date against each, posted at the four places.

Clause two - conceded. The first payment made within the week. Two thousand five hundred gold, against a chest of six thousand two hundred and fifty-seven.

Clause three - refused, in the posted document, with the reason stated in full: that the council may not give away a power it holds under charter, and that it would be lying to the Watch if it pretended otherwise.

And, not asked for: that this council will publish the result of any inspection of any collector of the city's revenue - the Watch, the harbour guard, the warehousemen, the guilds - within seven days, whether it finds anything or not, at the same four places. Beginning with the inspection of the third week, which found nothing, and which is to be posted tomorrow.

"You understand what you have just written," said Thackeray.

"I have written that we will never again audit anyone quietly."

"Yes."

"I do." Keel capped the ink. "I have spent four weeks explaining to this council that a thing can be correct and still cost more than it is worth. It appears I have been listening to myself."

Garrow read it over twice.

"They'll take it," he said.

"They will take it," Keel agreed. "That is not the same as it being wise, and I would like that in the minute as well."

Tick 5 - The Instrument Turns

The council was correct six times, lost ground three times, and spent the first week of its life going backwards in the chest - and nothing went wrong.

The week at a glance

Everything that happened to the council rather than by its choice.

The first deficit week since the charter, and no roll caused it. The schedule is simply larger than the margin, which the treasury file said it would be. Seven payments remain.

What the council spent

Fifteen of fifteen actions. Nobody was held in reserve - which cost nothing only because the windfall roll missed.

The thing that changed the size of the affair is not on this table as a thread. Until Friday the council was following a careful man. It is now looking at something with enough people, enough discipline and enough nerve to withdraw a compromised part inside a single night.

Read in this order

  1. The Four Places - the north gate muster yard, and then the whole city. The council pays the debt it ran up in week three, in person. This is the instrument working.
  2. The Captain's Petition - the chamber on Tuesday, the quay-side guardhouse on Thursday. Sixty-one of seventy-four. The two men who had just carried a petition could not carry this one, and the two who did failed by a point.
  3. The Second Sheet - the same instrument, four days later, pointed the other way. Read it immediately after the petition: the sting is entirely in the order of the week.
  4. The Amendment - the customs house, Thursday. The week's only clean result, and the first measurement of how long it takes the far side to be frightened.
  5. The Lower Stair - the lane, Monday to Thursday; the stair on Friday. A failure and then the largest fact of the month. Last, because nothing after it reads the same.

Artifact: the petition of the harbour guard.

The Amendment

Tick 5. The customs house, Thursday.

Thackeray went back for the later entries under the mark, having spent two days in a guardhouse being eloquent at a man who was not going to move, and said she found the second errand restful.

Records query under authority. Demand: reading Galician bonded entries past the minimum lead. Fit +2. Difficulty 7. Rolled 9. Total 11, margin 4.

She did not find the later entries. She found a correction to the earlier ones.

An amendment, filed the previous week against the bonded entries standing under the bare mark: a tidying, a clerical rectification, a handful of figures restated and two dates moved by a day. Entirely proper, entirely minor, and entirely pointless unless somebody wanted those particular entries to look slightly different from how they had looked when four hundred and sixty-one of them were read aloud in a room by a councillor with an index.

"It is not a cover-up," she told the chamber. "It is a correction. That is what makes it extraordinary."

"Explain," said Garrow.

"A man who wished to hide would have done nothing. Entries that nobody has questioned do not need correcting. This is somebody who heard that we had read his paper and could not bear to leave it untidy." She was taking notes while she said it, which is habitual and which the others have stopped finding rude. "And an amendment is itself a record. It has a date on it. It has a reason written on it. It has a hand. And it is archived, because corrections always are - you cannot have a correction that can be lost, or the original would mean nothing."

What it opened

Margin 4 - an intel bonus. The council took a substrate upgrade, which is the rarest result available to it.

An amendment must cite what it amends. This one cited the instruments by number - the bills of exchange themselves, named, numbered, and sitting in the archive of a building the council already has the run of.

The factor's mark has been, until Thursday, a thing written in a working ledger that wears out: rebound, recopied, lost to fire and to tidying, decaying a little every week whether anyone looked at it or not.

It is now paper that does not rot. An archived record does not fade. It will be exactly as good in a year as it is tonight.

"Then we have won something," said Partridge.

"We have won something that can only be taken from us all at once," said Thackeray. "A ledger goes a page at a time. An archive goes in one night or it does not go at all." She finally looked up from the notes. "Which means that if this is ever taken from us, we will know the hour it happened, and so will everyone else in that building."

And the other thing

She also knows who filed it: the mark's own Needleford correspondent, by instruction, in the week after the council's query - which is not a deduction and did not come out of the ledger. It came out of the amendment's own face. Somebody signs these.

"So he is not in Needleford," said Keel.

"He is not in Needleford. He has a man here, and his man does what he is told within about a fortnight of being told it." Thackeray closed the book. "Which means that from the week we first read his paper, we have been able to measure how long it takes him to be frightened."

Keel, later, to nobody in particular:

"Four hundred and sixty-one entries, and the thing of value was the four-hundred-and-sixty-second, which he wrote himself, after we left."

transcripts

The Captain's Petition

Tick 5. The chamber, Tuesday; the quay-side guardhouse, Thursday.

Dray delivered it himself, read it out, and left without waiting to be answered, which Keel said afterward was the most carefully managed thing that had happened to the council since the charter.

"He is not angry," Keel said. "Men who are angry stay to watch you read it."

What the council could not spend

Four of the council's fifteen actions were already gone before Tuesday - two each for Keel and Garrow, spent at the muster yard answering the Watch. A speaker at a petition costs two.

So the two men who had just carried a petition could not carry this one. Keel had one action and the law was not the hill anyway. Garrow had one action and was not going to be asked to talk to a captain about notice periods.

"We send Partridge," said Thackeray.

"On what ground?"

"Candour. He has nothing else and he does not need anything else."

"Then I will go with him," she said, "on evidence, because I can put the week-three query on the table and show him exactly which four hundred and sixty-one entries it touched and which it did not."

Committed: Partridge on candour, Thackeray on evidence. Four actions. Nobody in the room knew yet how either would score.

What they offered

The council granted the first clause and the second without argument - the naming, and the fair copy at the hour of posting. Both were owed and both were cheap.

The third it would not grant, and Keel wrote the reason out in a single sentence that Partridge then declined to read aloud because he said he would rather say it in his own words.

And then the council offered a thing Dray had not asked for: that the captain may have a man of his own in the room from the moment any inspection of the quay books opens - not notice beforehand, but presence throughout, from the first page.

"It answers what he is actually frightened of," Partridge said. "He is not frightened of being searched. He is frightened of being searched in the dark."

The guardhouse

Fit +4 - Partridge on candour at +3, Thackeray on evidence at +2, both positive. Difficulty 8: most of the petition's weight refused, less two for the unasked-for offer, less one for the evidence, plus two because the refused clause is the reason the petition exists.

Rolled 3. Total 7, against 8. Failed by one.

Partridge was good. He said the true thing and the unflattering part of it: that the council had searched the quay books on its own initiative, had said nothing for a fortnight, had only corrected it by accident in a sheet written for somebody else, and that nobody in the chamber had noticed what they were doing to the harbour guard because nobody in the chamber had thought about the harbour guard at all.

Thackeray put the query on the table and walked him through it, entry by entry, and Dray read it properly, which took some time.

Then he folded his hands.

"Before you go on. Your quartermaster laid a query on the quay books on Wednesday."

Partridge, who had not known, said: "Yes."

"I delivered this on Tuesday." Dray said it without heat at all, which was worse. "I am not accusing anyone of spite. I do not think he thought about me for one minute. That is rather the point I came here to make, and he has made it better than I could."

He let that sit.

"Now the other thing. You refused this clause to the Watch on the charter. You cannot refuse it to me on the charter, because notice is not inspection and you know it. So you are refusing it on preference."

"Yes," said Partridge.

"Say the preference."

"We think seven days is long enough to move something."

Dray nodded slowly, as though a sum had come out.

"Then we are not suspected," he said. "We are merely not trusted, which is a smaller word and a longer one." He pushed the query back across the table. "Your man in the room is a good offer. I will think about it. I will not take it this week, because if I take it this week it is a kindness, and in a month it will be all I have."

"And because of Wednesday," said Thackeray.

"And because of Wednesday. I would have taken it on Monday."

He did not withdraw the petition. He did not raise his voice at any point. He asked whether the fair-copy clause would be honoured in the meantime, was told yes, and said that he would send his thanks for that separately so that it was not confused with this.

"He is going to say no twice," Thackeray said on the stair, "and take it the third time, and by then it will be worth more to him."

"He is going to say no twice," Keel agreed, when they told him, "and the second no will be harder than the first, because the rules of the thing are that it gets harder every time we fail. We have spent four actions and one week of a three-week clock and we have moved nothing at all."

"We moved him," said Partridge.

"We did not move the document." Keel put his pen down. "I am not saying you were wrong. I am saying we had the best speakers we could afford, and we could not afford the best speakers."

transcripts/tick-005-the-captains-petition.md
transcripts

The Four Places

Tick 5. The north gate muster yard, and then the whole city.

Two speakers went, which cost the council four of its fifteen actions in a week it could not spare them, and Garrow said afterwards that it was the cheapest thing they had bought since the Bill of Rates.

Keel stood on the law. He read the third clause aloud, then read the charter, and then explained - without decoration, in the voice he uses for figures - why the second could not grant the first. He did not apologise for it. He said that a council which pretended to give away a power it was holding in trust would be lying to them, and that he would rather be refused honestly than obliged dishonestly, and that he assumed they felt the same.

Garrow stood on candour. He said the part Keel would not: that the search had been his own doing, over Garrow's objection, three to two; that it found nothing; and that the council had then said nothing for a week, which was the whole of the injury and nobody had needed to do it.

Roper listened to both and asked one question.

"The fourth part. Every collector, every time, found or not found, inside seven days. Is that a promise to us, or a promise to the city?"

"To the city," said Keel. "You are merely first."

"Then put it up."

What went up at the four places

Four sheets, posted at the gate, the Hithe, the weighing yard and the chamber door, the same four places as the Bill of Rates.

  1. The schedule. Eight weeks, with a date against each, the first dated that week.
  2. The refusal, in full, with the charter clause printed under it.
  3. The undertaking. Every inspection of every collector of the city's revenue, published within seven days, found or not found.
  4. The result of the week-three inspection, which found nothing.

The fourth sheet is the one that mattered and nobody in the chamber had noticed why.

The clause went somewhere Keel had not aimed it

Keel's week-three records query had been laid on the toll collection - the gate books and the quay books. He had searched the harbour guard in the same breath as the Watch, and found nothing there either, and said nothing about that for a week too.

So the fourth sheet cleared two bodies, and only one of them had asked.

The harbour guard is owned by the nobles and guild masters, has never been owed a penny by this council, and has spent a fortnight being quietly suspected by a city that noticed the quartermaster in their books. By Thursday their captain had sent a note to the guildhall asking whether the council's undertaking was to be relied upon, which is not gratitude, but is not what he would have sent a fortnight ago.

And the guildhall read the same sheet and drew the opposite conclusion. A council that has promised to publish every search it makes has promised, in the same sentence, to make them.

Keel, reading the note from the harbour guard:

"I drafted that clause for two hundred and fifty men at a gate."

"It went further than you aimed it," said Thackeray.

"It went further than I can take back." He set it down. "Which is the same sentence I said to this council on Friday, and I was complaining then."

transcripts/tick-005-the-four-places.md
transcripts

The Lower Stair

Tick 5. The lane by the Hithe, Monday to Thursday. The lower stair, Friday.

Brackwater put the question to the council before she moved, as she always does.

"He knows he was followed. He does not know for how long, or by whom, or whether it was the council. There are three things he can do and I would like to be told which of them we are prepared to pay for."

"Name them," said Keel.

"He stays and is careful. He stays and feeds me something false. Or he goes."

"And what do you want to do?"

"I want to take him," said Brackwater, "and I am telling you that because you asked, and not because I am asking."

Keel did not look up. "On what charge."

"None."

"Then no."

"Then no," she agreed, and that was the whole of it. She has never once argued that point and the council has stopped expecting her to.

What she did instead

Ask around, no names. The lodging houses at the lane end. Demand: finding where a careful foreigner lodges without the asking travelling. Fit +1 - the tradecraft is hers, but a silent foreign woman asking after a lodger is the most memorable thing to happen in a lodging house that week. Difficulty 7.

Rolled 1. Total 2, against 7. Failed badly.

Four days of it. She was pleasant, she was vague, she bought nothing and promised nothing, and she came away with the names of eleven houses and no lodger in any of them, because the woman who keeps the house at the lane end decided within about nine seconds that whatever this was, it was not going to be settled in her parlour, and everyone else in the lane took their cue from her.

"I was wrong about the shape of it," Brackwater said. "I can follow a man in this city. I cannot ask in this city. They see a foreigner before they see a councillor and I have no way to make them stop."

What came to her anyway

On Thursday a boy she had not spoken to came and found her, because of a thing that had been said in front of him on Tuesday and had not made sense until the lane filled up with questions.

The man was gone. Not quietly - quickly. And he had not gone alone.

A room at the lane end was emptied between the middle of the night and first light, by two men who were not the lodger and who knew which stair to use. A boat was hired at the lower stair before dawn, by one of the two, paid in coin, for a passage upriver. The lodger was not the one who hired it and the lodger was not the one who paid. He was walked down and put in it.

He did not run. He was moved.

Brackwater, reporting it, in about four sentences, in the voice she uses:

"I have been thinking about him as a man. He is not a man, he is a part. Parts are not frightened and parts do not run. Somebody looked at the part, decided it was compromised, and withdrew it inside a night - which is the first thing in four weeks that has told us the size of what we are looking at."

"It also means we lost him," said Partridge.

"It means we lost him." She set her notebook on the table. "I would rather know there are enough of them to do that than still be following one careful man around a bridge."

The lower stair, Friday

Physical search. Two actions. Demand: reading a departure that was arranged in a hurry. Fit +3. Difficulty 7. Rolled 8. Total 11, margin 4.

She did not go back to the lane. She went to the water, where the thing had actually happened, and worked the lower stair instead - the sheds, the hire boards, the men who were awake at that hour because they are always awake at that hour.

The hire was written down. It is written down because watermen have been losing boats to people who hire them and do not bring them back for as long as there have been boats, and so every stair in Needleford keeps a book: the hirer, the hour, the craft, the destination given, the deposit, and a mark or a name.

Sim Rudd keeps the book at the lower stair. He is not frightened of the council and he does not like being in it, and he let Brackwater read it because she asked for one night and not for the book.

Margin 4 - an intel bonus. A new thread: the hire, resting on the hire book, which is a working record, and on Rudd's own memory of the two men.

What it has in it, tonight: an hour, a craft, a deposit left unclaimed, a destination given as upriver, and a mark in the hirer's own hand that is not a name.

Keel, when he was shown it:

"Upriver. North bank, a day's pull, which we have had for a fortnight. Thirty miles of bank, and now a boat somewhere on it."

"Can we follow it?"

"We can follow nothing. We have no vessel, no authority past the city boundary stones worth the name, and no notion whether he was put ashore at the first bend or the last." He looked at the mark for a while. "We have a man's handwriting and the hour he was afraid. That is more than we had on Sunday and it is not a boat."

transcripts/tick-005-the-lower-stair.md
transcripts

The Second Sheet

Tick 5. The chamber, Monday. The customs house, Wednesday. The four places, Saturday.

Keel raised it on Monday, first item, before the petition had even arrived.

"The three hundred and seventy-six gold is in the second week of the rate-book. I audited the third week. The ruling is that I may name a week that has gone, so I intend to name the second one."

Garrow said: "No."

"On what ground."

"On the ground that we stood in their yard four days ago and told them we were keeping the power to search them, and if the first thing we do with it is search them, then what we told them was not a principle, it was a notice."

"The money is missing."

"Three hundred and seventy-six gold," said Garrow. "We are two weeks' wages short of them. They are owed seventeen thousand five hundred."

"Those are different questions."

"Not to them they aren't."

Thackeray asked the question that decided it.

"If you find it, what happens?"

"Then I have found it."

"No. Mechanically. What happens on Saturday?"

Keel was quiet for rather longer than he usually is.

"Then I post a sheet at the four places saying that the council inspected the Watch's books for a second time, and what it found. Within seven days. Found or not found."

"You wrote that clause," said Garrow.

"I did. I said at the time I did not think it was wise." He straightened the papers in front of him, which is what he does instead of raising his voice. "I still do not. I am going to do it anyway, because a shortfall in the collection that nobody chases is a shortfall that happens again, and I would rather be disliked for looking than comfortable for not."

Three to two. The same two as in week three, and the same result.

The customs house, Wednesday

Records query under authority, naming the second week of the rate-book. Ambrose Keel alone. Demand: reading a toll collection's books against the weighings for a named past week's shortfall. Fit +3. Difficulty 6.

Rolled 1. Total 4, against 6. Failed.

He had the right week. He had the weighings, the gate tallies and the receipt book in three stacks and his own index between them, and he worked through the second week of the rate-book from Monday morning to Sunday night, twice, and at the end of it the column added up.

It adds up because somebody made it add up. Keel knows this. He said so, out loud, to a clerk who had not asked.

"A book that reconciles is not an honest book. It is a book that has been made to reconcile." He closed it. "Which I cannot put on a sheet, because it is an opinion."

The four places, Saturday

The sheet went up because the undertaking said it had to.

Inspection of the toll collection, second week of the rate-book, made under authority by Ambrose Keel, quartermaster, on behalf of the council. The gate books, the quay books and the weighings were compared. Nothing was found.

Four lines. The shortest of the sheets and the only one that cost anything.

Roper read it at the gate on Saturday afternoon with about thirty men around him and did not say very much. What he said was reported to Garrow by two separate people in the same words, which is usually how you know a thing was said carefully.

"They kept the power on Friday and used it on Wednesday."

And then: "At least they put it up."

Garrow, in chamber, afterwards:

"I'm not going to say I told you so."

"You are entitled to."

"It wouldn't help." Garrow looked at the window for a while. "Here is the part you want to write down. They're not angrier than they were. They're more careful than they were, and so is the captain on the quays, and the two of them have started comparing notes about us. That's new. That didn't exist a fortnight ago."

"The undertaking made it," said Keel.

"The undertaking made it possible. You made it."

transcripts/tick-005-the-second-sheet.md
transcripts

Tick 6 - A Hundred and Eight

Saturday. The chamber. The week's remittances counted, the week's papers laid, and two numbers that nobody wanted on the same table.

Keel put the first sheet down at ten o'clock and did not sit.

"The council is in deficit for the second consecutive week. I will give the figures once and then I will stop, because the figures are not the difficult part."

"Go on," said Partridge.

"Ordinary income at this season is about ten thousand three hundred. Ordinary expenditure, with every line funded and nothing cut, is eight thousand. The ordinary margin is therefore about two thousand two hundred and thirty gold a week." He turned the sheet so it faced the room, which was a courtesy and not a necessity, since nobody else was going to read it. "The schedule we published to the Watch is two thousand five hundred a week for eight weeks. One has been paid. Six remain after today. The chest stands at five thousand six hundred and ninety-five."

"And at that rate?"

"At that rate the chest is empty in the twentieth week of the year and the schedule has two payments still on it. The seasonal modifier peaks in the fourteenth week and then falls for twenty-six. I have not allowed for a robbery, a failure on an upkeep line, or a petition conceded with money, and I will not, because allowing for them would be an invention and the arithmetic is bad enough without one."

Garrow said: "How much is the schedule short by, in a week?"

"Two hundred and seventy gold last week. Two hundred and ninety-two this week."

"Then it was always short."

"It was always short," Keel said. "I knew that when I drafted it. I said so in chamber and the council voted for it anyway and I voted for it with them, because a schedule we can almost keep is worth more than a schedule we can keep and nobody believes. I am not reopening that. I am telling you that the almost runs out in the spring and not in the winter, which is earlier than I said, and the reason it is earlier is this."

He put down the second sheet.

"Guild and market dues. Twelve hundred gold, assessed by the guilds, collected by the guilds, remitted by the guilds on the Saturday. One thousand and ninety-two gold was remitted this Saturday."

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"A hundred and eight," said Thackeray.

"A hundred and eight."

"Is it an error?"

"It is not an error in the addition, because I have done the addition. Whether it is an error of some other kind I cannot say from a remittance sheet, and that is precisely the difficulty." Keel laid his hand flat on the paper, which for Keel is a gesture of some violence. "There is one way to find out what a hundred and eight gold is, and it is a records query under authority on the guilds' own assessment. It costs an action. It is noticed. And under the undertaking this council gave in the fourth week, the result of it goes up on a wall at four places within seven days, found or not found."

Partridge had got there already and had gone rather pale.

"Two days ago," he said, "the guildhall asked us to put them in the room."

"Two days ago the guildhall asked us to put them in the room."

"And if we audit them on Monday - "

"Then the first act of this council following the guildhall's petition is to search the guildhall's books, and to post that it has done so, at the four places, in the same hand as the undertaking." Keel's voice did not change at all. "It will not matter what we find. It will not matter that the shortfall is real, that I noticed it on the Saturday, and that I would have noticed it in any week in which it occurred. Warden Pell will be asked by a hundred and eleven people whether the council searched the guilds because of the petition, and he will say, honestly, that he does not know."

"And if we don't audit them?"

"Then a collection in this city has learned that it can be a hundred gold short in a week in which it has the council in a difficult position." He took his hand off the paper. "I would like to say that is the smaller harm. I have been trying to say it since eight o'clock this morning and I cannot make the sentence true."

Garrow, from the window: "How many weeks can we leave it?"

"Three. Four. There is no clock on it except the next shortfall."

"Then leave it three." Garrow turned round. "If it comes again, it isn't the petition. If it doesn't come again, we were right to wait and we have lost a hundred and eight gold, which is a day and a half of the Watch." He sat down. "I'd rather lose the coin than hand the warden the only grievance he hasn't got."

"That is also my view," Keel said, "and I want it recorded that it is a judgement and not a finding, because in six weeks somebody will ask why the council sat on a shortfall and I intend the minute to say that we chose to."

The rest of the sheet

Thackeray's week. She laid a query on the archived bills at the customs house - the bills themselves, by number, out of the amendment - and came away with nothing at all.

"It is not gone," she said, when Garrow asked whether that was the end of it. "That is the thing I want to be clear about, because I was quite insufferable about it last week and I would like to be accurate about it this week. It is paper, it is archived, it does not rot, and it will be exactly where it is in a year. I went and I asked the wrong questions of it for a day and a half. That is not the same as a thread going cold and I would be grateful if nobody writes it down as though it were."

And Hallet, who has been dead eleven months. She spent her third day at the Blackcap, where she is nobody and knows she is nobody, and asked after a drowned boatman in a room full of men who had no reason to tell an Acaadian anything - and two of them told her.

"He said he was coming into more. We had that. What we did not have is what he said it was for." She found the page. "He told them the work was to run through the winter. He had three weeks of it in the autumn and he had been told there would be more and he had said yes. That is why he settled his score at the Blackcap in full - not because he knew he was in danger. Because he thought he was in regular employment."

"He was hired for a job that was meant to outlast him," Keel said.

"He was hired for a job that was meant to run eleven months ago and go on. And somebody is still paying the same mark on the same eleven-week turn." She closed the book. "I don't know what that is. But a man does not hire winter labour to lift one thing once."

Garrow's two measurements. Relief, fire and contingency, inspected: 80, sound. The cheapest line on the sheet and the healthiest, and the only one that has never been looked at.

"All three are now known by number," Keel said. "Quays at seventy, dredging at sixty, relief at eighty. For the first time since the charter this council knows what it owns."

"And the first thing it does with knowing," Garrow said, "is work out which one to stop paying for."

"Yes."

"Relief has the most room to fall and the fewest men watching it fall." Garrow said it flatly, as a fact about the world, because it is one. "That's why I went and looked at it."

And the river. He took a boat up to the upper boundary stone on the Friday with a pilot and came back with no information the council had asked for and a good deal it had not: that the stone is a mile above the bridge, that the narrows begin directly above it and a following boat can be seen from ahead there, that the first real landing beyond is Harrowby staithe, three hours up, where everything stops or is seen not stopping.

"And past the stone?"

"Past the stone the Watch doesn't go," Garrow said. "I'm not putting that to them and they're not asking me to. Anything past the stone is one of us, or it's nobody."

He laid the pilot's note on the table beside the sheet with the hundred and eight on it.

"We'd not have caught the boat," he said. "I want that said out loud, because it has been sitting in this room all week like something we failed at. Six beats of a start on the water, a week cold, and a boundary stone a mile up. We'd have spent four actions and two hundred men being tired, and watched him go round the bend at Harrowby."

"So we were right not to go," said Partridge.

"We were right not to go this week." Garrow put a finger on the pilot's note. "Next time there's a boat, I want men at Harrowby before there's a boat. That's the whole of what I learned and it cost an action and it was worth it."

Not checks

The burn

A cover placed against a thread pays out to anyone who pulls that thread inside the burn window, whether the pull succeeds or not. Check 7 failed by four on a rolled 1 and the window was open. What the council was handed is in ../ledger/threads.md and what it cost the far side is sealed.

The council does not know burns exist. Brackwater went back to that room for her own reasons and will not be told that the worst roll of her tenure bought the week's best lead.

No halt. Refusing a petition is awkward to undo and not impossible; it touches seventy-four quay men; it cost three points of standing and no coin; it invents nothing.

The petition delivered on Thursday is a different matter, and the council has not answered it. See ../state/current.md.

Mechanics the week reached for and did not find

Flagged for the DM, not resolved here:

  1. What an answer to a petition costs in actions. ../rules/petitions.md prices CONCEDE, ANSWER IN PART and REFUSE at 1 action each. Tick 5 charged 2 per speaker, 4 for a pair, and ../ledger/factions.md repeats that figure in prose. They cannot both be right. This tick followed the rules file - 1 action for the answer, 1 more for the second councillor speaking - and the discrepancy is on the stack.
  2. A records query under authority costs "standing with the holder", and the customs establishment has no standing log. Three queries have now been laid on the bonded entries - ticks 2, 5 and 6 - and none has cost anything, because the holder is not a faction. Either it should be one, or the rule should say what happens when the holder is an office rather than a body.

What the week was. The council spent six weeks being correct in public and was handed the bill for it on Thursday by a seventy-one-year-old cooper's son who went out of his way to say he did not dislike them.

And the two things nobody in the chamber has put together. The guilds asked to be in the room on Thursday. The guilds were a hundred and eight gold short on Saturday. The council noticed both and has decided, for good reasons, that it cannot look at the second one without poisoning the first.

transcripts/tick-006-summary.md
transcripts

Tick 6 - The Bench, the Stair and the Boy

Tuesday and Friday. Three errands in two wards, two of which failed, and the one that failed worst was the one that paid.

Tuesday, the lower stair

She asked for the book.

That was the whole of it, and she knew it was the whole of it before she had finished the sentence, and she did not try to take it back, because taking it back is worse.

Sim Rudd went on coiling. He coiled about eight feet of line while she stood there, and then he said:

"No."

"You let me read a night of it."

"You asked for the night."

"I am asking for the autumn."

"Aye." Another arm's length of rope. "That's the book."

She waited, which is what she is for. Rudd hung the coil on its peg and picked up the next one and did not look at her once.

"Man comes to a stair, wants a boat," he said. "I write it. Been writing it thirty year. Reason I can write it is nobody's ever come and read it." He put a thumb on a splice and worked it. "Last week I gave you a night because you asked for a night. Give you the autumn, I've not got a book no more. I've got a thing the council reads."

"I am not the council on this stair."

"You are on every stair." He said it without heat at all, which is why it landed. "Was you not, you'd not have got the night."

She stayed a while longer, because leaving immediately would have told him he was right in a way she did not want him to be, and then she went.

She did not get the mark. Keel and Garrow sat over the eleven-week calendar on Saturday morning with nothing to lay beside it, having committed their Tuesday to the comparison on Monday.

Keel's note on the matter, in full:

"The decision to hold two actions against a document we did not yet have was mine. It was not unreasonable and it was wrong. I will not do it again."

Tuesday, the bench off Fetter Lane

It is not a bench anybody built. It is four feet of stone sill outside the Ropewalk where the wall goes back a yard, and men who want a week's work sit on it from first light, and men who want men come and look at them.

Partridge sat on it for half an hour and asked nobody anything.

He asked after a chest, and a hand that had been crushed in the spring, and somebody's mother, and whether the lighterman's eldest had got the place at the tannery, and by the time Abel Yeo came and sat down two feet away it would have been strange if he had not.

"Surgeon."

"Abel. How's Nell?"

"She's - aye. She's well, surgeon. She's well." Yeo put his hands between his knees. "She asks after you."

"She does not, she tells everyone I made her drink something foul, and she's right, and it worked." Partridge let that sit. "I'm after the autumn before last. Three weeks of lifting, off this bench."

"I'll not say he did and I'll not say he didn't."

"I've not asked you about anybody yet."

Yeo laughed, badly.

"No. No, you've not." He looked down the row at the other men on the sill, who were not looking at him, carefully. "It were off this bench. I'll say that much. Hallet and me and Crisp Dawkin and two I'd not know again. Three week, good coin, paid Fridays, and you went up the river and you come back Sunday night and you never asked where you'd been because nobody asks where they've been."

"Who booked it?"

"It weren't a man booked it, surgeon, that's - " He stopped. "That's the thing I never - it were an account. That's how it's done here, when it's a yard hiring. Man comes down with a name and the name's good for the wages, and you take the name, and you don't take the man."

"What was the name?"

"Thurlow." Yeo said it the way a man says a word he has not had cause to think about in two years. "Thurlow and Company, carriers. Thurlow and Company, carriers. That's what was called out off the step, three mornings running, and that's what was wrote on the ticket." He turned his hands over. "Called it out myself twice, after, when they come back for more."

"They came back."

"They come back," Yeo said, "and I'll not say he did and I'll not say he didn't, but they come back."

There is no Thurlow and Company in the carting guild's own list of yards. There is no Thurlow and Company paying dues. There are eleven carriers in Needleford and the council knows the name of every one of them, because Keel wrote the list himself in the second week.

Friday, the room at the lane end

The room had been scrubbed, limewashed and let to a family of five.

Brackwater had two actions and she spent both of them on it, and she went over the boards, the sill, the chimney breast, the gap behind the wainscot and the yard at the back, and the only thing in that room that was not of the family who lived in it now was a boot-nail in a floor joint, and a boot-nail in a floor joint is a boot-nail in a floor joint.

She came down the stairs at four in the afternoon with nothing, which she does not mind, and with nothing twice in one week, which she does.

The boy was sitting on the bottom step.

"You're the one that come about the top room."

She looked at him.

"Last week it were my mam you asked," said Nye Soal, "and she said nobody and nothing and she'd not have strangers in the house talked of, and she says that to everybody, she said it to the tally man and he's been coming four years, and she wasn't lying exactly only she sleeps in the back and I sleep under the stair and that's by the door and I'm up before her every day of the week because of the milk."

"Yes."

"Well, I seen them."

She sat down on the step. She did not say anything, which is the thing she is better at than anyone in Needleford.

"Two of them, and they come down and the one carried the chest end-on and the other had the bag and they was quiet about it but they wasn't quick, that's what I thought after, they wasn't quick at all, and then they was gone down the lane toward the water." He took a breath he did not need. "And then in the morning one of them come back."

"In the morning."

"In the morning," said Nye. "After light. There was a bag got left, up against the door frame, and he come back up for it and he come past me and he said sorry and he stepped round me, and he went up and he come down with it, and he went off slow, and I thought he were a lodger. I thought he were a lodger till the room were let again and I counted and there weren't nobody could have been."

"What did he look like?"

"He were - " and then the boy stopped, and did the thing that is the whole reason this is worth anything. "No. He weren't tall. I said tall to the ostler's lad but he weren't, he were middling, he were about the same as you, and he had a good coat and bad boots and the coat were better than the boots by a long way and that's what I thought were queer, and he had a thumb - " he held his own up, " - he had a thumb he couldn't bend, this one, he took the bag with the other hand and he had to turn right round to get the latch."

Brackwater was quiet for some seconds.

"How old are you?"

"Twelve."

"Does your mother know you talk to people on the step?"

"No."

"Keep it that way," she said, and gave him nothing at all, and went to find Partridge.

She reported it in eleven words, standing, in the chamber, at six o'clock:

"The room is nothing. There is a boy. One came back in the morning."

And when Keel asked her what she made of it:

"Nothing yet. He stepped round a child and said sorry."

And when Keel asked her again, differently, as he does:

"A man in a hurry does not come back for a bag."

transcripts/tick-006-the-bench-the-stair-and-the-boy.md
transcripts

Tick 6 - The Roll

Thursday. The chamber, and then Partridge's back room at the almshouse with ninety-one names on the table. Warden Osric Pell, for the guildhall.

He came at the hour he had asked for, with two men to carry the roll and no lawyer, and he read the whole of it aloud himself, which took eleven minutes and cost him his voice twice.

Keel had expected money. He said afterwards that this was the single largest error of judgement he had made since the charter, and that he had made it on the evidence, which is what made it interesting.

"We do not petition about money," Pell read. "The council should understand that first, because it has spent five weeks expecting us to."

Thackeray stopped writing.

He went through it unhurried. The fee corrected upward in the first week and not raised, and the guildhall not asked. The amendment drafted in the guildhall's own words and sent to the guildhall as a copy - "and we are grateful for the copy, and we note that it was a copy." The undertaking at the four places, which the guildhall read on a wall.

"Each of those is a small thing. We do not say otherwise."

Garrow, who had been looking at the window, turned round at that.

"We say that in five weeks this council has been correct about us, fair to us, prompt with us, and has not once needed to have us in the room while it was being any of those things - and that a city governed well by five strangers is still a city governed by five strangers."

Then the ask. Prior sight of any measure touching a rate, a bonded fee or the use of a quay. A guildhall man in the chamber while it is read, heard before the vote, as of right and not by the council's leave.

"We do not ask for a vote. We know the charter. Councillor Keel has read us the charter twice and we have no complaint about his reading of it."

"We ask to be in the room."

Keel spoke first, because he always does and because the others have learned to let him.

"The council will not answer this today. Under the ordinary practice a demand of this weight carries four weeks and the council intends to use them. I will not pretend to you that this is a courtesy. It is because the answer is difficult and I would rather give you a difficult answer in four weeks than an easy one now."

"That is what I came for," Pell said.

"You came for the room."

"I came for four weeks of this council thinking about the room." The warden rolled the thing up himself, one turn at a time, with hands that did not work well. "If I had wanted a quick answer I would have asked for money. You would have given me a schedule by Friday. You are very good at schedules."

"We are," Keel said.

"I know. The Watch has one." Pell put the roll under his arm. "Councillor, I want to say a thing and then I will go, because I am seventy-one and standing is work. I do not dislike this council. The guildhall does not dislike this council. I would be a liar if I let you think there was heat in this."

"Then why now?"

"Because you are new, and careful, and you want to be thought well of." He said it without any edge at all, which was what made Thackeray write it down twice. "In ten years you will be none of those things, or you will be gone and someone else will be sitting there who never was any of them. I am not establishing a right against you. I am establishing it against whoever comes next, and the only moment it can be established is while the men in the chairs still mind what is thought of them."

He got to the door before Partridge caught up with him and helped him down the step, which he allowed.

Ninety-one of a hundred and twelve

That evening. Partridge had the roll copied before it left the chamber, which is the only reason any of this was possible, and he and Thackeray went through it at the almshouse with two lamps.

"Eighty-one," Thackeray said. "Eighty-one and a fraction. He needed eighty."

"So three."

"Three signatures the other way and the instrument is waste paper, yes, and no - and I want to be careful here, because this is the part where I usually say something confident and am wrong - three as delivered. The threshold is eighty of those who could sign, and the people who could sign are the hundred and twelve he counted, and he counted them, which means he chose who was in the hundred and twelve." She tapped the sheet. "A warden who wanted a bigger majority could have found twelve lesser companies with a share of a quay between them and put them in the denominator, and had ninety-one of a hundred and twenty-four, and failed."

"Or left people out and had ninety-one of ninety-five."

"He did not do that either." She sat back. "He gave us an honest denominator and a thin majority. I find that much more frightening than a fat one."

Partridge had been going down the missing names with a finger, the way he went down a list of a ward's sick.

"Twenty-one not on it," he said. "Four ropers - that's one family, they're all one family, and the eldest is dying and I've been to the house. Two corn factors who are in Oldhithe until the ford opens, so they weren't asked, they were absent. The cooper's company isn't on it and the warden is a cooper, which I cannot make sense of and I'm not going to pretend I can. Eleven lightermen's masters." He stopped.

"And."

"And Marget Stroud."

Thackeray looked up.

"The warehousemen's guild is not on this roll," Partridge said. "Not Stroud, not her factor, not her yard man, not one of the nine who signed her own petition in the first week. The guild that has had two good weeks out of us, and the only guild in this city that has ever had anything out of us, has left the guildhall's roll alone."

"Does that mean she is with us?"

"No." He said it immediately. "It means she didn't sign. Those are enormously different and I've been in this city five weeks and I am not going to guess which one it is at ten o'clock at night off a copied list." He pushed the lamp back. "Ines, I can tell you who is not on it. I cannot tell you one single thing about whether any of them would stay off it if the warden went round in the morning and asked them properly. I'd be making it up, and you'd write it down, and in three weeks Keel would quote it at a room."

"That is the most useful thing anybody has said to me all week," Thackeray said, "and I want it minuted that I think so, because I am about to spend four days wanting to make something of it."

What the council has, at the end of Thursday: a count, a denominator it believes is honest, and twenty-one names.

What the council does not have: any reason to think the twenty-one are a crack rather than a coincidence.

Garrow, on the stair, on the way out, to nobody:

"Three signatures."

And then:

"That's not a weakness. That's a man who knows exactly what he can carry."
