Tick 6 - What the Captain Asked For

Monday. The guardhouse on the weighing yard quays, an hour after the morning weighing. Keel and Partridge. The third ask of the harbour guard's petition, answered.

The guardhouse smelled of tar and wet rope and was colder inside than the quay. Owen Dray had a brazier and had not lit it, because it was the second week of a month in which he had been told twice that the yards were watching their coal.

He stood when they came in, and offered Partridge the only chair, and did not offer Keel anything, which Keel noted and did not mind.

"Surgeon. Councillor."

"Captain." Partridge did not take the chair. "How's the knee?"

"It is the same knee."

"It'll be the same knee in ten years if you keep standing on stone all day. Sit down. No - sit down, I'm not asking as anything, I'm asking as a man who has to look at it." He waited until Dray sat, and then leaned on the table corner so that he was lower than the captain and not higher. "We've come about the third thing."

"I am aware."

"We're going to refuse it."

Dray did not move. He had, Keel thought, worked out some while ago that the council would come in person if it was going to refuse, and had therefore known the answer from the moment the clerk said two councillors were on the quay.

"I would like it in the order you said it," Dray said. "The refusal first, and then the reason. Not the reason first. I have had the reason first from three administrations."

"That is reasonable," Keel said, "and it is the order in which I had intended to say it."

He did not sit either. He took out the charter extract, which he had copied himself, and he did not open it, because he knew it.

"The harbour guard asked that no inspection of the quay books be made without seven days' written notice to the captain. The council refuses that. It refuses it entirely and not in part, and it will not offer a shorter period, and it will not offer it on a trial. That is the answer and it will be minuted this afternoon in those words."

"Thank you."

"The clause is the fourteenth. It vests inspection of any collection in the council and in no other body, and it does not qualify the power with notice, with consent or with occasion. Your petition says, correctly, that the charter neither grants you notice nor forbids us to give it. That is also my reading. It is a thing the council may do."

"But will not."

"But will not. Four days ago this council refused the same thing to the Watch, in the muster yard, in front of two hundred men, on the ground that the power is not ours to narrow by promise. If I were to come here and narrow it by promise for seventy-four men, having refused it to two hundred and fifty, I would be saying that the clause means one thing on the water and another thing at the gates. It does not. It means one thing."

Dray turned his hands over on the table, slowly, the way a man does when he is putting an argument down rather than picking one up.

"I had thought you might say that the guard is owned by the guildhall and the Watch is owned by you."

"That is true and it is not the reason."

"No." A pause. "I believe you."

"There is a second part," Partridge said, "and it's the part Councillor Keel will not say, so I'm going to say it, and you'll want to hear it because it's the honest one."

"Say it."

"We will not promise seven days because seven days is long enough to move something." He said it quite gently, in the voice he used for telling people things about their own bodies. "That's it. That's the whole of it. We don't think you've moved anything. We don't think you will. But a council that gives the promise to a clean house has given it to every house, and we can't hand out the honest version only to the men we happen to trust, because then it isn't a rule, it's a favour, and favours get withdrawn by whoever sits here after us."

"And you believe you would find something, in the seven days."

"We believe somebody would. Not you."

Dray looked at the unlit brazier for a while.

"I wrote that last line to be answered," he said. "The line about a council that cannot give notice. I did not expect it to be answered. I expected to be told that the matter was under consideration and that the council valued the guard's long service." He looked up. "You have refused me and told me why in the same quarter of an hour. Nobody has done that here before."

"It is still a refusal," Keel said.

"It is. And my men will read it as one, and I will not tell them otherwise, because I have told them the truth every week for eleven years and I am not going to start decorating it for this council's benefit." He stood, slowly, on the knee. "Councillor. You have cost yourself three days of goodwill and bought something you cannot spend."

"What is that?"

"A reputation for not making exceptions." Dray opened the guardhouse door for them himself. "It is the most expensive thing in this city and I do not think you have worked out yet that you are buying it."

Partridge stopped on the quay and looked back at the shut door.

"He's going to be a problem."

"He is going to be an institution," Keel said. "They are not the same thing and the second is worse."

"He asked to be refused. Who asks to be refused?"

"A man who intends to ask again in the spring and wants a clean record of having asked properly." Keel put the extract back in his coat. "He will have one. I will see that the minute says he asked properly."

The same afternoon, at the gate, Roper had it off a quay man within the hour, which is how everything travels in Needleford.

He said one thing about it, to nobody in particular, while signing a relief roster:

"They told the guard no as well."

And then, after a while, because he is a careful man and it was not quite enough on its own:

"Same clause. Same week."

Tick 7 - Seven Days

Friday and Saturday. Two records queries, one of which found nothing and had to be published, and one of which found three hundred and fifteen gold.

Friday morning, the guildhall's assessment

Keel laid the query on Monday and worked it through Wednesday and Thursday and most of Friday, and found nothing.

He said so in chamber in one sentence and then said the rest of it, which took longer.

"The guilds' assessment for the week reconciles against the trade I can see. I cannot see all of the trade, because it is self-assessed, which is the whole difficulty with the line and has been since before this council existed. I found nothing. I want the council to understand what that sentence costs."

"The undertaking," said Garrow.

"The undertaking. The result of every inspection of every collector, published within seven days, found or not found. I drafted it. I voted for it. I have honoured it twice and it has cost us standing twice and I have said both times that it was worth it."

He put the draft notice down.

"On Saturday this will go up at the four places. It will say that the council of Needleford laid a records query under authority upon the assessment of the guild and market dues, and that nothing was found. It will go up eight days after the guildhall delivered a petition asking to be in the room, and nine days after I stood here and said we should not do this precisely because of how it would read."

"And then we did it," said Partridge.

"And then two collectors came up short in the same week and we did it." Keel did not sit down. "I would like it recorded that I changed my own mind on this and that I was right the first time."

"You were not," said Garrow.

Keel looked at him.

"You were not," Garrow said again, in the same voice, which is the only voice he has. "You were right about what it would cost. You were wrong that the cost was the biggest thing in the room. A hundred and eight gold twice is a man taking a tenth and finding out nobody is coming." He turned back to the window. "If we'd waited three weeks like I said, it'd be four hundred by then and he'd be teaching somebody."

"You were the one who said wait."

"Aye. And it came again in a week, and that's my answer, and I'd rather give it out loud than have it in the minute that I was quiet."

Friday afternoon, the quay share

The second query was the one nobody had expected anything from.

The passage toll comes in on two sides. The Watch takes the gates and the waggons; the harbour guard takes the quays and the hulls. The gate side was clean and had been clean for six weeks. Keel audited the water side because he had audited the land side and because leaving half a stream unexamined in a week when the other half was short is the kind of thing he would be unable to sleep through.

Three hundred and fifteen gold.

Not in one place. Across nine entries, over a week, in a hand that was not Dray's, against hulls that had paid and were written down as having paid a little less.

He checked it four times, which took him until the lamps, and the fourth time he did it backwards from the weighings, and it came out at three hundred and fifteen gold both ways.

Saturday, the guardhouse

They went down together, all three of them this time, and Keel went in first because he is the one who found it and he would not let anybody else carry it for him.

Dray had lit the brazier. That was the thing Partridge noticed and could not stop noticing: he had lit the brazier, because three councillors on a Saturday is either very good news or very bad and he had decided to be hospitable about either.

"Councillors."

"Captain." Keel put the sheet on the table, squared, facing him. "The council laid a records query under authority upon the quay share of the passage toll for the week ending Friday. Three hundred and fifteen gold has been taken from the collection. Nine entries. I have checked it four times and the fourth time I checked it from the weighings backward."

Owen Dray did not pick the sheet up for a long moment. When he did, he read it the way he reads everything, slowly, all the way to the bottom, and then he read it again.

"That's Pellow's hand," he said.

"I do not know whose hand it is."

"It's Pellow's hand. He's done my quay book for eleven years." He set it down very carefully, exactly where it had been. "Was it eleven years?"

"I have the week. I do not have the eleven years and I am not going to pretend to you that I know how far back it goes, because the week is what I audited and the week is what I can prove."

"No." Dray put his hands on the table. "No, you wouldn't."

Partridge started to say something and Dray lifted two fingers, not sharply, and he stopped.

"Let me get there, surgeon. I'll get there faster on my own."

He got there in about fifteen seconds.

"You'll post it."

"Within seven days. At the four places. Found or not found." Keel's voice did not change. "It will name the harbour guard, because the undertaking says the finding is published and the finding is about the harbour guard, and I will not write it in a way that lets a man reading the wall think it might have been somebody else."

"And the money?"

"Recovered. It is the city's."

"And Pellow?"

"Prosecuted." Keel paused, for the first time. "Captain, I want to say the next part exactly once and I want you to hear the order of it, because you asked me for the order last time and you were right to. The council is not negotiating this. It is not negotiating it because the undertaking makes a negotiation indistinguishable, on a wall, from having been bought. The moment it is posted that we found three hundred and fifteen gold, any arrangement we come to with you privately is a thing this city will read as the price of silence - and it would not be, and that would not matter."

Dray nodded slowly, three or four times.

"I asked you for seven days' notice," he said.

"Yes."

"Three weeks ago. In this room." He was not angry. That was the thing that made it worse. "And you refused me, and the surgeon told me why, and he said - "

"I said seven days is long enough to move something," said Partridge.

"You did. And I said - " Dray stopped. "What did I say?"

"You said a council that cannot give seven days' notice is a council that believes it would find something in the seven days."

"Aye." He looked at the brazier. "And you did."

Nobody filled the silence, which took some doing, because two of the three of them fill silences for a living.

"I did not know," Dray said at last. "I want to say that and I want you to write down that I said it, and I want you to write down underneath it that I would say that whether it were true or not, and that you have no way of knowing. I'd not insult you by pretending there's a difference you can see from where you're standing."

"I have written it," said Keel. "Both halves."

"Good." Dray stood, on the knee, slowly. "Then here's the rest of mine, and then I'd like you to go, and it's not discourtesy.

"My men collect one half of your toll on the water. We're paid by the yards, not by you. We asked you for one thing and you refused it to our faces and gave us a reason and I told my men it was an honest reason, which it was. On Saturday they will read on a wall that the harbour guard of Needleford was found thieving. Not Pellow. The harbour guard. Because that's what a wall says and that's what a man walking past reads."

"Captain - "

"I'm not asking you to change it, surgeon. It's the true thing and you'd be lying to soften it." He went to the door and opened it himself, as he had before. "Three weeks ago you bought a reputation for not making exceptions and I told you it was the most expensive thing in this city."

"You did."

"I'd not worked out it was me that was going to pay for it."

The notice went up at the four places on the Saturday, in the same hand as the undertaking, as the undertaking requires.

Two sheets went up that morning. The first said that the council had searched the guild and market assessment and found nothing. The second said what it found on the quays.

Keel stood and read both of them through once, in the rain, at the gate wall, and then went back up the hill.

What he said about it afterwards, to nobody, in the minute:

"The undertaking has now done to a clean body what it did for one, and to a guilty one what it was written to do. It is working exactly as drafted. I should like the record to show that I understand that, and that I would draft it again, and that I was not prepared for how it would feel to be correct about this."

Tick 7 - It Was Me That Was Going to Pay for It

The council audited the two collectors that came up short, found nothing in one and three hundred and fifteen gold in the other, and its own undertaking made the rest of the week for it.

The join. The thumb and the account are the same man. He gave the name Thurlow at a barber-surgeon's in the autumn before last; the account called Thurlow and Company, carriers opened in the same autumn and hired Hallet off a bench on Fetter Lane.

And Brackwater's pattern, assembled from three weeks of apparently wasted errands: "They cannot answer a question. They can only remove the person it was asked about. There are not very many people in a thing like this. They have been spending them."

Read in this order

  1. tick-007-whatever-you-give-them.md - Hanne Vesk hands Partridge the factors' petition and tells him, while she does it, exactly what it is for. First, because Keel's reading of it on the walk back is the constraint the council is now governing under.
  2. artifacts/petition-of-the-sea-factors-year-0.md - short, and it concedes the council's best argument in its fourth paragraph.
  3. tick-007-thurlow.md - the barber-surgeon, the registers, and the moment Partridge puts two names together by accident at seven in the evening. The best week the investigation has had.
  4. tick-007-seven-days.md - the two audits and the guardhouse. Read it last and read it slowly. Dray's line from tick 5 comes back on him and nobody in the room is able to soften it.
  5. tick-007-resolution.md - every fit, difficulty and roll, and the severity arithmetic that came to 13.

What the week was. The council did four correct things and was punished for three of them, caught a thief it was not looking for, and found the name of a man it has been chasing for seven weeks in a barber-surgeon's day-book.

The sentence to take to the halt. The undertaking of tick 4 - publish the result of every inspection of every collector, found or not found - has now cleared a body that wanted clearing, injured a body that wanted consulting, and destroyed a body that was guilty. It has worked exactly as drafted every single time, and nobody has yet proposed changing it.

"I should like the record to show that I understand that, and that I would draft it again, and that I was not prepared for how it would feel to be correct about this."

Tick 7 - Thurlow

Wednesday and Thursday. A barber-surgeon off the Shambles, a shelf of registers nobody has opened in nine years, and a stair where everybody Brackwater asks about stops being there.

Wednesday, Odo Tench's shop

There are nineteen men in Needleford who will cut you, set you or draw your teeth, and Partridge knows all nineteen by name, by reputation, and by which of them he would let touch his own family.

He went to four of them. The fourth was Odo Tench.

"A thumb," Tench said. "Which one?"

"Right. Doesn't bend. He has to turn the whole hand to get at a latch."

"At the knuckle or at the base?"

"I don't know. I've a boy's account and the boy's twelve and the boy showed me his own, so it's the base."

"Then it's not a cut and it's not a break, it's a crush." Tench was sharpening something and did not stop. "A crush at the base that sets stiff is a thing you get from a weight coming down on a hand that's under something - a cask, a stone, a chest end. And whoever set it set it fast, because the way you keep the bend is you keep it open and you hurt the man for six weeks, and nobody who is in a hurry does that."

"So a yard man."

"So a man who was in a hurry." Tench put the blade down. "Gil, I've set two of those in nine years and one of them was a woman. The other one's in the book."

He had to climb on a stool to get it and he would not let Partridge do it.

The day-book of Odo Tench, barber-surgeon, runs back eleven years and is written in a hand that does not change. It is a working record. It has a date against every line and a fee against every date, because Tench is not a sentimental man and the book exists so that he gets paid.

"Autumn before last," he said, going back through it with a finger. "Here. Crush at the base of the right thumb, man of middling height, foreign in the mouth but not in the words. Paid in full and did not come back for the second dressing, which is why it's stiff, and I'd have told him so if he'd stayed to be told."

"Did he give you a name?"

"They all give me a name. Half of them are lying and I write what they say, because what I need is a thing to call the line when they come back."

"Odo."

"Thurlow," said Tench. "He gave the name Thurlow."

Thursday, the registers

Thackeray had spent the whole of Wednesday failing at the customs house and was in a mood that Keel described afterwards, approvingly, as useful.

She went looking for Thurlow and Company, carriers, in the places a carrier has to be: the carting guild's list of yards, the dues roll, the licence book for waggons over four horses, the quay-side porterage lists, and the bond register.

It is in exactly one of them.

"It is not a business," she said, in chamber, with four books open and a fifth under her elbow. "I want to be careful, because I have been wrong in this chamber twice in three weeks - no, I have. I have. Let me say the thing I am sure of first. There is no Thurlow and Company. It holds no yard, pays no dues, has never licensed a waggon, and is not in the guild's list, which Keel wrote himself in the second week and which I have now checked against the guild's own copy, because I did not want to be standing here having checked only our copy."

"And the fifth book," said Garrow.

"The fifth book is the porterage list at the Hithe, which is the list of accounts that are good for wages - which is a different thing entirely from a list of businesses, and which I did not know existed until Tuesday." She turned it round. "Thurlow and Company, carriers. Opened the autumn before last. Eleven entries. Good for wages, every time, settled every Friday, never queried."

"How does a thing that isn't a business get onto that list?"

"Somebody stands for it." She put her finger on the margin. "There is a column for it. It has a name in it."

Nobody said anything.

"Crispin Vout. He is a bill-broker off Fetter Lane, he vouches accounts for a fee, and the Hithe porterage list has his name against nine of them." She closed the book. "Eight of the nine are perfectly ordinary. I checked. I checked all eight before I came in here, because I have learned that this council will ask."

"It will," said Keel.

"It did. I have the answer." She sat down rather hard. "Ambrose, I do not think Vout is anything. I think Vout is a man who takes a fee to write his name in a margin and does not ask, and there are men like that in every city and most of the time they are harmless. But he wrote it, and he is here, and he is alive."

The two halves

It was Partridge who did it, which nobody expected, and he did it by accident at about seven o'clock while Thackeray was still talking.

"Say the name again."

"Thurlow and Company, carriers - "

"No. The one in the margin. No - the other name. The company." He had gone quite still, which is not a thing he does. "Ines. Say Thurlow again."

"Thurlow."

"He gave Tench the name Thurlow."

The room took a moment.

"The man with the thumb," said Brackwater.

"The man with the thumb walked into a barber-surgeon's shop off the Shambles in the autumn before last with his hand crushed, and gave the name Thurlow, and paid in full, and did not come back." Partridge put both hands flat on the table. "And in the same autumn an account called Thurlow and Company, carriers, opened on the Hithe porterage list and started hiring river men by the week off a bench on Fetter Lane. Hallet was one of them."

"That is not a coincidence," Thackeray said, "and I want to be the one to say it is not a coincidence, because I have spent two weeks being wrong and I am going to be right about this one at the top of my voice."

Keel had not moved.

"Say what it means," he said. "Plainly. Do not decorate it."

"It means the account is not a front somebody set up for them," said Thackeray. "It means the account is one of them. The name they hire under is the name he gives a stranger when he is hurt and not thinking - which is what a man does with a name he has been wearing for two years." She looked up. "We have been looking for a business. There isn't one. There is a man with a stiff thumb who calls himself Thurlow, and he is in this city, and he was in that lane three weeks ago carrying a bag in daylight."

And the stair, which gave nothing

Brackwater had spent her Tuesday at the lower stair asking nobody in particular about nothing in particular, which is the cheapest and quietest way to ask anything, and she came back with no answer to the question she had gone with.

She came back with something else, and she reported it in the order she always does, which is the order a thing happened and not the order it matters.

"The man who hired the boat in the spring has not been at the stair since the week we came. The watermen say he was a regular. A boat went up on a Tuesday night with two aboard and one came back."

"Another one?" said Partridge.

"Another one."

"That's - Isolde, that's twice. The messenger in the fifth week and now - "

"Three times," she said.

They all looked at her.

"The auctioneer was bought and forgot. The messenger was taken out. The man at the stair is gone." She was still standing, because she had not been asked to sit. "Every person I have put a question near has stopped being in this city within the fortnight."

"That is not your doing," Garrow said.

"I did not say it was my doing." She let that sit for exactly as long as it needed. "I said it is a pattern, and it is the first one we have that is about them and not about us."

Keel wrote for a while.

"Put it plainly."

"They cannot answer a question," Brackwater said. "They can only remove the person it was asked about." And then, because Keel was still looking at her and because he does that until she finishes: "There are not very many people in a thing like this. They have been spending them."

Tick 7 - Whatever You Give Them

Tuesday. The counting-house at Vesk & Sons' lower berth, the Hithe. Partridge receives the petition of the sea factors from Hanne Vesk, and Keel works out on the way back what it is actually for.

She had sent word that she would bring it to the chamber and Partridge had sent word back that he would come down, which is the sort of thing he does without asking anyone, and which Keel has stopped objecting to on the grounds that it keeps working.

The counting-house smelled of coffee, which almost nothing in Needleford does.

"Surgeon." Hanne Vesk did not get up, because she had a ledger open and her thumb in it. "Sit. There's coffee and you'll want it, you look like a man who's been in a chamber."

"I've been in four. How's the crane?"

"The crane is standing, which you know, because your colleague came down after dark and stood under it, which I have mentioned to everybody in this city at least twice." She closed the ledger. "I want you to know that I am going to hand you something unpleasant in about a minute and that none of it is about the crane."

"All right."

"Good. Most people here would want that said at the end." She slid the thing across. "The sea factors of Needleford, eleven houses, nine signatures. It isn't eighty per cent of anything that matters, before your councillor asks - it's eighty-one, and I counted it the way the warden counted his, because I am not going to be the second person to bring this council a petition and the first one to bring it a bad one."

Partridge read it. It took him less time than Pell's had.

"Hanne, this is the guildhall's."

"It is the guildhall's," she agreed, "in the same way that a second ship built to a first ship's lines is the first ship's. I did not copy the words. I did not have to. Osric read his out in your chamber on a Thursday and it was in every counting-house on the river by the Saturday, and by Monday I had four houses in this room asking me the same question, which was: is that going to be available to us."

"And what did you tell them?"

"I told them I would find out." She poured him the coffee he had not asked for. "And then I thought about it for a night, and I realised that was the wrong thing to have said, because you have not answered him yet. If I wait for you to answer him, I am asking you for a thing you have already decided. If I come now, I am asking you to decide two things at once."

"You're making it harder on purpose."

"I am making it honest on purpose. There's a difference and I'd like the record to show which one it is." She sat back. "Surgeon, in the second week of this council an instrument of a factor house was read aloud, clause by clause, in a room full of rivermen, and refused by name. It was the right refusal. It was my competitor's paper and I was glad. And I went home and I thought: there is no room in this city where a council does that to a man's paper and the man is present."

"Councillor Keel will say the instrument was unlawful."

"It was. That is not the part I am petitioning about and he knows it, and I would rather you told him I know he knows it." She tapped the sheet. "We are not asking for a veto. We are asking that no instrument of a factor house be read or ruled upon in open chamber without the house being heard first. That is all. It is four weeks of your life and a chair."

"And if we refuse?"

"Then you refuse, and I will tell nine houses you refused, and I will say that you came down to the Hithe to do it rather than sending a clerk, because that is also true." She smiled, very slightly. "I should say, in case it is not obvious: I do not expect to win this. I expect to be in the room when you decide what the guildhall gets, which I will be, because it is now on your table in writing."

The walk back

Keel was waiting at the bridge, because Keel waits at bridges.

Partridge handed it over and watched him read it standing up, in a wind, with one hand, without his expression altering at any point.

"Well?"

"Weight four," Keel said. "Three ticks. The deadline is tick ten."

"That's the guildhall's deadline."

"That is the guildhall's deadline." He folded it. "Surgeon, I would like to say a thing once and then I am going to go and be unpleasant to a clerk about a shortfall. We cannot answer this before we answer the guildhall. Not should not. Cannot."

"Because it's smaller."

"Because it is smaller and because it is the same ask. Her houses want to be heard before a ruling on their paper. His companies want to be heard before a measure on their rates. Those are one principle wearing two coats." He started walking. "If we concede hers on Monday, we have conceded his on Monday, at a weight of four, having been paid for a weight of seven - and every body below zero in this city will have watched us do it for the price of the cheapest petition on the table."

"And if we refuse hers?"

"Then we have refused his, on Monday, at a weight of four, without ever having read our own paper on it." Keel did not slow down. "There is no answer to the sea factors that is not also an answer to the guildhall. The only thing we may do with her petition is let the clock run, and that is not cleverness, it is the only lawful door in the room."

"She knows that."

"She said it to you in the second sentence and you have not noticed yet." Keel stopped at the end of the bridge. "She did not come early to be first, Partridge. She came early so that whatever we give the guildhall, we will have given it with her petition open on the table, which means we will have given it to her as well, without her spending a single thing for it."

Partridge thought about that for most of the way back up the hill.

"That's rather good," he said eventually.

"It is extremely good. I intend to tell her so." Keel took out his notebook. "At the point where it stops being useful to her."
